Bergman algebras The graded universal algebra constructions
R. Hazrat, H. Li, R. Preusser

Abstract
. A half a century ago, George Bergman introduced stunning machinery which would realise any commutative conical monoid as the non-stable K K -theory of a ring. The ring constructed is “minimal” or “universal”. Given the success of graded K K -theory in classification of algebras and its connections to dynamics and operator algebras, the realisation of Γ \Gamma -monoids (monoids with an action of an abelian group Γ \Gamma on them) as non-stable graded K K -theory of graded rings becomes vital. In this paper, we revisit Bergman’s work and develop the graded version of this universal construction. For an abelian group Γ \Gamma , a Γ \Gamma -graded ring R R , and non-zero graded finitely generated projective (left) R R -modules P P and Q Q , we construct a universal Γ \Gamma -graded ring extension S S such that S ⊗ R P ≅ S ⊗ R Q S\otimes_{R}P\cong S\otimes_{R}Q as graded S S -modules. This makes it possible to bring the graded techniques, such as smash products and Zhang twists into Bergman’s machinery. Given a commutative conical Γ \Gamma -monoid M M , we construct a Γ \Gamma -graded ring S S such that 𝒱 gr ​ ( S ) \mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S) is Γ \Gamma -isomorphic to M M . In fact we show that any finitely generated Γ \Gamma -monoid can be realised as the non-stable graded K K -theory of a hyper Leavitt path algebra. Here 𝒱 gr ​ ( S ) \mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S) is the monoid of isomorphism classes of graded finitely generated projective S S -modules and the action of Γ \Gamma on 𝒱 gr ​ ( S ) \mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S) is by shift of degrees. Thus the group completion of M M can be realised as the graded Grothendieck group K 0 gr ​ ( S ) K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(S) . We use this machinery to provide a short proof to the fullness of the graded Grothendieck functor K 0 gr K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0} for the class of Leavitt path algebras (i.e., Graded Classification Conjecture II).



In memory of Nikolai Vavilov who conveyed the joy of mathematics to us in Chinese, German and Persian

1.
Introduction

In 1974 in the seminal paper
[
7
]
George Bergman introduced machinery whereby by adding sufficient generators and relations to an algebra, one can lift any pair of non-zero finitely generated projective modules to become isomorphic. To be precise, let
R
be an algebra over a field
\mathsf{k}
, and let
(P_{i},Q_{i})_{i\in I}
be pairs of non-zero finitely generated projective left
R
-modules. Bergman’s machinery adjoins certain generators and relations to
R
, so that the resulted extension algebra
S
obtained provides universal isomorphisms of modules,
S\otimes_{R}P_{i}\cong S\otimes_{R}Q_{i}
, where
i\in I
. Furthermore, the non-stable
K
-theory of the algebra
S
can be obtained from that of
R
by identifying the pairs
(P_{i},Q_{i})_{i\in I}
in the structure.

Starting from a field
\mathsf{k}
and choosing the pair of
\mathsf{k}
-modules
(\mathsf{k}^{n},\mathsf{k}^{m})
,
n,m\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
, Bergman observed that his machinery retrieves the celebrated
Leavitt algebra
R=L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,m)
, where

R^{n}\cong\mathsf{k}^{n}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}}R\cong\mathsf{k}^{m}\otimes_{\mathsf{k}}R\cong R^{m}
as
R
-modules. Many combinatorial algebras constructed in the last 50 years, such as Leavitt path algebras and their generalisations, can be obtained from Bergman’s machinery.

Two natural questions immediately arise:

(1)

The structural properties
: To describe the structure of the extension algebra
S
obtained from
R
relative to the pairs
(P_{i},Q_{i})_{i\in I}
.

(2)

The classification
: Given pairs
(P_{i},Q_{i})_{i\in I}
of
R
-modules and
(P^{\prime}_{i},Q^{\prime}_{i})_{i\in I}
of
R^{\prime}
-modules, for
\mathsf{k}
-algebras
R
and
R^{\prime}
, respectively, to describe how the extension
\mathsf{k}
-algebras
S
and
S^{\prime}
obtained from the Bergman machinery, respectively, compare.

We highlighted Leavitt path algebras as substantial activity is currently taking place
on both questions (1) and (2).
In fact, there are several conjectures concering the classification problems and what could be the right invariant for Leavitt path algebras
[
1
]
. Leavitt path algebras associated to graphs have rich
\mathbb{Z}
-graded structures (arising by assigning degree
1
to edges) which play a vital role in their studies. Their non-stable
graded

K
-theory is related to invariants of symbolic dynamics and the equivariant
K
-theory of operator algebras and the current open conjecture is that the graded
K
-theory could be a complete invariant for these algebras (see
[
13
,
15
]
and §
9
). This is one of the main motivations of this project: to study the Bergman machinery in the graded setting.

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a commutative ring and let
R
be a unital
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Further let
N
be an
R
-module and
P
a finitely generated projective
R
-module. Let
R\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
be the category of
\mathsf{k}
-algebras
T
equipped with a
\mathsf{k}
-homomorphism
R\rightarrow T
. Bergman’s work started by showing that the following functor is representable:

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{N,P}:R\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}&\longrightarrow{}\operatorname{Sets}\\
T&\longmapsto\operatorname{Hom}(T\otimes_{R}N,T\otimes_{R}P).\end{split}

(1.1)

That is, there exists a universal algebra
S
(or the initial object
R\rightarrow S
in an appropriate category), denoted by
R\langle f:\overline{N}\rightarrow\overline{P}\rangle
, which provides the
S
-homomorphism
S\otimes_{R}N\rightarrow S\otimes_{R}P
. Bergman further showed that there exists a universal algebra
S
, denoted by
R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle
, such that the extension of a given
R
-module homomorphim
f:N\rightarrow P
vanishes.

Combining these two constructions, allows for forming very interesting universal rings. For example, given a homomorphism
f:P\rightarrow Q
, one can construct a universal ring
R\langle\overline{f}^{-1}\rangle
, where the extension of
f
becomes invertible. The two important universal constructions needed to realise any conical monoid are the following: Let
P
and
Q
be non-zero finitely generated projective left
R
-modules. Constructing the universal homomorphisms
h:\overline{P}\rightarrow\overline{Q}
and
h^{\prime}:\overline{\overline{Q}}\rightarrow\overline{\overline{P}}
in subsequent extensions and setting
h^{\prime}\overline{h}-1
and
\overline{h}h^{\prime}-1
to zero, we obtain the universal ring

S:=R\big\langle h,h^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle,

(1.2)

where
S\otimes_{R}P\cong S\otimes_{R}Q
as
S
-modules.

On the other hand, for
P
a non-zero finitely generated projective left
R
-module, by constructing the universal homomorphism
e:P\rightarrow P
and setting the homomorphism
e^{2}-e
to zero we form a universal ring

T:=R\big\langle e:\overline{P}\to\overline{P};~e^{2}=e\big\rangle,

(1.3)

such that the extension of
e
in
T
is an idempotent endomorphism.

Bergman then proved
[
7
, Theorems 5.1, 5.2]
that
\mathcal{V}(S)\cong\mathcal{V}(R)/\langle[P]=[Q]\rangle
and

\mathcal{V}(T)\cong\langle\mathcal{V}(R),[P_{1}],[P_{2}]\rangle/\langle[P]=[P_{1}]+[P_{2}]\rangle
, for the universal rings
S
and
T
of (
1.2
) and (
1.3
), respectively. Here
\mathcal{V}(S)
is the monoid of isomorphism classes of finitely generated projective left
S
-modules.
These constructions allowed Bergman to build up a machinery to realise any (finitely generated) conical commutative monoid as the non-stable
K
-theory of a ring.

Graded algebras frequently appear when there is a group acting on an algebraic structure. A graded ring can be a model of an algebraic structure that captures and reflects the time evolution and the dynamics.
A ring
R
is
graded
by a group
\Gamma
when, roughly,
R
can be partitioned by
\Gamma
in a way that is compatible with the structure of
R
. (See §
2.2
for more details.) Consequently, three categories play a prominent role in this setting: the category of left
R
-modules
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
, the category of graded left
R
-modules
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
, and the category of left
R_{0}
-modules
R_{0}
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
, where
R_{0}
is the subring of
R
consisting of the elements in the partition corresponding to the identity element
0
of
\Gamma
. A substantial portion of the theory of graded rings concerns the relationships between these categories. While the applications of this theory are numerous, one prominent example is the fundamental theorem of
K
-theory, proved by Quillen
[
22
]
, using the category of graded modules in a crucial way.

Our starting point is to consider a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
over a
\Gamma
-graded commutative ring
\mathsf{k}
, a
\Gamma
-graded (left)
R
-module
N
and a
\Gamma
-graded finitely generated projective (left)
R
-module
P
, where
\Gamma
is an abelian group, and consider the corresponding functors in the graded setting:

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{gr}}_{N,P}:R\text{-gr-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}&\longrightarrow{}\operatorname{Sets}\\
T&\longmapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{T\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}(T\otimes_{R}N,T\otimes_{R}P)\end{split}

(1.4)

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}_{N_{0},P_{0}}:R_{0}\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}_{0}}&\longrightarrow{}\operatorname{Sets}\\
T&\longmapsto\operatorname{Hom}(T\otimes_{R_{0}}N_{0},T\otimes_{R_{0}}P_{0})\end{split}

(1.5)

Here
R\text{-gr-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
is the category of
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras
T
equipped with a graded
\mathsf{k}
-homomorphism
R\rightarrow T
. In this paper we show that
\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{gr}}_{N,P}
is representable, i.e., there is a
\Gamma
-graded algebra
S
such that
\operatorname{Hom}(S,-)\cong\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{gr}}_{N,P}
. Furthermore, if
R
is strongly graded, then
S\cong R\ast_{R_{0}}S_{0}
, where
S_{0}
is the representation for
\mathcal{F}_{N_{0},P_{0}}
and
\ast
is the coproduct. Forgetting the grading on
S
gives us the representation for

\mathcal{F}_{N,P}
of (
1.1
).

Having these graded universal constructions, we can realise any conical commutative
\Gamma
-monoid as the nonstable graded
K
-theory of a suitable graded ring. Namely, for such a
\Gamma
-monoid
M
, there is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
such that
M\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
, as
\Gamma
-monoids. Here
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
is the monoid of isomorphism classes of graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules and the action of
\Gamma
on
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
is by shift of degrees (Theorem
8.1
).
In order to carry out our
\Gamma
-monoid realisations, we had two options; either to develop the entire theory of monoids for co-products rings (
[
8
]
) in the graded setting, or to start with a graded ring, and use the fact that the category of graded modules is equivalent to the category of modules over its corresponding smash product (i.e., to pass from the graded setting to the non-graded case and use the available techniques).
We opted for the second option; we pass from the graded structure via smash product to the category of modules and
use the established results in the non-graded setting. The price we had to pay for such a shortcut,
was to drop some generality, such as working with graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras, where
\mathsf{k}
is a field concentrated in degree zero, rather than with
\mathsf{k}
-algebras, where
\mathsf{k}
is a graded field.

Our realisations of
\Gamma
-monoids allow us to provide a short proof to one of the Graded Classification Conjectures (See Conjecture
9.1
(1)): Let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is concentrated in degree zero and
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
is cancellative. Then any order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module
homomorphism
\phi:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
with

\phi([L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)])=[A]

is induced by a unital
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-homomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow A
, i.e.,
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(\psi)=\phi
. Here
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
is the Leavitt path algebra associated to the graph
E
. Replacing the algebra
A
with a Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
provides a positive answer to the fullness conjecture.

In general given two finitely generated projective modules, it is not straightforward to describe Bergman’s (localisation) algebras explicitly. Given finitely generated
R
-modules
P
and
Q
, and a map
f:P\rightarrow Q
, there are few instances where the localisation rings
R\langle f:\overline{P}\rightarrow\overline{Q}\rangle
, or
R\langle\overline{f}^{-1}\rangle
are explicitly described. We refer to the paper of Sheiham
[
26
]
where the localisation of triangular matrix rings has been described. In this paper, by representing the finitely generated projective modules by the corresponding idempotent matrices, we first describe the Bergman’s constructions in terms of generators and relations. Thus given idempotent matrices
e\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)
, we define the Bergman algebra
B_{R}(e,f)
via generators and relations such that
e
and
f
become equivalent idempotents in
B_{R}(e,f)
in a universal way.
The construction can be extended to families of pairs of idempotents
(e,f):=\{(e_{i},f_{i})\}_{i\in I}
. In fact this construction will be carried out for graded rings and homogeneous idempotents in Section
4
. The algebra
B_{R}(e,f)
obtained this way, coincides with
the universal localisation ring
R\langle f,f^{-1}:\overline{P}_{i}\cong\overline{Q}_{i}\mid i\in I\rangle
, for pairs of finitely generated projective modules
(P_{i},Q_{i}),i\in I
which represent
(e_{i},f_{i}),{i\in I}
.

Returning to the graded setting, we show that when
R
is a
\Gamma
-graded commutative
\mathsf{k}
-semisimple ring which is concentrated in degree zero, then the universal localisation ring
R\langle f,f^{-1}:\overline{P}_{i}\cong\overline{Q}_{i}\mid i\in I\rangle
, for any family of pairs of finitely generated projective modules
(P_{i},Q_{i}),i\in I
, is graded isomorphic to a Leavitt path algebra of a hypergraph and conversely any such hyper Leavitt path algebra can be realised as a graded Bergman’s construction over a
\Gamma
-graded commutative
\mathsf{k}
-semisimple ring (Theorem
4.10
). Built on this, we show that any finitely generated conical
\Gamma
-monoid can be realised as the non-stable graded
K
-theory of a hyper Leavitt path algebra (Theorem
8.2
).

As mentioned, starting with a field
\mathsf{k}
, and projective modules
\mathsf{k}
and
\mathsf{k}^{n}
, Bergman’s machinery would give the Leavitt algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,n)
. This ring can be graded by assigning
1
and
-1
to the suitable generators. However, we can start with a field
\mathsf{k}
concentrated in degree zero, and consider the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded projective modules
\mathsf{k}
and
\mathsf{k}(-1)^{n}
, where
\mathsf{k}(-1)
is the projective module
\mathsf{k}
shifted by
-1
. Applying our graded Bergman construction, we obtain the algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,n)
which is naturally
\mathbb{Z}
-graded from the outset. This allows us to build the graded invariants such as non-stable graded
K
-theory from the machinery we develop.

The paper is organised as follows: Section
2
provides a background needed for the concepts of
\Gamma
-monoids and graded algebras, as well as the notion of smash products. In Section
3
, we recall the notion of Leavitt path algebras of hypergraphs, which generalises several extensions of Leavitt path algebras. This is the generalisation which covers all Bergman algebras constructed from commutative semisimple rings as a base ring. In Section
4
, we introduce Bergman algebras, via generators and relations, both the non-graded version and the graded version. Section
5
which is the core part of this paper, carries out Bergman’s localisation construction
[
7
]
in the setting of graded rings.
Section
6
investigates how the graded Bergman localisation behaves under the smash product. This is needed in Section
7
to compute the non-stable graded
K
-theory of graded Bergman algebras. Section
8
develops many consequences of the graded Bergman machinery: we realise any conical
\Gamma
-monoid as the non-stable graded
K
-theory of an appropriate graded algebra. In fact we show that any finitely generated
\Gamma
-monoid can be obtained as the non-stable graded
K
-theory of a hyper Leavitt path algebra. Using our result, we provide a short positive answer to one of the Graded Classification Conjectures (Conjecture
9.1
(1)) for Leavitt path algebras in Section
9
.

In this paper, all rings are unital and all modules are considered as left modules unless otherwise stated.
\mathbb{N}
denotes the natural numbers with
0
and
\mathbb{N}^{+}=\mathbb{N}\backslash\{0\}
.

2.
\Gamma
-monoids and graded algebras

2.1.
\Gamma
-monoids

Recall that a
monoid
is a semigroup with an identity element. Throughout this paper monoids are commutative, written additively, with the identity element denoted by
0
. A monoid homomorphism
\phi:M\rightarrow N
is a map between monoids
M
and
N
which respects the structures and satisfies
\phi(0)=0
. Every monoid
M
is equipped with a natural preordering:
n\leq m
if
n+p=m
for some
p\in M
.
We write

n\propto m\text{ if }n\leq km,\text{ for some }k\in\mathbb{N}^{+},

and

n\asymp m\text{ if }n\propto m\text{ and }m\propto n.

Thus
n\asymp m
if there is a
k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
such that
m\leq kn
and
n\leq km
.
We say
i\in M
is
an order unit
if
m\propto i
for any
m\in M
.
We say
M
is
conical
if
m+n=0
implies that
m=n=0
, where
m,n\in M
. We say
M
is
cancellative
if
m_{1}+n=m_{2}+n
implies
m_{1}=m_{2}
, for all
m_{1},m_{2},n\in M
.

Given a group
\Gamma
, a
\Gamma
-monoid
consists of a monoid
M
equipped with an action of
\Gamma
on
M
(by monoid automorphisms). We denote the action of
\alpha\in\Gamma
on
m\in M
by
{}^{\alpha}m
. Throughout this paper
\Gamma
will be an abelian group and
M
a commutative monoid. A monoid homomorphism
\phi\colon M\rightarrow N
between two
\Gamma
-monoids is called
\Gamma
-
monoid homomorphism
if
\phi
respects the actions of
\Gamma
, i.e.,
\phi({}^{\alpha}m)={}^{\alpha}\phi(m)
for all
m\in M
. The natural preordering is respected by the action of
\Gamma
.

An
order unit
of a
\Gamma
-monoid
M
is an element
i\in M
such that for any
m\in M
, there are
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{t}\in\Gamma
such that

m\leq\sum_{k=1}^{t}{}^{\gamma_{k}}i.

(2.1)

We denote the
\Gamma
-monoid
M
along with the order unit
i
by
(M,i)
and call it a
pointed
\Gamma
-monoid
. A morphism
f:(M,i)\rightarrow(N,j)
in the category of pointed
\Gamma
-monoids, is a
\Gamma
-monoid morphism
f
such that
f(i)=j
.

The order unit
i
is called a
strong order unit
if for any
m\in M
, we can choose all
\gamma_{k}=0
in (
2.1
). We say
i
is a
invariant order unit
if
{}^{\gamma}i=i
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

A
congruence
on a
\Gamma
-monoid
M
is an equivalence relation
\sim
on
M
such that
m\sim m^{\prime}
and
n\sim n^{\prime}
implies
m+n\sim m^{\prime}+n^{\prime}
, and
m\sim m^{\prime}
implies
{}^{\gamma}m\sim{}^{\gamma}m^{\prime}
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
m,m^{\prime},n,n^{\prime}\in M
. If
\sim
is a congruence on
M
, then
M/\sim
is a
\Gamma
-monoid in the obvious way. We call
M/\sim
a
quotient
of
M
.

If
M
is a
\Gamma
-monoid and
(m_{i},n_{i})
, where
i\in I
, a family of pairs of elements of
M
, then
the congruence on the
\Gamma
-monoid
M
generated by the relations
m_{i}=n_{i}
will be the congruence generated on
M
as a monoid by the relations
{}^{\gamma}m_{i}={}^{\gamma}n_{i}
, where
i\in I
and
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

The action of the abelian group
\Gamma
respects the relations
\propto
and
\asymp
and one can see that
\asymp
is an equivalence relation.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-monoid and
i\in M
a
\Gamma
-order unit. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The element
i
is a strong order unit;

(2)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
we have
i\asymp{}^{\gamma}i
;

(3)

The equivalence class
[i]
under
\asymp
, which is clearly a
sub-semigroup of
M
, is in fact a
\Gamma
-subsemigroup of
M
.

(4)

If
M
has a strong order-unit, then all
such elements form an equivalence class under
\asymp
.

Proof.

The proofs are straightforward.
∎

Clearly the image of a homomorphism
f:M\to N
, denoted by
\operatorname{im}f
, is a
\Gamma
-submonoid of
N
. The
kernel

\ker f
of
f
is the congruence
\sim
on
M
defined by
m\sim m^{\prime}
if
f(m)=f(m^{\prime})
, so that
M/\ker f
is a
\Gamma
-monoid and the canonical homomorphism
M/\ker f\rightarrow N
is injective (see Lemma
2.3
).

Let
X
be a set. The
free
\Gamma
-monoid on
X
, denoted by
F\langle X\rangle
, is constructed as the free monoid on the set
X\times\Gamma
, with the action of
\delta\in\Gamma
on
(x,\gamma)
defined by
(x,\gamma+\delta)
. To be precise,

F\langle X\rangle=\Big\{\sum_{x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma}n_{x,\gamma}(x,\gamma)\mid n_{x,\gamma}\geq 0\text{, for any }x\in X\text{ and }\gamma\in\Gamma;\text{ almost all }n_{x,\gamma}\text{ are zero}\Big\}

which becomes a
\Gamma
-monoid with addition

\sum_{x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma}n_{x,\gamma}(x,\gamma)+\sum_{x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma}n^{\prime}_{x,\gamma}(x,\gamma)=\sum_{x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma}(n_{x,\gamma}+n^{\prime}_{x,\gamma})(x,\gamma)

and
\Gamma
-action

{}^{\delta}\Big(\sum_{x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma}n_{x,\gamma}(x,\gamma)\Big)=\sum_{x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma}n_{x,\gamma}x(\gamma+\delta).

One checks easily that
F\langle X\rangle
has the following universal property.

Lemma 2.2
.

Let
X
be a set,
M
a
\Gamma
-monoid and
f:X\to M
a map. Let
g:X\to F\langle X\rangle
be the map defined by
x\to x(0)
. Then there is a unique homomorphism
h:F\langle X\rangle\to M
of
\Gamma
-monoids such that
f=hg
.

Lemma 2.3

(Homomorphism theorem)
.

Let
f:M\to N
be a homomorphism of
\Gamma
-monoids. Then
M/\ker f\cong\operatorname{im}f
.

Proof.

Define a map
\bar{f}:M/\ker f\to\operatorname{im}f
by
\bar{f}([m])=f(m)
for any
m\in M
. Clearly
\bar{f}
is well-defined and bijective. One checks easily that
f
is a homomorphism of
\Gamma
-monoids.
∎

Proposition 2.4
.

Any
\Gamma
-monoid is isomorphic to a quotient of a free
\Gamma
-monoid.

Proof.

Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-monoid. It follows from Lemma
2.2
that there is a surjective homomorphism
\gamma:F\langle X\rangle\to M
of
\Gamma
-monoids. Thus
F\langle X\rangle/\ker\gamma\cong\operatorname{im}\gamma=M
by Lemma
2.3
.
∎

Let
X
be a set and
R
a set of relations
m_{i}=n_{i}
, where
m_{i},n_{i}\in F\langle X\rangle
, for any
i\in I
. We write
M=\langle X\mid R\rangle
if
M\cong F\langle X\rangle/\sim
, where
\sim
is the congruence on
F\langle X\rangle
generated by
R
. In this case we say that
M
is a
\Gamma
-monoid presented by the generating set
X
and the relations
R
.

2.2.
Graded algebras

In this section we collect basic facts that we need about graded rings and graded algebras. We refer the reader to
[
17
,
21
]
for the theory of graded rings.

Let
\Gamma
be an abelian group with identity denoted by
0
. A ring
R
(possibly without unit)
is called a
\Gamma
-graded ring
if
R=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}R_{\gamma}

such that each
R_{\gamma}
is an additive subgroup of
R
and
R_{\gamma}R_{\delta}\subseteq R_{\gamma+\delta}
for all
\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma
. The group
R_{\gamma}
is
called the
\gamma
-
homogeneous component
of
R.
When it is clear from context
that a ring
R
is graded by the group
\Gamma,
we simply say that
R
is a
graded
ring
. We denote the set of all homogeneous elements of the graded ring
R
, by
R^{h}
.

A unital
\Gamma
-graded ring
R=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}R_{\gamma}
is
strongly graded
if
R_{\alpha}R_{\beta}=R_{\alpha+\beta}
for any
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
and it is
crossed-product
if each component
R_{\alpha}
,
\alpha\in\Gamma
contains an invertible element. Furthermore, we say
R
is a
graded field
if it is commutative and all non-zero homogeneous elements are invertible. The notion of strongly graded, in particular, is an important case as it widely generalises the case of group rings, and it has been gradually established that many results on group rings can be extended to strongly graded rings.

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a
\Gamma
-graded commutative ring and
R
a unital
\Gamma
-graded ring. We say
R
is a
graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
if it is equipped with a graded ring homomorphism
\phi:\mathsf{k}\rightarrow Z(R)
, where
Z(R)
is the centre of
R
. Note that since
\Gamma
is abelian,
Z(R)
is a graded ring. Let
S
be a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Then we say
S
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
if it is equipped with a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow S
. We denote the category of

\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
by
R\text{-gr-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
. Considering
\Gamma
to be trivial we obtain the category of
R\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
.

For a graded
A
-module
M
, we define the
\alpha
-
shifted
graded

A
-module
M(\alpha)
as

M(\alpha)=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}M(\alpha)_{\gamma},

where
M(\alpha)_{\gamma}=M_{\alpha+\gamma}
. That is, as an ungraded module,
M(\alpha)
is a copy of

M
, but the grading is shifted by
\alpha
.

For a
\Gamma
-graded ring
R
, the category of graded
R
-modules is denoted by
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
and the category of
R
-modules by
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
.
For
\alpha\in\Gamma
, the
shift functor

\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}:R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}\longrightarrow R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}},\quad M\longmapsto M(\alpha)

is an isomorphism with the property
\mathcal{T}_{\alpha}\mathcal{T}_{\beta}=\mathcal{T}_{\alpha+\beta}

for
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
.

Let
R
be a strongly
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. For any
R_{0}
-module
N
and any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, we identify the
R_{0}
-module
R_{\gamma}\otimes_{R_{0}}N
with its image in
R\otimes_{R_{0}}N
. Since
R=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}R_{\gamma}
and
R_{\gamma}
are
R_{0}
-bimodules,
R\otimes_{R_{0}}N
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R
-module, with

R\otimes_{R_{0}}N=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}R_{\gamma}\otimes_{R_{0}}N.

Recall that we have the
restriction functor

(-)_{0}:R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}\longrightarrow R_{0}\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}

(2.2)

sending
M

to
M_{0}
and
\phi
to
\phi_{|_{M_{0}}}
with
M
a graded
R
-module and
\phi
a graded module homomorphism from
M
, and the
induction functor

\begin{split}R\otimes_{R_{0}}-:R_{0}\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}&\longrightarrow R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}\\
N&\longmapsto R\otimes_{R_{0}}N\\
\psi&\longmapsto R\otimes\psi.\end{split}

(2.3)

When
R
is strongly graded, Dade’s Theorem
[
17
, Theorem 1.5.1]
(see also

[
21
, Theorem 3.1.1]
), guarantees that the functors
(-)_{0}
and
R\otimes_{R_{0}}-
form mutually inverse equivalences of categories.

Note that in general
(-)_{0}:R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}\xrightarrow{}R_{0}\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
does not induce a functor
(-)_{0}:R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}\xrightarrow{}R_{0}\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
. But
R
is strongly graded if and only if
R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
is equivalent to
R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
, if and only if
R\text{-}{\bf gr}
is equivalent
R_{0}\text{-}{\bf mod}
, both under the functors (
2.2
) and (
2.3
). Here
R\text{-}{\bf gr}
denotes the category of graded finitely generated
R
-modules and
R_{0}\text{-}{\bf mod}
is the category of finitely generated
R_{0}
-modules.

Let
R
,
S
and
A
be
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras and let
i:A\rightarrow R
and
j:A\rightarrow S
be
\mathsf{k}
-graded algebra monomorphisms (so we identify
A
as a graded subring of
R
and
S
). The coproduct
R*_{A}S
can be given a natural
\Gamma
-grading, so that this coproduct is indeed the pushout for the graded maps
i
and
j
.

2.3.
Matrix form of graded homomorphisms

Let
\Gamma
be an abelian group,
\mathsf{k}
a
\Gamma
-graded commutative ring with identity and
R
a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra.

A graded
R
-module is called a
graded free
module, if it is a free module with homogeneous basis. Clearly a graded free module is a free module. A graded free
R
-module can be presented as
\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\gamma_{i})
, where
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
.

A graded
R
-module
P
is called a
graded projective
module if
P
is a projective
R
-module. One can check that
P
is graded projective if and only if the functor
\operatorname{Hom}_{R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}(P,-)
is an exact functor in the category
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
if and only if
P
is graded isomorphic to a direct summand of a graded free
R
-module (
[
17
, Proposition 1.2.15]
). So a module is graded projective module if and only if it is graded and projective. This is not the case for graded free module. A module could be graded and free, but not graded free (see
[
17
, § 1.2.4]
).

In particular, if
P
is a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module, then there is a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module
Q
such that

P\bigoplus Q\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}R(\alpha_{i}),

(2.4)

with
\alpha_{i}\in\Gamma
. We denote
\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}R(\alpha_{i})
by
R^{n}(\overline{\alpha})
with
\overline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})
.

One observes that
{\operatorname{Hom}}_{R}(R(\delta_{j}),R(\delta_{i}))\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}R(\delta_{i}-\delta_{j})
as graded
R
-modules. Hence we have the following isomorphisms:

\begin{split}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i}),\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j}))&\cong\prod_{i\in I}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(R(\alpha_{i}),\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j}))\\
&\cong\prod_{i\in I}\bigoplus_{j\in J}\operatorname{Hom}_{R}(R(\alpha_{i}),R(\beta_{j}))\\
&\cong\prod_{i\in I}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i}).\end{split}

(2.5)

For a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
,
\overline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
,
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{j})_{j\in J}
with
\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}\in\Gamma
, set

\mathbb{M}_{I\times J}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]:={\begin{pmatrix}R_{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i}}\end{pmatrix}}_{i\in I,j\in J},

(2.6)

where for each
i\in I
, only a finite number of entries in
(R_{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i}})
are non-zero. Thus
\mathbb{M}_{I\times J}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]
consists of matrices with the
ij
-entry in
R_{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i}}
, with a finite number of non-zero elements in each row. One observes that
\mathbb{M}_{I\times J}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]
represents the set
\operatorname{Hom}_{R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}(\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i}),\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j}))
as follows: Suppose that
u:\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
is a graded
R
-module homomorphism. Then for each
i\in I
, we can write
u(1_{i})=(u_{ij})_{j\in J}
, where
1_{i}
is the element of
\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i})
whose
i
-th component is
1
and whose other components are zero (note that
\deg(1_{i})=-\alpha_{i})
), and
u_{ij}\in R(\beta_{j})_{-\alpha_{i}}=R_{\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i}}
are all zero except for a finite number of
j
in
J
. Then the graded homomorphism
u:\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
is given by multiplying the matrix
(u_{ij})_{i\in I,j\in J}\in\mathbb{M}_{I\times J}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]
from the right. Suppose that
v:\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{k\in K}R(\gamma_{k})
is given by multiplying the matrix
(v_{jk})_{j\in J,k\in K}\in\mathbb{M}_{J\times K}(R)[\overline{\beta}][\overline{\gamma}]
from the right. Then the composition
v\circ u
is given by multiplying the matrix
(u_{ij})_{i\in I,j\in J}\cdot(v_{jk})_{j\in J,k\in K}
from the right. Here,

(u_{ij})_{i\in I,j\in J}\cdot(v_{jk})_{j\in J,k\in K}

(2.7)

is the multiplication of matrices. Notice that the matrix representation of the composition of functions
v\circ u
is obtained by swapping the orders (
2.7
).

The above notion of
mixed-shift
was developed for graded right
R
-modules in
[
17
, §1.3.4]
.

In fact, any element in
\prod_{i\in I}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i})
in the last equation of (
2.5
) can be written as
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
, where
x_{i}\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j}-\alpha_{i})
is a row vector indexed by
J
(
x_{i}
having finitely many non-zero entries for each
i\in I
).

For a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
,
\overline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{m})\in\Gamma^{m}
,
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\cdots,\beta_{n})\in\Gamma^{n}
, (
2.6
) takes the form

\mathbb{M}_{m\times n}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]={\begin{pmatrix}R_{\beta_{1}-\alpha_{1}}&R_{\beta_{2}-\alpha_{1}}&\cdots&R_{\beta_{n}-\alpha_{1}}\\
R_{\beta_{1}-\alpha_{2}}&R_{\beta_{2}-\alpha_{2}}&\cdots&R_{\beta_{n}-\alpha_{2}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
R_{\beta_{1}-\alpha_{m}}&R_{\beta_{2}-\alpha_{m}}&\cdots&R_{\beta_{n}-\alpha_{m}}\\
\end{pmatrix}},

(2.8)

and
\mathbb{M}_{m\times n}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]
represents the set
\operatorname{Hom}_{R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}(R^{m}(\overline{\alpha}),R^{n}(\overline{\beta}))
. When
m=n
, we simply write
\mathbb{M}_{m\times n}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]
as
\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}]
.

By (
2.5
) we have the graded matrix ring

\operatorname{End}_{R}(R^{n}(\overline{\alpha}))\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\alpha_{j}-\alpha_{i})
, denoted by
\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})
. More precisely

\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})={\begin{pmatrix}R(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{1})&R(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1})&\cdots&R(\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{1})\\
R(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2})&R(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{2})&\cdots&R(\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{2})\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
R(\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{n})&R(\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{n})&\cdots&R(\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{n})\end{pmatrix}}

(2.9)

For each
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})_{\gamma}
, the
\gamma
-homogeneous elements, are the

n\times n
-matrices over
R
with the degree shifted (suspended) as follows

\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})_{\gamma}={\begin{pmatrix}R_{\gamma+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{1}}&R_{\gamma+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{1}}&\cdots&R_{\gamma+\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{1}}\\
R_{\gamma+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{2}}&R_{\gamma+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{2}}&\cdots&R_{\gamma+\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{2}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
R_{\gamma+\alpha_{1}-\alpha_{n}}&R_{\gamma+\alpha_{2}-\alpha_{n}}&\cdots&R_{\gamma+\alpha_{n}-\alpha_{n}}\end{pmatrix}}.

(2.10)

We identify graded finitely generated projective modules with homogeneous idempotent matrices of degree zero. This is taken from
[
17
, Lemma 3.2.3]
.

Lemma 2.5
.

We have the following statements.

(1)

Any graded finitely generated projective module gives rise to a homogeneous idempotent matrix of degree zero.

(2)

Any homogeneous idempotent matrix of degree zero gives rise to a graded finitely generated projective module.

Proof.

For (1), let
P
be a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. Then
there is a graded module
Q
such that
P\oplus Q\cong R^{n}(\overline{\alpha})
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\overline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})\in\Gamma^{n}
. Define the homomorphism
e\in\operatorname{End}_{R}(R^{n}(\overline{\alpha}))

which sends
Q
to zero and acts as identity on
P
. Clearly,
e
is an idempotent and
graded homomorphism of degree zero. Thus
e\in\operatorname{End}_{R}(R^{n}(\overline{\alpha}))_{0}\cong\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})_{0}
.

For (2), let
e\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})_{0}
be a homogeneous idempotent matrix of degree zero, where
\overline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{1},\cdots,\alpha_{n})
with
\alpha_{i}\in\Gamma
. Then
1-e\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\alpha})_{0}
and

R^{n}(\overline{\alpha})=R^{n}e(\overline{\alpha})\oplus R^{n}(1-e)(\overline{\alpha}).

This shows that
R^{n}e(\overline{\alpha})
is a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module.
∎

In the first part of the paper we assume
\mathsf{k}
to be a commutative graded ring. In the second part, for the realisation of conical
\Gamma
-monoids, we assume
\mathsf{k}
to be a (graded) field concentrated in degree zero.

2.4.
Non-stable Graded
K
-theory

For a
\Gamma
-graded ring
R
with identity and a graded finitely generated projective (left)
R
-module
P
, let
[P]
denote the class of graded
R
-modules graded isomorphic to
P
. Then the set

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)=\big\{\,[P]\mid P\text{ is a graded finitely generated projective R-module}\,\big\}

(2.11)

with direct sum as addition constitutes a
\Gamma
-monoid structure defined as follows:
[P]+[Q]=[P\oplus Q]
and for
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
[P]\in\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
,
{}^{\gamma}[P]=[P(\gamma)]
. The group completion of
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
is called the
graded Grothendieck group
and is denoted by
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(R)
, which
as the above discussion shows is a
\mathbb{Z}[\Gamma]
-module.

Following the notion of order unit for a
\Gamma
-monoid in §
2.1
,
an element
[P]\in\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
is an order unit for
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
if for any
[Q]\in\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
we have
[Q]\leq\sum_{1\leq i\leq n}[P(\gamma_{i})]
for some
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma_{i}\in\Gamma
.
Since for any graded finitely generated projective module
Q
, there is a graded module
P
such that
Q\oplus P\cong R(\gamma_{1})\oplus R(\gamma_{2})\oplus\cdots\oplus R(\gamma_{t})
, it follows that that
[R]
is an order unit (see
[
17
, §3]
).

The following easy proposition shows that the order unit
[R]
in
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
can determine when
R
is a strongly graded or a crossed product ring (see also
[
21
, Theorems 2.10.1 and 2.10.2]
). This will be used in Theorem
8.1
.

Proposition 2.6
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded ring with identity and
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
the monoid of graded finitely generated projective modules. Then

(1)

The ring
R
is strongly graded if and only if
[R]
is an strong order unit.

(2)

The ring
R
is crossed-product if and only if
[R]
is an invariant order unit.

Proof.

(1) The ring
R
is strongly graded if and only if, for any
\alpha\in\Gamma
,
1=\sum_{i=1}^{n_{\alpha}}r_{i}s_{i}
, where
r_{i}\in R_{\alpha}
and
s_{i}\in R_{-\alpha}
. This is equivalent to having matrices
{\mathbf{r}}=(r_{1},\dots,r_{n_{\alpha}})\in\mathbb{M}_{1\times n_{\alpha}}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][0]
and

{\mathbf{s}}=(s_{1},\dots,s_{n_{\alpha}})^{t}\in\mathbb{M}_{n_{\alpha}\times 1}(R)[0][\overline{\alpha}]
with
{\mathbf{r}}{\mathbf{s}}=1
.
In turn this is equivalent to having an epimorphism
\bigoplus_{n_{\alpha}}R\rightarrow R(\alpha)\rightarrow 0
, which is equivalent to
[R(\alpha)]\leq n_{\alpha}[R]
in
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
. This is now equivalent to
[R]
being a strong order unit.

(2) This follows from the observation that there is a graded
R
-module isomorphism
R\cong R(\alpha)
if and only if there is an invertible element in
R_{\alpha}
.
∎

2.5.
Graded hereditary rings

Recall that a unital ring
R
is called a left hereditary ring if any left ideal of
R
is a projective
R
-module
[
28
, Chapter 4]
. Analogously, we say a
\Gamma
-graded ring
R
is called a
left graded hereditary ring
if any graded left ideal of
R
is a graded projective module.

The following proposition is the graded version of well-known equivalent properties of hereditary rings whose proof follows mutatis mutandis, with an attention given to the grading.

Proposition 2.7
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded ring. The following statements are equivalent.

(1)

R
is left graded hereditary;

(2)

Any graded submodule of a graded projective left
R
-module is graded projective;

(3)

Any graded submodule of a graded projective left
R
-module is projective;

(4)

The graded left global dimension is less than two;

(5)

{\rm Ext}_{R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}^{2}(-,-)
vanishes.

Proposition
2.7
(3) implies that a
\Gamma
-graded ring which is hereditary is indeed graded hereditary. However, the following example shows that a graded hereditary ring is not necessarily hereditary. This is in contrast with the fact that graded regular rings and regular rings which are graded (i.e., their (graded) global dimension is finite) coincide.

Consider a strongly
\Gamma
-graded ring
R
. By Dade’s theorem (see Section
2.2
), the categories
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
and
R_{0}
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
are equivalent. An application of Proposition
2.7
shows that if
R_{0}
is hereditary, then
R
is a graded hereditary ring. The

\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring
R=\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
with
\deg(x)=1
and
\deg(x^{-1})=-1
is a strongly graded ring with the zero-component the ring
\mathbb{Z}
which is hereditary (PID rings are hereditary). Thus
R
is a graded hereditary ring. However,
R
is not hereditary: for example, the (non-graded) ideal
\langle 2,x-1\rangle
is not a projective module.

Note that the graded ring
\mathsf{k}[x,x^{-1}]
, where
\mathsf{k}
is a field, is indeed a PID ring and thus a hereditary ring. We will see the graded rings coming out of the graded Bergman constructions are indeed hereditary and thus they are graded hereditary as well.

2.6.
Smash products of graded algebras

2.6.1.
Smash products

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded ring. The
smash product
ring
R\#\Gamma
is defined as the set of all formal sums
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}
, where
r^{(\gamma)}\in R
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
, the
p_{\gamma}
’s are symbols, and all but finitely many coefficients
r^{(\gamma)}
are zero. Addition is defined component-wise and multiplication is defined by linear extension of the rule
(rp_{\alpha})(sp_{\beta})=rs_{\alpha-\beta}p_{\beta}
, where
r,s\in R
and
\alpha,\beta\in\Gamma
. Instead of
1p_{\gamma}
we may write
p_{\gamma}
. If
R
is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative graded ring concentrated in degree
0
, then
R\#\Gamma
is a (not-necessarily unital)
\mathsf{k}
-algebra with scalar multiplication
\lambda\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\lambda r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}
, for any
\lambda\in\mathsf{k}
and
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}\in R\#\Gamma
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative graded ring concentrated in degree
0
. Then the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R\#\Gamma
has the presentation

\displaystyle R\#\Gamma=\Big\langle rp_{\gamma}~(r\in R^{h},\gamma\in\Gamma)\mid{}

\displaystyle rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}=(r+s)p_{\gamma}\quad(r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),

\displaystyle~rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}=rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\quad(r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle.

Proof.

Denote by
F
the free
\mathsf{k}
-algebra generated by the set
\{rp_{\gamma}~(r\in R^{h},\gamma\in\Gamma)\}
, and by
I
the two-sided ideal of
F
generated by the set

\big\{rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}-(r+s)p_{\gamma}\mid r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma\big\}\bigcup\big\{rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}-rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\mid r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma\big\}.

Clearly the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\theta:F\to R\#\Gamma
defined by
\theta(rp_{\gamma})=rp_{\gamma}~(r\in R^{h},\gamma\in\Gamma)
induces a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\bar{\theta}:F/I\to R\#\Gamma
(since
I\subseteq\ker(\theta)
). Since the elements
rp_{\gamma}~(r\in R^{h},\gamma\in\Gamma)
generate the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R\#\Gamma
, the homomorphism
\bar{\theta}
is surjective. It remains to show that
\bar{\theta}
is injective. Suppose that
\bar{\theta}(x)=0
, where
x\in F/I
is non-zero. Clearly we can write
x=r_{1}p_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+r_{n}p_{\gamma_{n}}
, where
r_{1},\dots,r_{n}\in R^{h}\setminus\{0\}
and
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. For
\gamma\in\Gamma
set
J(\gamma):=\{1\leq i\leq n\mid\gamma_{i}=\gamma\}
. We may assume that

\deg(r_{i})\neq\deg(r_{j}),\text{ for any }\gamma\in\Gamma\text{ and }i\neq j\in J(\gamma).

(2.12)

It follows from

0=\bar{\theta}(x)=r_{1}p_{\gamma_{1}}+\dots+r_{n}p_{\gamma_{n}}=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\Big(\sum_{i\in J(\gamma)}r_{i}\Big)p_{\gamma}

that
\sum_{i\in J(\gamma)}r_{i}=0
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. In view of (
2.12
) this implies
r_{i}=0
, for any
1\leq i\leq n
, a contradiction. Thus we have shown that
\bar{\theta}
is injective.
∎

2.6.2.
Smash products of quotients and extensions

We continue with the assumption that
R
is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative graded ring concentrated in degree
0
. If
I
is a graded ideal of
R
, then we denote by
I\#\Gamma
the ideal of
R\#\Gamma
consisting of all elements
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}
such that
r^{(\gamma)}\in I
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Note that the quotient ring
R/I
is
\Gamma
-graded ring.

Proposition 2.9
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative graded ring concentrated in degree
0
, and
I
a graded ideal of
R
. Then

(R/I)\#\Gamma\cong R\#\Gamma/I\#\Gamma.

Proof.

We define the map

\displaystyle f:(R/I)\#\Gamma

\displaystyle\longrightarrow R\#\Gamma/I\#\Gamma

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(r^{(\gamma)}+I)p_{\gamma}

\displaystyle\longmapsto\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}+I\#\Gamma.

Suppose that
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(r^{(\gamma)}+I)p_{\gamma}=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(s^{(\gamma)}+I)p_{\gamma}
in
(R/I)\#\Gamma
. Then
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(r^{(\gamma)}-s^{(\gamma)}+I)p_{\gamma}=0
and hence
r^{(\gamma)}-s^{(\gamma)}\in I
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. It follows that
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(r^{(\gamma)}-s^{(\gamma)})p_{\gamma}\in I\#\Gamma
and hence
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}+I\#\Gamma=\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}s^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}+I\#\Gamma
. Thus
f
is well-defined. Clearly
f
is a surjective
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism. It remains to show
f
is injective. Suppose that
f(\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(r^{(\gamma)}+I)p_{\gamma})=0
, i.e.,
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}+I\#\Gamma=0
. Then
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}r^{(\gamma)}p_{\gamma}\in I\#\Gamma
and hence
r^{(\gamma)}\in I
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. It follows
\sum\limits_{\gamma\in\Gamma}(r^{(\gamma)}+I)p_{\gamma}=0
and thus
f
is injective.
∎

If
X
is a set, we denote by
R\langle X\rangle
the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra obtained from
R
by adjoining the set
X
(i.e.,
R\langle X\rangle
is the free product of
R
and the free
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
k\langle X\rangle
generated by
X
). If in addition we are given a map
\deg:X\to\Gamma
, then
R\langle X\rangle
becomes a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra with the induced grading. We denote by
X\#\Gamma
the set
\{xp_{\gamma}\mid x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma\}
.

Proposition 2.10
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative graded ring concentrated in degree
0
, and
X
a set and
\deg:X\to\Gamma
a map. Consider
R\langle X\rangle
as a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra with the induced grading. Then

R\langle X\rangle\#\Gamma\cong R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J,

where
J
is the ideal of
R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle
generated by the set

\big\{(xp_{\gamma})(1p_{\gamma})-xp_{\gamma},~(1p_{\gamma+\deg(x)})(xp_{\gamma})-xp_{\gamma}\mid x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma\big\}.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
2.8
that
R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J
has the presentation

\begin{split}R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J=\Big\langle rp_{\gamma},xp_{\gamma}~(r\in R^{h},x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma)\mid{}&rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}=(r+s)p_{\gamma}\quad(r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&~rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}=rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\quad(r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&~(xp_{\gamma})(1p_{\gamma})=xp_{\gamma}\quad(x\in X,~\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&~(1p_{\gamma+\deg(x)})(xp_{\gamma})=xp_{\gamma}\quad(x\in X,~\gamma\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(2.13)

Since the relations in presentation (
2.13
) above are satisfied in
R\langle X\rangle\#\Gamma
, there is a
k
-algebra homomorphism

f:R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J\rightarrow R\langle X\rangle\#\Gamma,

such that
f(rp_{\gamma})=rp_{\gamma}
and
f(xp_{\gamma})=xp_{\gamma}
, for any
r\in R^{h},x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma
. Clearly the elements
rp_{\gamma},xp_{\gamma}
, where
r\in R^{h},x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma
generate the
k
-algebra
R(X)\#\Gamma
and hence
f
is surjective. It remains to show that
f
is injective.

Let
w
be a non-empty and finite word over the alphabet
\{rp_{\gamma},xp_{\gamma}~(r\in R^{h},x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma)\}
. Then
w=y_{1}p_{\gamma_{1}}\dots y_{n}p_{\gamma_{n}}
for some
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}\in R\cup X
and
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. We call
w
a
path (ending in
\gamma_{n}
)
if
\gamma_{i-1}-\gamma_{i}=\deg(y_{i})~(i=2,\dots,n)
. We denote the set of of all paths by
W
, and for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
the set of all paths ending in
\gamma
by
W_{\gamma}
. Define a map
\zeta:W\to R\langle X\rangle
by
\zeta(y_{1}p_{\gamma_{1}}\dots y_{n}p_{\gamma_{n}})=y_{1}\dots y_{n}
. Now suppose that
f(a)=0
for some
a\in R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J
. It follows from the presentation (
2.13
) that
a=\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}w
where
\lambda_{w}\in k~(w\in W)
. Hence

0=f(a)=f\Big(\sum_{w\in W}\lambda_{w}w\Big)=f\Big(\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\sum_{w\in W_{\gamma}}\lambda_{w}w\Big)=\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma}\sum_{w\in W_{\gamma}}\lambda_{w}\zeta(w)p_{\gamma}.

It follows that
\sum_{w\in W_{\gamma}}\lambda_{w}\zeta(w)=0
in
R(X)
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. One checks easily that this implies that
\sum_{w\in W_{\gamma}}\lambda_{w}w=0
in
R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J
, for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Hence
a=0
and thus
f
is injective.
∎

Corollary 2.11
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative graded ring concentrated in degree
0
,
X
a set and
\deg:X\to\Gamma
a map. Consider
R\langle X\rangle
as a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra with the induced grading. Let
I
be a graded ideal of
R\langle X\rangle
. Then

(R\langle X\rangle/I)\#\Gamma\cong R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J,

where
J
is the ideal of
R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle
generated by the image of
I\#\Gamma
and the set

\big\{(xp_{\gamma})(1p_{\gamma})-xp_{\gamma},~(1p_{\gamma+\deg(x)})(xp_{\gamma})-xp_{\gamma}\mid x\in X,\gamma\in\Gamma\big\}.

(2.14)

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
2.9
that
(R\langle X\rangle/I)\#\Gamma\cong R\langle X\rangle\#\Gamma/I\#\Gamma
. By Proposition
2.10
, there is an isomorphism
g:R\langle X\rangle\#\Gamma\to R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J^{\prime}
, where
J^{\prime}
is the ideal of
R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle
generated by (
2.14
). Hence

(R\langle X\rangle/I)\#\Gamma\cong R\langle X\rangle\#\Gamma/I\#\Gamma\cong(R\#\Gamma\langle X\#\Gamma\rangle/J^{\prime})/g(I\#\Gamma).

The assertion of the corollary follows.
∎

2.6.3.
An isomorphism of categories

Recall that
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
denotes the category of
\Gamma
-graded
R
-modules and
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
the full subcategory of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
whose objects are the projective objects of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
that are finitely generated as a
R
-module. Moreover,
R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
denotes the category of unital
R\#\Gamma
-modules and
R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
the full subcategory of
R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
whose objects are the projective objects of
R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
that are finitely generated as a
R\#\Gamma
-module. In
[
3
]
it was shown that there is an isomorphism
\psi:R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}\to R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
, and in
[
24
]
it was shown that
\psi
restricts to an isomorphism
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}\to R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
. The isomorphism
\psi
maps an object
M
of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
to the object
M\#\Gamma
of
R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
which is defined as follows. As abelian group
M\#\Gamma=M
. The
R\#\Gamma
-action on
M\#\Gamma
is defined by
(rp_{\alpha})m=rm_{\alpha}
for any
r\in R
,
\alpha\in\Gamma
and
m\in M=M\#\Gamma
. On morphisms,
\psi
is just the identity map.

3.
Leavitt path algebras

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a field. Consider the symbols

X=(x_{ij})
and
X^{*}=(x^{*}_{ji})
, where
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq n+k
:

X=\left(\begin{matrix}x_{11\phantom{n+{},}}&x_{12\phantom{n+{},}}&\dots&x_{1,n+k}\\
x_{21\phantom{n+{},}}&x_{22\phantom{n+{},}}&\dots&x_{2,n+k}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x_{n,1\phantom{n+{},}}&x_{n,2\phantom{n+{},}}&\dots&x_{n,n+k}\end{matrix}\right),\qquad X^{*}=\left(\begin{matrix}x^{*}_{11\phantom{n+{},}}&x^{*}_{12\phantom{n+{},}}&\dots&x^{*}_{1,n\phantom{n+{},}}\\
x^{*}_{21\phantom{n+{},}}&x^{*}_{22\phantom{n+{},}}&\dots&x^{*}_{2,n\phantom{n+{},}}\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
x^{*}_{n+k,1}&x^{*}_{n+k,2}&\dots&x^{*}_{n+k,n}\end{matrix}\right).

(3.1)

The
Leavitt algebra

L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
is defined as

L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)=\frac{\mathsf{k}\langle X,X^{*}\rangle}{\langle XX^{*}=I_{n},\,X^{*}X=I_{n+k}\rangle}.

(3.2)

Here
\mathsf{k}\langle X,X^{*}\rangle
is the free
\mathsf{k}
-algebra generated by the symbols
x_{ij}
and
x_{ji}^{*}
’s. Furthermore, the relations
XX^{*}=I_{n}
and
X^{*}X=I_{n+k}
, where
I_{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathsf{k})
and
I_{n+k}\in\mathbb{M}_{n+k}(\mathsf{k})
are the identity matrices, stand for the collection of relations after multiplying the matrices and comparing the two sides of the equations.

These rings were first studied by Leavitt
[
19
]
and
[
20
, p.130, footnote 6]
. Leavitt showed the type of the ring
L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
is
(n,n+k)
. Furthermore,
L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
, for
n>1
and
k\geq 1
, is a domain, whereas
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,k+1)
, is a simple ring.

If in (
3.2
) we only consider the relation
X^{*}X=I_{n+k}
, then we call the resulting algebra
Cohn algebra

C_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)=\frac{\mathsf{k}\langle X,X^{*}\rangle}{\langle X^{*}X=I_{n+k}\rangle},

(3.3)

which was considered by Cohn
[
11
]
, who showed that

C_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
, for
n>1
and
k\geq 1
, is a domain, whereas
C_{\mathsf{k}}(1,k+1)
has only one non-trivial proper ideal generated by relation
XX^{*}=I_{n}
. This result also implies the simplicity of
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,k+1)
.

The simple rings
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,k+1)
received significant attentions as they are the discrete versions of Cuntz algebras. Modeled on this, Leavitt path algebras are introduced
[
1
]
, which attach to a directed graph a certain algebra. In the case that the graph has one vertex and
k+1
loops, it recovers Leavitt algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,k+1)
.

In order to recover the Leavitt algebras
L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
, for
n>1
, a plethora of generalisations of Leavitt path algebras were introduced, such as Leavitt path algebra of separated graphs, weighted graphs and ultra graphs.
Here, following
[
24
]
, we introduce the Leavitt path algebras of hypergraphs. We show that Bergman algebras (see Section
4
) with coefficients in a commutative semisimple ring are precisely Leavitt path algebras of hypergraphs (Theorem
4.10
).

3.1.
Leavitt path algebras of hypergraphs

For sets
I
and
X
, a function
x:I\rightarrow X
,
i\mapsto x_{i}=x(i)
is called a
family of elements in
X
indexed by
I
. A family
x
of elements in
X
indexed by
I
is usually denoted as
(x_{i})_{i\in I}
. We call the family
(x_{i})_{i\in I}

non-empty
if
I\neq\emptyset
.

A
(directed) hypergraph
is a quadruple
H=(H^{0},H^{1},s,r)
, where
H^{0}
and
H^{1}
are sets and
s
and
r
are maps associating to each
h\in H^{1}
a non-empty family
s(h)=(s(h)_{i})_{i\in I_{h}}
, respectively,
r(h)=(r(h)_{j})_{j\in J_{h}}
of elements in
H^{0}
. The elements of
H^{0}
are called
vertices
and the elements of
H^{1}

hyperedges
. In this paper all hypergraphs are assumed to be
{\it regular}
, i.e. for any hyperedge
h
the sets
I_{h}
and
J_{h}
are finite.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
H
be a hypergraph. The not necessarily unital
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
presented by the generating set

\{v,h_{ij},h_{ij}^{*}\mid v\in H^{0},h\in H^{1},i\in I_{h},j\in J_{h}\}

and the relations

(i)

uv=\delta_{uv}u\quad(u,v\in H^{0})
,

(ii)

s(h)_{i}h_{ij}=h_{ij}=h_{ij}r(h)_{j},~r(h)_{j}h_{ij}^{*}=h_{ij}^{*}=h_{ij}^{*}s(h)_{i}\quad(h\in H^{1},i\in I_{h},j\in J_{h})
,

(iii)

\sum\limits_{j\in J_{h}}h_{ij}h_{i^{\prime}j}^{*}=\delta_{ii^{\prime}}s(h)_{i}\quad(h\in H^{1},~i,i^{\prime}\in I_{h})
and

(iv)

\sum\limits_{i\in I_{h}}h_{ij}^{*}h_{ij^{\prime}}=\delta_{jj^{\prime}}r(h)_{j}\quad(h\in H^{1},~j,j^{\prime}\in J_{h})

is called the
Leavitt path algebra of the hypergraph
H
. We will sometimes call a Leavitt path algebra of a hypergraph a
hyper Leavitt path algebra
.

It is not difficult to check that if
H
is a hypergraph, then
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
has a set of local units, namely the set of all finite sums of distinct elements
of
H^{0}
. Furthermore,
L_{K}(H)
is a unital ring if and only if
H^{0}
is finite (
[
24
, Proposition 8]
).

Remark 3.2
.

Let
H
be a hypergraph. We can describe the relations (ii)-(iv) in Definition
3.1
using matrices as follows. For any hyperedge
h\in H^{1}
, let
e_{h}
be the
I_{h}\times I_{h}
-matrix whose entry at position
(i,i^{\prime})
is
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}s(h)_{i}
, and
f_{h}
the
J_{h}\times J_{h}
-matrix whose entry at position
(j,j^{\prime})
is
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}r(h)_{j}
. Moreover, we denote for any hyperedge
h\in H^{1}
the
I_{h}\times J_{h}
-matrix whose entry at position
(i,j)
is
h_{ij}
also by
h
, and the
J_{h}\times I_{h}
-matrix whose entry at position
(j,i)
is
h^{*}_{ij}
by
h^{*}
. Then
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
is the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra presented by the generating set
\{v,h_{ij},h_{ij}^{*}\mid v\in H^{0},h\in H^{1},i\in I_{h},j\in J_{h}\}
and relations

(I)

the elements of
H^{0}
are pairwise orthogonal idempotents,

(II)

h=e_{h}hf_{h},\quad h^{*}=f_{h}h^{*}e_{h}\quad(h\in H^{1})
,

(III)

hh^{*}=e_{h}\quad(h\in H^{1})
and

(IV)

h^{*}h=f_{h}\quad(h\in H^{1})
.

Remark 3.3
.

Let
H
be a hypergraph and
\Gamma
an abelian group. For any
h\in H^{1}
choose an
i_{h}\in I_{h}
and a
j_{h}\in J_{h}
. A map
w
which associates to any generator
h_{ij}~(h\in H^{1},i\in I_{h},j\in J_{h})
an element from
\Gamma
, such that

w(h_{ij})=w(h_{ij_{h}})-w(h_{i_{h}j_{h}})+w(h_{i_{h}j})

for any
h\in H^{1}
,
i\in I_{h}
and
j\in J_{h}
is called a
\Gamma
-weight map
for
H
. By
[
24
, Lemma 70]
, a
\Gamma
-weight map
w
for
H
induces a
\Gamma
-grading on
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
such that
\deg(v)=0
,
\deg(h_{ij})=w(h_{ij})
and
\deg(h_{ij}^{*})=-w(h_{ij})
for any
v\in H^{0}
,
h\in H^{1}
,
i\in I_{h}
and
j\in J_{h}
.

Example 3.4

(Leavitt path algebras)
.

Suppose that
E
is a
directed graph
, i.e. a quadruple
E=(E^{0},E^{1},r,s)
, where
E^{0}
and
E^{1}
are
sets and
r,s
are maps from
E^{1}
to
E^{0}
. The elements of
E^{0}
are called
vertices
and the elements of
E^{1}

edges
. We may think of an edge
e\in E^{1}
as an arrow from
s(e)
to
r(e)
. Assume that
E
is
row-finite
, i.e. for each vertex
v\in E^{0}
there are at most finitely many edges in
s^{-1}(v)
. Denote by
E^{\operatorname{reg}}
the set of
regular
vertices of the graph
E
, i.e. the set of all vertices
v
for which
s^{-1}(v)
is not the empty set. The
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
presented by the generating set
\{v,e,e^{*}\mid v\in E^{0},e\in E^{1}\}
and the relations

(i)

uv=\delta_{uv}u\quad(u,v\in E^{0})
,

(ii)

s(e)e=e=er(e),~r(e)e^{*}=e^{*}=e^{*}s(e)\quad(e\in E^{1})
,

(iii)

\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}ee^{*}=v\quad(v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}})
and

(iv)

e^{*}f=\delta_{ef}r(e)\quad(e,f\in E^{1})

is called the
Leavitt path algebra
of
E
(cf. for example
[
1
]
). For each
v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}
write
s^{-1}(v)=\{e^{v,j}\mid j\in J_{v}\}
. Define a hypergraph
H=(H^{0},H^{1},s^{\prime},r^{\prime})
by
H^{0}=E^{0}
,
H^{1}=\{h^{v}\mid v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}\}
,
s^{\prime}(h^{v})=(v)_{i\in\{1\}}
and
r^{\prime}(h^{v})=(r(e^{v,j}))_{j\in J_{v}}
. There is a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra isomorphism
\phi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
such that
\phi(u)=u
,
\phi(e^{v,j})=h^{v}_{1j}
and
\phi((e^{v,j})^{*})=(h^{v}_{1j})^{*}
for any
u\in E^{0}
,
v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}
and
j\in J_{v}
.

Example 3.5

(Vertex-weighted Leavitt path algebras)
.

Suppose that
(E,w)
is a
vertex-weighted
graph, i.e.
E
is a directed graph and
w
is a map associating to each regular vertex a positive integer. We again assume that
E
is row-finite. The
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E,w)
presented by the generating set
\{v,e_{i},e_{i}^{*}\mid v\in E^{0},e\in E^{1},1\leq i\leq w(s(e))\}
and the relations

(i)

uv=\delta_{uv}u\quad(u,v\in E^{0})
,

(ii)

s(e)e_{i}=e_{i}=e_{i}r(e),~r(e)e_{i}^{*}=e_{i}^{*}=e_{i}^{*}s(e)\quad(e\in E^{1},1\leq i\leq w(s(e)))
,

(iii)

\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}e_{i}e_{j}^{*}=\delta_{ij}v\quad(v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}};~1\leq i,j\leq w(v))
and

(iv)

\sum_{1\leq i\leq w(v)}e_{i}^{*}f_{i}=\delta_{ef}r(e)\quad(v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}};~e,f\in s^{-1}(v))

is called the
Leavitt path algebra
of
(E,w)
. For each
v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}
write
s^{-1}(v)=\{e^{v,j}\mid j\in J_{v}\}
. Define a hypergraph
H=(H^{0},H^{1},s^{\prime},r^{\prime})
by
H^{0}=E^{0}
,
H^{1}=\{h^{v}\mid v\in E^{0}_{\operatorname{reg}}\}
,
s^{\prime}(h^{v})=(v)_{i\in\{1,\dots,w(v)\}}
and
r^{\prime}(h^{v})=(r(e^{v,j}))_{j\in J_{v}}
. There is a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra isomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E,w)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
such that
\psi(u)=u
,
\psi(e^{v,j}_{i})=h^{v}_{ij}
and
\psi((e^{v,j}_{i})^{*})=(h^{v}_{ij})^{*}
for any
u\in E^{0}
,
v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}
,
i\in\{1,\dots,w(v)\}
and
j\in J_{v}
.

4.
Bergman algebras

Let
R
be a ring and
e\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)
be idempotent matrices. Recall that
e
and
f
are called
equivalent
, denoted
e\sim f
, if there are matrices
h\in\mathbb{M}_{m\times n}(R)
and
h^{*}\in M_{n\times m}(R)
such that
e=hh^{*}
and
f=h^{*}h
.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
R
be a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra, where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative ring. Let
e\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)
be idempotent matrices. Let
h=(h_{ij})
and
h^{*}=(h^{*}_{ji})
, where
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, be a collection of symbols. Then we define a chain of
Bergman algebras

B^{1}_{R}(e,f)\longrightarrow B^{2}_{R}(e,f)\longrightarrow B^{3}_{R}(e,f)\longrightarrow B_{R}(e,f),

as follows:

\displaystyle B^{1}_{R}(e,f)

\displaystyle:=R\big\langle h\big\rangle\big/\big\langle ehf=h\big\rangle

\displaystyle B^{2}_{R}(e,f)

\displaystyle:=R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle\big/\big\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*}\big\rangle

\displaystyle B^{3}_{R}(e,f)

\displaystyle:=R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle\big/\big\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*},hh^{*}=e\big\rangle

and

\displaystyle B_{R}(e,f)=B^{4}_{R}(e,f)

\displaystyle:=R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle\big/\big\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*},hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=f\big\rangle.

(4.1)

We further define,

B_{R}(e)=R\big\langle h\big\rangle\big/\big\langle ehe=h,h^{2}=h\big\rangle.

Here
R\langle h,h^{*}\rangle
is the free ring generated by symbols
h_{ij}
and
h^{*}_{ji}
’s with coefficients from the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
. We assume the symbols commute with
\mathsf{k}
. In fact
R\langle h,h^{*}\rangle
is
the coproduct
R*_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}\langle h_{ij},h^{*}_{ji}\mid 1\leq i\leq m,1\leq j\leq n\rangle
. Furthermore, the relations such as
ehf=h
stand for the collection of relations after multiplying the matrices and comparing the two sides of the equation. Clearly there is a canonical
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphim
R\rightarrow B_{R}^{i}(e,f)
, where the images of
e
and
f
in
B_{R}(e,f)
become equivalent.

In this note, we particularly concentrate on the Bergman algebra
B_{R}(e,f)
defined in (
4.1
), as many of combinatorial algebras can be realised as such Bergman algebras.

Example 4.2
.

We can recover classical Cohn and Leavitt algebras (
3.3
) and (
3.2
), respectively, as follows. For a field
\mathsf{k}
, and idempotent matrices
1\in\mathsf{k}
and
I_{n}\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(\mathsf{k})
, we have

\displaystyle B^{1}_{\mathsf{k}}(1,I_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{k}\langle x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}\rangle

\displaystyle B^{2}_{\mathsf{k}}(1,I_{n})

\displaystyle\cong\mathsf{k}\langle x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n},x_{1}^{*},x_{2}^{*},\dots,x_{n}^{*}\rangle

\displaystyle B^{3}_{\mathsf{k}}(I_{n},1)

\displaystyle\cong C_{\mathsf{k}}(1,n)

\displaystyle B_{\mathsf{k}}(1,I_{n})=B^{4}_{\mathsf{k}}(1,I_{n})

\displaystyle\cong L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,n),

furthermore

\displaystyle B(I_{n},I_{n+k})=B^{4}(I_{n},I_{n+k})

\displaystyle\cong L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)

\displaystyle B^{3}(I_{n+k},I_{n})

\displaystyle\cong C_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)

Remark 4.3
.

In this note we use categories of left modules over rings. One can also use categories of right modules. In that case, one would have
B^{3}_{\mathsf{k}}(1,I_{n})\cong C_{\mathsf{k}}(1,n)
returning to right
R
-modules.

Example 4.4
.

This example demonstrates how rich the structure of seemingly easy to construct Bergman algebras of (
4.1
) could be. Consider the Bergman algebra
B_{\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathsf{k})}(1,1)
. Here
1
is the identity matrix of the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathsf{k})
. One can immediately see that

B_{\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathsf{k})}(1,1)\cong\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathsf{k})*_{\mathsf{k}}\mathsf{k}[x,x^{-1}].

Furthermore, the discussion in Section 4 of
[
7
]
shows that the Leavitt algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(2,2)
coincides with a corner of this Bergman algebra, specifically,

L_{\mathsf{k}}(2,2)\cong e_{11}B_{\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathsf{k})}(1,1)e_{11}.

Lemma 4.5
.

Let
R
be a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra, where
\mathsf{k}
is a commutative ring. Let
e\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)
be idempotent matrices. If idempotent matrices
e^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{k}(R)
and
f^{\prime}\in\mathbb{M}_{l}(R)
are equivalent to
e
and
f
, respectively, then
B_{R}^{i}(e,f)
is
\mathsf{k}
-algebra isomorphic to
B_{R}^{i}(e^{\prime},f^{\prime})
, where
1\leq i\leq 4
.

Proof.

We show that if
e\sim e^{\prime}
then
B_{R}^{i}(e,f)\cong B_{R}^{i}(e^{\prime},f)
. The general result follows by symmetry.

Since
e\sim e^{\prime}
, there are matrices
x\in\mathbb{M}_{m\times k}(R)
,
y\in\mathbb{M}_{k\times m}(R)
such that

e=xy
,
e^{\prime}=yx
.
Define the map
\phi:B_{R}^{i}(e,f)\rightarrow B_{R}^{i}(e^{\prime},f)
by sending
h\mapsto xh^{\prime}
and
h^{*}
\mapsto{h^{*}}^{\prime}y
(if
h^{*}
is present in the definition of
B_{R}^{i}(e,f)
). The converse map
\psi:B_{R}^{i}(e^{\prime},f)\rightarrow B_{R}^{i}(e,f)
is defined by sending
h^{\prime}\mapsto yh
and
{h^{*}}^{\prime}\mapsto h^{*}x
. We leave it to the reader to check these assignments respect the defining relations and give a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra isomorphism between the two algebras.
∎

Example 4.6
.

We note that if the idempotents
e\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)
are equivalent, i.e.,
e=hh^{*}
and
f=h^{*}h
, then by considering
h_{1}=ehf
and
h_{1}^{*}=fhe
we have
e=h_{1}h_{1}^{*}
and
f=h_{1}^{*}h_{1}
, with,
eh_{1}f=h_{1}
and
fh_{1}^{*}e=h_{1}^{*}
. However the algebras

B^{\prime}(e,f):=R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle\big/\big\langle hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=f\big\rangle.

and

B_{R}(e,f)=R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle\big/\big\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*},hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=f\big\rangle.

are not necessarily isomorphic, as the following example shows.

Suppose that
\mathsf{k}
is a field (or more generally an integral domain). Let
e=f\in\mathbb{M}_{2}(\mathsf{k})
be the idempotent matrix which has a one at position
(1,1)
and zeros elsewhere (note that the projective
\mathsf{k}
-module
P
defined by
e
is just
\mathsf{k}
, the free
\mathsf{k}
-module of rank 1).

Let
R
be the free
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
\mathsf{k}\langle h,h^{*}\rangle
subject to the relations
\{hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=e,ehe=h,eh^{*}e=h^{*}\}
. It follows from the relations
ehe=h
,
eh^{*}e=h^{*}
that all entries of
h
and
h^{*}
that are not at position
(1,1)
are zero. So
R
is just the Laurent polynomial ring
\mathsf{k}[x,x^{-1}]
.

Let
S
be the free
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
\mathsf{k}\langle h,h^{*}\rangle
subject to the relations
\{hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=e\}
. Since

e=hh^{*}=hh^{*}hh^{*}=heh^{*}=heeh^{*}

implies
e=eheeh^{*}e,
it follows that
h_{11}h^{*}_{11}=1
. But we also have

h_{11}h^{*}_{11}+h_{12}h^{*}_{21}=(hh^{*})_{11}=e_{11}=1

and hence
h_{12}h^{*}_{21}=0
. If both
h_{12}
and
h^{*}_{21}
are non-zero in
S
, then the ring
S
has zero divisors, while
R=\mathsf{k}[x,x^{-1}]
has not. Suppose now that one of the entries
h_{12}
or
h^{*}_{21}
is zero in
S
. Clearly

h^{*}_{21}h_{12}+h^{*}_{22}h_{22}=(h^{*}h)_{22}=e_{22}=0

and hence
h^{*}_{22}h_{22}=0
since
h_{12}=0
or
h^{*}_{21}=0
. We show that
h^{*}_{22}
and
h_{22}
are non-zero in
S
, which implies that
S
has zero divisors.

Let
R^{\prime}
be the ring obtained from
R=\mathsf{k}[x,x^{-1}]
by adjoining two elements
y
and
z
subject to
yz=0
and
zy=0
. Then
y
and
z
are non-zero in
R^{\prime}
. Since

\operatorname{diag}(x,y)\operatorname{diag}(x^{-1},z)=\operatorname{diag}(1,0)=\operatorname{diag}(x^{-1},z)\operatorname{diag}(x,y),

there is a ring homomorphism
S\rightarrow R^{\prime}
mapping any
r\in R
to itself, and

h_{11}\mapsto x,h^{*}_{11}\mapsto x^{-1},h_{22}\mapsto y,h^{*}_{22}\mapsto z,

and the other entries of
h
and
h^{*}
to zero. Since the image of
h_{22}
under this homomorphism is non-zero,
h_{22}
must be non-zero as well. Similarly it follows that
h^{*}_{22}
is non-zero.
Thus
S
has zero divisors while
R
has not.

The rings introduced in Definition
4.1
, come directly from Bergman’s machinery of universal constructions, by working with idempotent matrices instead of projective modules. Let
P
and
Q
be non-zero finitely generated projective
R
-modules. In
[
7
]
, Bergman constructed a ring

S_{1}=R\big\langle h:\overline{P}\rightarrow\overline{Q}\big\rangle

such that there is a “universal”
S_{1}
-module homomorphism

h:S_{1}\otimes_{R}P\rightarrow S_{1}\otimes_{R}Q
. He further constructed universal algebras

S_{2}=R\big\langle h,h^{*}:\overline{P}\rightarrow\overline{Q},\overline{Q}\rightarrow\overline{P}\big\rangle

and

S_{3}=R\big\langle h,h^{*}:\overline{P}\rightarrow\overline{Q},\overline{Q}\rightarrow\overline{P},h^{*}h=1\big\rangle

and finally

S=S_{4}=R\big\langle h,h^{*}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle.

Thus, over
S
we have
S\otimes_{R}P\cong S\otimes_{R}Q
.

Representing
P
and
Q
as idempotent matrices
e
and
f
, respectively, in §
5.3
we will establish that the rings
S_{i}
, above coincide with the Bergman algebras
B^{i}_{R}(e,f)
,
1\leq i\leq 4
, in Definition
4.1
, which explains our terminology. In fact in §
5.3
we will carry out the graded version of these constructions. By setting the grade group trivial we obtain the above statements. We note that the paper
[
7
]
works with right modules whereas we work here with left modules.

We can extend the definition of Bergman algebras to families of pairs of idempotents. Let

(e,f):=\{(e_{i},f_{i})\}_{i\in I}
, be a collection of pairs of idempotents. Then we define

B_{R}(e,f):=R\big\langle h_{i},h_{i}^{*}\mid i\in I\big\rangle\big/\big\langle e_{i}h_{i}f_{i}=h_{i},f_{i}h_{i}^{*}e_{i}=h_{i}^{*},h_{i}h_{i}^{*}=e_{i},h_{i}^{*}h_{i}=f_{i}\mid i\in I\big\rangle.

(4.2)

Similarly we define
B^{i}_{R}(e,f)
, for
1\leq i\leq 3
as well as
B_{R}(e)
.

Example 4.7
.

Let
(e,f)
be the collection
\{(1,1),(1,1)\}
. Then

B_{\mathsf{k}}(e,f)=\mathsf{k}\langle x,x^{-1},y,y^{-1}\rangle,
the (non-commutative) Laurent polynomial ring with two variables.

Next we define the graded version of the Bergman algebras of Definition
4.1
.

Definition 4.8
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra, where
\mathsf{k}
is a
\Gamma
-graded commutative ring. Let
e\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)(\overline{\beta})_{0}
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\gamma})_{0}
be idempotent matrices, where
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{m})
and
\overline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n})
. Furthermore, let

h=(h_{ij})
and

h^{*}=(h^{*}_{ji})
, where
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
, be a collection of symbols. To the symbols

h_{ij}
assign the degree
\gamma_{j}-\beta_{i}
and
h^{*}_{ji}
the degree
\beta_{i}-\gamma_{j}
for
1\leq i\leq m
and
1\leq j\leq n
. Then the Bergman algebra
B_{R}^{i}(e,f)
defined in Definition
4.1
form
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. For

(e,f):=\{(e_{i},f_{i})\}_{i\in I}
, a collection of pairs of homogeneous idempotents, the graded Bergman algebra
B^{i}_{R}(e,f)
is defined similarly as in (
4.2
)

It has already been established in the literature that Leavitt path algebras can be realised as Bergman algebras (using the module theoretical language). This identification was then used to describe the non-stable
K
-theory of Leavitt path algebras (Theorem 3.2.5 in
[
1
]
).

Lemma 4.9
.

Suppose that
R=\Pi_{t\in T}\mathsf{k}
, where
\mathsf{k}
is field and
T
some finite set. For any
t\in T
, let
\epsilon_{t}
be the element of
R
whose
t
-component is
1
and whose other components are
0
. Then any idempotent matrix with entries in
R
is equivalent to a matrix of the form
\operatorname{diag}(\epsilon_{t_{1}},\dots,\epsilon_{t_{n}})
, where
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T
.

Proof.

Recall that there is a
1-1
correspondence between equivalence classes of idempotent matrices over
R
and isomorphism classes of finitely generated projective
R
-modules. For any
t\in T
, let
P_{t}=R\epsilon_{t}
. It is well-known that any finitely generated projective
R
-module
P
is isomorphic to a direct sum
P=P_{t_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus P_{t_{n}}
, where
t_{1},\dots,t_{n}\in T
. It follows that the corresponding idempotent matrix equals (up to equivalence)
(\epsilon_{t_{1}})\oplus\dots\oplus(\epsilon_{t_{n}})=\operatorname{diag}(\epsilon_{t_{1}},\dots,\epsilon_{t_{n}})
.
∎

The following theorem shows that if the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
is commutative
\mathsf{k}
-semisimple, then the Bergman algebras
B_{R}(e,f)
of (
4.2
) coincide with the class of hyper Leavitt path algebras of Section
3.1
.

Theorem 4.10
.

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a field,
\Gamma
an abelian group and
R
a commutative semisimple
\mathsf{k}
-algebra considered as
\Gamma
-graded algebra concentrated in degree zero. Let
(e,f)
be a family of pairs of homogeneous idempotents. Then the Bergman algebra
B_{R}(e,f)
as defined in
(
4.2
)
is
\Gamma
-graded isomorphic to a unital hyper Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
whose grading is induced by a weight map. Conversely, any unital hyper Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
whose grading is induced by a weight map is graded isomorphic to a Bergman algebra
B_{R}(e,f)
, where
R
is a commutative semisimple
\mathsf{k}
-algebra concentrated in degree zero.

Proof.

First we show that if
R
is a
\Gamma
-graded commutative semisimple
\mathsf{k}
-algebra concentrated in degree zero, then
B_{R}(e,f)
is graded isomorphic to a unital hyper Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
whose grading is induced by a weight map. Write
R=\Pi_{t\in T}\mathsf{k}
, where
T
is some finite set. For any
t\in T
we let
\epsilon_{t}
be the element of
R
whose
t
-component is
1
and whose other components are
0
. Moreover, write
(e,f)=\{(e_{u},f_{u})\}_{u\in U}
, where for any
u\in U
,
e_{u}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{m_{u}}(R)(\overline{\alpha}_{u})_{0}
and
f_{u}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n_{u}}(R)(\overline{\beta}_{u})_{0}
,
\overline{\alpha}_{u}\in\Gamma^{m_{u}}
and
\overline{\beta}_{u}\in\Gamma^{n_{u}}
, are homogeneous idempotent matrices. Since
R=\Pi_{t\in T}\mathsf{k}
, by Lemma
4.5
and
4.9
, we may assume that for any
u\in U
,
e_{u}=\operatorname{diag}(\epsilon_{t_{u,1}},\dots,\epsilon_{t_{u,m_{u}}})
and
f_{u}=\operatorname{diag}(\epsilon_{t^{\prime}_{u,1}},\dots,\epsilon_{t^{\prime}_{u,n_{u}}})
, for some
t_{u,1},\dots,t_{u,m_{u}},t^{\prime}_{u,1},\dots,t^{\prime}_{u,n_{u}}\in T
. Define a hypergraph
H=(H^{0},H^{1},s,r)
by
H^{0}=\{v_{t}\mid t\in T\}
,
H^{1}=\{h_{u}\mid u\in U\}
,
I_{h_{u}}=\{1,\dots,m_{u}\}~(h_{u}\in H^{1})
,
J_{h_{u}}=\{1,\dots,n_{u}\}~(h_{u}\in H^{1})
,
s(h_{u})_{i}=v_{t_{u,i}}~(h_{u}\in H^{1},i\in I_{h_{u}})
and
r(u)_{j}=v_{t^{\prime}_{u,j}}~(h_{u}\in H^{1},j\in J_{h_{u}})
. It follows from Remark
3.2
that
B_{R}(e,f)\cong L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
as
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. Since
H^{0}=T
is finite,
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
is unital (see
[
24
, Proposition 8]
). Now define a
\Gamma
-weight map
w
for
H
by
w((h_{u})_{ij})=(\beta_{u})_{j}-(\alpha_{u})_{i}
for any
h_{u}\in H^{1}
,
i\in I_{h_{u}}
and
j\in J_{h_{u}}
. One checks easily that
w
is indeed a
\Gamma
-weight map for
H
, and that the isomorphism
B_{R}(e,f)\cong L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
is graded with respect to the
\Gamma
-grading on
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
induced by
w
.

Now let
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
be a unital hyper Leavitt path algebra whose grading is induced by a
\Gamma
-weight map
w
. Since
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
is unital,
H^{0}
is finite. Let
R:=\Pi_{v\in H^{0}}\mathsf{k}
. For any
v\in H^{0}
we let
\epsilon_{v}
be the element of
R
whose
v
-component is
1
and whose other components are
0
. We may assume that for any
h\in H^{1}
,
I_{h}=\{1,\dots,m_{h}\}
and
J_{h}=\{1,\dots,n_{h}\}
, and moreover that
i_{h}=1=j_{h}
. Since
w
is a
\Gamma
-weight map for
H
, we have
w(h_{ij})=w(h_{i1})-w(h_{11})+w(h_{1j})
for any
h\in H^{1}
. For any
h\in H^{1}
,
1\leq i\leq m_{h}
and
1\leq j\leq n_{h}
set
\alpha_{h,i}:=-w(h_{i1})+w(h_{11})
and
\beta_{h,j}:=w(h_{1j})
. Moreover, set
\overline{\alpha}_{h}:=(\alpha_{h,1},\dots,\alpha_{h,m_{h}})
and
\overline{\beta}_{h}:=(\beta_{h,1},\dots,\beta_{h,n_{h}})
. For any
h\in H^{1}
let
e_{h}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{m_{h}}(R)(\overline{\alpha}_{h})_{0}
be the idempotent matrix whose entry at position
(i,i^{\prime})
is
\delta_{ii^{\prime}}\epsilon_{s(h)_{i}}
, and
f_{h}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n_{h}}(R)(\overline{\beta}_{h})_{0}
the idempotent matrix whose entry at position
(j,j^{\prime})
is
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\epsilon_{r(h)_{j}}
. It follows from Remark
3.2
that
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)\cong B_{R}(e,f)
as
\mathsf{k}
-algebras, where
(e,f)=\{(e_{h},f_{h})\}_{h\in H^{1}}
. One checks easily that this isomorphism is graded as well.
∎

5.
Graded Bergman universal construction

In this section we start by extending Bergman’s work to the graded setting. We will see that if one wants to realise a conical
\Gamma
-monoid as a non-stable
K
-theory of a ring, that ring needs to be
\Gamma
-graded and the type of
K
-theory to be considered should be the graded
K
-theory.

Throughout this section,
\Gamma
is an abelian group,
\mathsf{k}
a commutative
\Gamma
-graded ring and
R
a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra, i.e.,
R
is equipped with a graded homomorphism
\mathsf{k}\rightarrow Z(A)
.

5.1.
Graded universal morphisms

We start with a simple lemma which will be used throughout the paper.

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
F,M
and
X,Y
be
\Gamma
-graded
R
-modules with the exact sequence of
\Gamma
-graded
R
-modules

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0,}

and a graded idempotent endomorphism
e:F\xrightarrow{}F
(i.e.,
e\circ e=e
). Set
P:={\rm Im}\,e
. Then for any graded module homomorphism
g:M\xrightarrow{}P
, there is a unique graded module homomorphism
h:X\xrightarrow{}F
in the following diagram

\textstyle{Y\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{X\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{M\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{F\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{e}
\scriptstyle{1-e}
\textstyle{P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{F}

satisfying
h\circ u=0
,
(1-e)\circ h=0
and
e\circ h=g\circ\varepsilon
. On the other hand, for any graded module homomorphism
h:X\xrightarrow{}F
with
h\circ u=0=(1-e)\circ h
, there is a unique graded module homomorphism
g:M\xrightarrow{}P
such that
e\circ h=g\circ\varepsilon
. Furthermore, we have that
g:M\xrightarrow{}P
