is the zero map if and only if the corresponding
h:X\xrightarrow{}F
is the zero map.

Proof.

For the idempotent map
e:F\xrightarrow{}F
, it is a fact that

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\operatorname{Ker}e={\rm Im}(1-e),&\\
{\rm Im}\,e=\operatorname{Ker}(1-e),&\\
F=\operatorname{Ker}e\oplus{\rm Im}\,e.&\end{cases}

Define
h(x)=(g\circ\varepsilon)(x)
for any
x\in X
. As
e(p)=p
for any
p\in P
, we have
e\circ h=h=g\circ\varepsilon
. Hence
(1-e)\circ h=(1-e)\circ e\circ h=0
and
h\circ u=g\circ\varepsilon\circ u=0
. For the uniqueness of
h
, suppose that there exists
h^{\prime}:X\xrightarrow{}F
such that
e\circ h^{\prime}=g\circ\varepsilon
,
h^{\prime}\circ u=0
and
(1-e)\circ h^{\prime}=0
. Then
h^{\prime}(x)=h^{\prime}(x)-((1-e)\circ h^{\prime})(x)=e\circ h^{\prime})(x)=(g\circ\varepsilon)(x)=h(x)
for any
x\in X
. Therefore
h^{\prime}=h
. One observes that if
g:M\xrightarrow{}P
is the zero map, one takes
h
to be the zero map which is the desired map. Obviously if
h:X\xrightarrow{}F
is the zero map, then
g=0
.

On the other hand, when
h\circ u=0
by the universal property of cokernel there exists
\tau:M\xrightarrow{}F
such that
h=\tau\circ\varepsilon
. Set
g=e\circ\tau
. Then
e\circ h=g\circ\varepsilon
. For the uniqueness of
g
, suppose there exists another
g^{\prime}:M\xrightarrow{}{}P
satisfying
e\circ h=g^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon
. Then we have
g\circ\varepsilon=e\circ h=g^{\prime}\circ\varepsilon
. Since
\varepsilon
is surjective, we have
g=g^{\prime}
.
∎

We are in position to extend two crucial theorems of Bergman on universal ring constructions
[
7
, Theorems 3.1 and 3.2]
to the setting of graded rings.

Theorem 5.2
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Suppose that
M
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R
-module and
P
a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. Then there exists a
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
,
S
, with a universal graded module homomorphism
f:S\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow{}S\otimes_{R}P
; that is, given any
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
,
T
, and any graded
T
-module homomorphism
g:T\otimes_{R}M\rightarrow{}T\otimes_{R}P
, there exists a unique graded homomorphism
S\rightarrow{}T
of
R\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
such that
g=T\otimes_{S}f
.

Proof.

We first write
M
as a cokernel of the graded
R
-module homomorphism
u:\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
of graded free
R
-modules; that is

\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon}}{{\longrightarrow}}M\longrightarrow 0,

is an exact sequence of graded
R
-modules. Using the matrix representation of §
2.3
, we have

u=(u_{ij})_{i\in I,j\in J}\in\mathbb{M}_{I\times J}(R)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\beta}].

Here
\overline{\alpha}=(\alpha_{i})_{i\in I}
and
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{j})_{j\in J}
.
We also write
P
as a direct summand of a graded free
R
-module
\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})
(see (
2.4
)). Hence,
P
can be written as the image of a graded idempotent endomorphism
e:\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})
. Similarly, by §
2.3
we have
1-e=(v_{mm^{\prime}})_{m,m^{\prime}\in K}\in\mathbb{M}_{K\times K}(R)[\overline{\gamma}][\overline{\gamma}]
with
\overline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{m})_{m\in K}
.

For any
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
and any graded module homomorphism
g:T\otimes_{R}M\xrightarrow{}T\otimes_{R}P
, we have the following diagram with the first two rows exact

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 71.53676pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 71.53676pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j\in 208.72113pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-0.8264pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 208.72113pt\raise-24.66669pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 253.0133pt\raise K}R(\gamma_{m}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 172.42026pt\raise-26.16112pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 189.92026pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 115.88005pt\raise-48.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 115.88005pt\raise-56.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 189.92026pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 253.0133pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 253.0133pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 67.33115pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m}))}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 205.72113pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 255.5133pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(5.1)

where by Lemma
5.1
,
g
is uniquely determined by a graded module homomorphism
h
with
h\circ\overline{u}=0=\overline{1-e}\circ h
.
But in matrix form,

h=(h_{jm})_{j\in J,m\in K}\in\mathbb{M}_{J\times K}(T)[\overline{\beta}][\overline{\gamma}],

with
h_{jm}\in T_{\gamma_{m}-\beta_{j}}
.
The condition
h\circ\overline{u}=0
is equivalent to

(u_{ij})_{i\in I,j\in J}\cdot(h_{jk})_{j\in J,m\in K}=0

(5.2)

in
\mathbb{M}_{I\times K}(T)[\overline{\alpha}][\overline{\gamma}]
, that is,

\sum_{j\in J}u_{ij}h_{jm}=0

(5.3)

for
i\in I,m\in K
. Similarly, the condition
\overline{1-e}\circ h=0
is equivalent to

(h_{jm})_{j\in J,m\in K}\cdot(v_{mm^{\prime}})_{m,m^{\prime}\in K}=0

(5.4)

in
\mathbb{M}_{J\times K}(T)[\overline{\beta}][\overline{\gamma}]
, that is

\sum_{m\in K}h_{jm}v_{mm^{\prime}}=0

(5.5)

for
j\in J,m^{\prime}\in K
.

Let us define
S_{0}
as the
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
obtained by adjoining generators
h_{jm}
, for
j\in J,m\in K
to the ring
R
. Set the degrees of
h_{jm}
to
\gamma_{m}-\beta_{j}
, where
j\in J
and
m\in K
. Then
S_{0}
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
. Define
S
to be the quotient of
S_{0}
subject to the two relations (
5.3
) and (
5.5
) which are homogeneous of degree
\gamma_{m}-\alpha_{i}
and
\gamma_{m^{\prime}}-\beta_{j}
, respectively, in the graded ring
S_{0}
. Then
S
is the desired
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
.
∎

Remark 5.3
.

(1)

The
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

S
is independent of the choices of resolutions for
M
and
P
. One observes that
S
has the universal property for fixed resolutions of
M
and
P
.

(2)

We prove that applying the forgetful functor
\mathcal{F}
to the
\Gamma
-graded ring
S
with a universal graded module homomorphism in Theorem
5.2
we have the ring in Bergman’s Theorem 3.1
[
7
]
. Actually when we apply the forgetful functor
\mathcal{F}
to the graded projective resolution
\bigoplus_{i\in I}R(\alpha_{i})\xrightarrow{u}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}M\xrightarrow{}0
, we have the non-graded projective resolution
\bigoplus_{i\in I}R\xrightarrow{u}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}M\xrightarrow{}0
. Similarly if we apply the forgetful functor
\mathcal{F}
to the idempotent endomorphism
e:\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})
, then we have the idempotent endomorphism
e:\bigoplus_{K}R\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{K}R
.

Given us any
R\text{-}\text{ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T^{\prime}
and any module homomorphism
g^{\prime}:T^{\prime}\otimes_{R}M\xrightarrow{}T^{\prime}\otimes_{R}P
,

3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(5.6)

where
h^{\prime}
is uniquely determined by
g^{\prime}
; see Lemma
5.1
. This
h^{\prime}
gives us a ring homomorphism
S\xrightarrow{}T^{\prime}
. Hence
S
satisfies the universal property as the ring in Bergman’s Theorem 3.1. Therefore
S
is the ring in Bergman’s Theorem 3.1.

(3)

The desired
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
S
in Theorem
5.2
has the graded
\mathsf{k}
-homomorphism
R\xrightarrow{}S
which sends
r
to
\overline{r}
for
r\in R
.

Theorem 5.4
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra,
M
a graded
R
-module,
P
a graded projective
R
-module, and
f:M\xrightarrow{}P
a graded module homomorphism. Then there exists a
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

S
such that
S\otimes_{R}f=0
, and
S
is universal for this property: Given any
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
with
T\otimes_{R}f=0
, there exists a unique graded homomorphism of
R\text{-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
,
S\xrightarrow{}T
.

Proof.

We write
P
as a direct summand of a graded free module
\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})
. Then for any graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
, the map
\overline{i}:T\otimes_{R}P\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{m\in K}T(\gamma_{m})
induced by the inclusion
i:P\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{m\in K}R(\gamma_{m})
is again an inclusion. Hence for each homogeneous elements
x\in M
, we write

f(x)=\sum_{m\in K}f_{mx}

where
f_{mx}
is a homogeneous element in
R(\gamma_{m})
. Observe that for any
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
,
T\otimes_{R}f=0
if and only if for any homogeneous elements
x\in\bigcup_{\alpha\in\Gamma}M_{\alpha}
all the
f_{mx}
is zero in
T
. So the desired universal ring
S
is the quotient of
R
by the two-sided ideal generated by these homogeneous elements
f_{mx}
. ∎

The ring constructed in Theorem
5.2
will be denoted by
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
(or
R\langle\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
), and that of Theorem
5.4
by
R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle
. Note that the graded
\mathsf{k}
-homomorphism from
R
to
R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle
in Theorem
5.4
is a canonical projection.

5.1.1.

Combining Theorems
5.2
and
5.4
we can construct graded rings with rich structures.
Let
f:P\rightarrow Q
be a module homomorphism between graded finitely generated projective

R
-modules
P
and
Q
. By Theorem
5.2
we construct a graded ring with a homomorphism
g:\overline{Q}\rightarrow\overline{P}
and then by two applications of Theorem
5.4
we set
1-g\overline{f}
and
1-\overline{f}g
to zero. We denote the graded ring obtained by
R\langle{\overline{f}}^{-1}\rangle
where the homomorphism
\overline{f}
is now invertible.

In a similar fashion, for graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules
P
and
Q
, we can adjoin a universal graded isomorphism between
\overline{P}
and
\overline{Q}
by first freely adjoining a graded map
\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q}
, then adjoining an inverse. We denote the resulting
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
by

R\langle i,i^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\overline{Q}\rangle.

Given a single graded finitely generated projective module
P
, one may obtain a
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
by adjoining to
R
a universal idempotent graded endomorphism of
\overline{P}
. This ring will be denoted by

R\langle i:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P};i^{2}=i\rangle.

5.2.
Universal morphisms for strongly graded rings

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a commutative
\Gamma
-graded ring and
R
a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. As mentioned in the introduction, in certain sections, such as §
6
and §
7.2
we need to assume that the graded ring
\mathsf{k}
is concentrated in degree zero. Here we also need to make this assumption, namely,
R
is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra, where
\mathsf{k}
is concentrated in degree zero. Note that, by our assumption
\mathsf{k}\subseteq R_{0}
.
Let
M
be a
\Gamma
-graded
R
-module and
P
a
\Gamma
-graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. It is natural to compare the Bergman algebras
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
and
R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle
, in case
P_{0}
is a finitely generated projective
R_{0}
-module. We will show that if
R
is strongly graded, then there is a
\Gamma
-graded isomorphism of algebras,

R\langle f:\overline{M}\longrightarrow\overline{P}\rangle\cong R\ast_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\longrightarrow\overline{P_{0}}\rangle.

This ties the two representable functors (
1.4
) and (
1.5
) together.

We first consider the grading for coproducts of two
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and
S
an
R_{0}\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
with
\Gamma
-grading. Then the coproduct
R\ast_{R_{0}}S
has a
\Gamma
-grading. The coproduct is, as a graded vector space, the space of all words in
R
and
S
, and the homogeneous elements of pure degree are words where the letters are homogeneous, and the degree is given by the sum of the degrees. One observes that when
S
is an
R_{0}\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
with trivial grading, the zeroth component of the coproduct
R\ast_{R_{0}}S
contains
R_{0}\ast_{R_{0}}S\cong S
. And the zeroth component of
R\ast_{R_{0}}S
can contain elements such as
rsr^{\prime}
, where
r,r^{\prime}\in R
are homogeneous and the sum of the degrees are zero.

Proposition 5.5
.

Let
\mathsf{k}
be a commutative
\Gamma
-graded ring concentrated in degree zero, and
R
a strongly
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Suppose that
M
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R
-module and
P
a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. Then we have the following isomorphism of
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras

R\ast_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\longrightarrow\overline{P_{0}}\rangle\cong R\langle f:\overline{M}\longrightarrow\overline{P}\rangle.

Proof.

Choose a projective resolution

\bigoplus_{i\in I}R_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle u_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\bigoplus_{j\in J}R_{0}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\varepsilon_{0}}}{{\longrightarrow}}M_{0}\longrightarrow 0

for
M_{0}
as
R_{0}
-module. Since
P
is a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module, and
R
is strongly graded, its zeroth component
P_{0}
is a finitely generated projective
R_{0}
-module. We write
P_{0}
as the image of the idempotent morphism
e_{0}:\bigoplus_{m\in K}R_{0}\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{m\in K}R_{0}
of
R_{0}
-modules.

For any
R_{0}\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T_{0}
and any module homomorphism
g_{0}:T_{0}\otimes_{R_{0}}M_{0}\xrightarrow{}T_{0}\otimes_{R_{0}}P_{0}
, we have the following diagram with the first two rows exact

where by Lemma

,

is uniquely determined by a module homomorphism

. Then we have

Here

and

) and (

); compare Remark

(2).

Since the graded ring

and we have a projective resolution of

-graded

where by Lemma
5.1
,
g
is uniquely determined by a graded module homomorphism
h
with
h\circ\overline{u_{0}}=0=\overline{1-e_{0}}\circ h
. Then we have

\begin{split}R\langle f:\overline{M}\longrightarrow\overline{P}\rangle&\cong R\langle h\rangle/\langle u_{0}h=0=h(1-e_{0})\rangle\end{split}

(5.7)

By Theorem
5.2
,
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. One observes that by the proof of Theorem
5.2
the elements of the matrix
h
as additional generators of
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
are of zero degree. By Lemma
5.1
, it follows that the universal homomorphism of
R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle
is the restriction of the universal homomorphism of
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
to the zeroth component.

Now we prove that

R\ast_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle\cong R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
as
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. One notes that we have the natural inclusion
\iota:R_{0}\xrightarrow{}R
, and the natural homomorphisms
\pi:R_{0}\xrightarrow{}R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle
sending
r_{0}
in
R_{0}
to
\overline{r_{0}}
, as well as
\pi^{\prime}:R\xrightarrow{}R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
sending
r
in
R
to
\overline{r}
. The homomorphism
\varphi:R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle\xrightarrow{}R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
is induced by the homomorphism
R_{0}\langle h_{0}\rangle\xrightarrow{}R\langle h\rangle
since the matrix
h
is induced by
h_{0}
, equivalently, we have
\varphi(r_{0})=r_{0}
for
r_{0}\in R_{0}
and
\varphi(h_{ij})=h_{ij}
for
h_{0}=(h_{ij})
. It follows directly that in the diagram below,
\varphi\circ\pi=\pi^{\prime}\circ\iota
. In order to prove that
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
is the coproduct
R*_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle
, one only needs to check that
R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
has the universal property. That is, taking any
R_{0}\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

S
and any homomorphisms
\alpha:R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle\xrightarrow{}S
and
\beta:R\xrightarrow{}S
with
\alpha\circ\pi=\beta\circ\iota
, we need to show that there exists a unique homomorphism
\theta:R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle\xrightarrow{}S
in the following diagram

\textstyle{R_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\beta}
\textstyle{R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\varphi}
\textstyle{R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{S}

such that
\alpha=\theta\circ\varphi
and
\beta=\theta\circ\pi^{\prime}
. First we define
\theta:R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle\xrightarrow{}S
, where
\theta(r)=\beta(r)
, for
r\in R
and
\theta(h_{ij})=\alpha(h_{ij})
for
h=(h_{ij})
. We observe that
\theta
is well defined as
\alpha
sends the generators of the two-sided ideal
\langle u_{0}h_{0}=0=h_{0}(1-e_{0})\rangle
to zero. And we can check directly that
\alpha=\theta\circ\varphi
and
\beta=\theta\circ\pi^{\prime}
. The uniqueness of
\theta
follows directly from the commutativity of the two triangles in the above diagram. One observes that
\theta
preserves the grading in the graded algebras setting. Therefore, we obtain that
R\ast_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle f_{0}:\overline{M_{0}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{0}}\rangle\cong R\langle f:\overline{M}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
as
\mathsf{k}
-algebras.
∎

Proposition 5.6
.

Let
R
be a strongly
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Suppose that
M
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R
-module,
P
a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module and
f:M\xrightarrow{}P
is a graded module homomorphism. Then we have the following isomorphism of
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras

R\ast_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle\overline{f_{0}}=0\rangle\cong R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle.

Here, we have
f_{0}=(-)_{0}(f)
. Furthermore, the zeroth component of the
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle
is isomorphic to
R_{0}\langle\overline{f_{0}}=0\rangle
.

Proof.

As
R
is a strongly
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and
P
is a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module,
P_{0}
is a finitely generated projective
R_{0}
-module. We write
P_{0}
as the summand of the free
R_{0}
-module
\bigoplus_{c\in C}R_{0}
. The fact that it is a direct summand insures that for any

R_{0}\text{-}{\rm ring}_{\mathsf{k}_{0}}

T
, the map
\overline{i}:T\otimes_{R_{0}}P_{0}\xrightarrow{}T\otimes_{R_{0}}(\bigoplus_{c\in C}R_{0})
induced by the inclusion
i:P_{0}\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{c\in C}R_{0}
is again an inclusion. Hence if we write for each
m_{0}\in M_{0}

f_{0}(m_{0})=\sum_{c\in C}f_{m_{0},c}

with
f_{m_{0},c}
in the component
R_{0}
corresponding to
c
. We can see that for any
R_{0}\text{-}{\rm ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
,
T\otimes_{R_{0}}f_{0}
will be zero if and only
if all the
f_{m_{0},c}
go to zero in
T
. Hence

R_{0}\langle\overline{f_{0}}=0\rangle\cong R_{0}/\langle f_{m_{0},c}\rangle_{m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C}.

Here
\langle f_{m_{0},c}\rangle_{m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C}
is the two-sided ideal of
R_{0}
generated by the elements
f_{m_{0},c}
for
m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C
.

One observes that
R\otimes_{R_{0}}P_{0}
is a direct summand of the free
R
-module
R\otimes_{R_{0}}(\bigoplus_{c\in C}R_{0})\cong\bigoplus_{c\in C}R
. Actually we have the maps

R\otimes_{R_{0}}M_{0}\longrightarrow{}R\otimes_{R_{0}}P_{0}\longrightarrow{}R\otimes_{R_{0}}(\bigoplus_{c\in C}R_{0})\cong\bigoplus_{c\in C}R.

We write for each
m_{0}\in M_{0}

R\otimes_{R_{0}}f_{0}(r\otimes m_{0})=\sum_{c\in C}rf_{m_{0},c}

with
r\in R
a homogeneous element, and
f_{m_{0},c}
in the component
R_{0}
corresponding to
c
. For any
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-}{\rm ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T^{\prime}
,
T^{\prime}\otimes_{R}f
will be zero if and only
if
T^{\prime}\otimes_{R}(R\otimes_{R_{0}}f_{0})
will be zero, if and only if all the
rf_{m_{0},c}
go to zero in
T^{\prime}
for any homogeneous elements
r\in R
,
c\in C
and
m_{0}\in M_{0}
. Hence
R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle\cong R/\langle rf_{m_{0},c}\rangle_{r\in R_{h},m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C}
. Here
R_{h}
is the set consisting of all homogeneous elements of
R
and
\langle rf_{m_{0},c}\rangle_{m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C}
is the two-sided ideal of
R
generated by the homogeneous elements
rf_{m_{0},c}
for
m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C
. Hence we have

R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle\cong R/\langle rf_{m_{0},c}\rangle_{r\in R_{h},m_{0}\in M_{0},c\in C}.

One observes that we obtain the following commutative diagram with
\varphi_{1}(r)=r
, for
r\in R
and
\psi_{1}(\overline{r_{0}})=\overline{r_{0}}
, for
r_{0}\in R_{0}
.

\textstyle{R_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\scriptstyle{\iota}
\textstyle{R_{0}\langle\overline{f_{0}}=0\rangle\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varphi_{1}}
\textstyle{R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle}

Here
\iota:R_{0}\xrightarrow{}R
is the inclusion and
\pi:R_{0}\xrightarrow{}R_{0}\langle\overline{f_{0}}=0\rangle
is the natural projection. One can check directly that
R\langle\overline{f}=0\rangle
satisfies the universal property of
R\ast_{R_{0}}R_{0}\langle\overline{f_{0}}=0\rangle
as
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. The proof is completed.
∎

Let
R
be a strongly
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Suppose that
M
and
P
be
\Gamma
-graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. Then we have the following two universal algebras:

R_{0}\langle i_{0},i^{-1}_{0}:\overline{M}_{0}\cong\overline{P}_{0}\rangle\text{ and }R\langle i,i^{-1}:\overline{M}\cong\overline{P}\rangle.

It would be interesting to establish a relation between these two algebras and
R\langle i,i^{-1}:\overline{M}\cong\overline{P}\rangle_{0}
similar to Proposition
5.5
.

5.3.
Matrix forms for three types of Bergman algebras

Let
\Gamma
be an abelian group,
\mathsf{k}
a
\Gamma
-graded commutative ring and
R
a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra.

Let
P
and
Q
be non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. Applying Theorem
5.2
we obtain a
\Gamma
-graded ring
R\langle i:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q}\rangle
. Recall from §
5.1.1
that we can also construct the graded rings
R\langle i,i^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\overline{Q}\rangle
and
R\big\langle i:\overline{P}\to\overline{P};~i^{2}=i\big\rangle
.
Further if
g:P\rightarrow Q
is a graded
R
-module homomorhism, then
R\langle\overline{g}^{-1}\rangle
is the universal ring which
g
becomes invertible. In this section, by representing the graded finitely generated projective modules by idempotent matrices,
we describe these
\Gamma
-graded rings via generators and relations,
in Lemmas
5.8
,
5.9
and
5.10
. Once the grade group
\Gamma
is set to be trivial, we obtain the Bergman algebras of Definition
4.1
.

Since
P
is graded finitely generated
R
-module, using (
2.4
), we can write
P
as the image of a graded idempotent endomorphism
e
of a graded free
R
-module of finite rank
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})
with
\beta_{j}\in\Gamma
. Similarly, we write
Q
as the image of a graded idempotent endomorphism
f
of a graded free
R
-module of finite rank
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k})
with
\gamma_{k}\in\Gamma
.

By Lemma
5.1
there is a unique graded homomorphism
h_{g}:\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k})
corresponding to the given graded module homomorphism
g:P\xrightarrow{}Q
such that we have the following diagram

(5.8)

with the first two rows exact, the right square commutative and
h_{g}\circ(1-e)=0
and
(1-f)\circ h_{g}=0
. One can represent
h_{g}
as a matrix
h_{g}\in\mathbb{M}_{m\times n}(R)[\overline{\beta}][\overline{\gamma}]
, where
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})
and
\overline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m})
(see(
2.8
)).
So Lemma
5.1
, one observes that there is a one-to-one correspondence between the set of graded
R
-module homomorphisms
g:P\xrightarrow{}Q
and the set of
R
-module homomorphisms
h_{g}:\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{m})
satisfying
eh_{g}f=h_{g}
.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and let
P
and
Q
be non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules.

(1)

We have that
R\langle i:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q}\rangle\cong R\langle h\rangle/{\langle ehf=h\rangle}
as
\Gamma
-graded algebras for some
n\times m
-matrix
h=(h_{jk})
with
h_{jk}
symbols of degree
\gamma_{k}-\beta_{j}\in\Gamma
, for
j=1,\cdots,n
and
k=1,\cdots,m
and homogeneous idempotents
e\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\beta})_{0}
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)(\overline{\gamma})_{0}
, where
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})
and
\overline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m})
. Moreover, there is a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
R\xrightarrow{}R\langle\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q}\rangle
sending
r
to
\overline{r}
for any
r\in R
.

(2)

Let
g:P\xrightarrow{}Q
be a graded
R
-module homomorphism. Then

R\langle\overline{g}=0\rangle\cong R/\langle h_{g}\rangle
, where
h_{g}
is the corresponding matrix from
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})
to
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k})
of
(
5.8
)
whose
jk
-th entry is of degree
\gamma_{k}-\beta_{j}\in\Gamma
and
\langle h_{g}\rangle
is the two-sided ideal generated by the entries of
h_{g}
.

Proof.

Writing,
P\bigoplus P^{\prime}\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{n}R(\beta_{i})
, we choose

\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{1-e}\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{e}P\xrightarrow{}0

as the projective resolution of
P
as graded
R
-module. Writing
Q\bigoplus Q^{\prime}\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\bigoplus_{i=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{i})
,
by the proof of Theorem
5.2
, for any
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
and any graded module homomorphsm
g^{\prime}:T\otimes_{R}P\xrightarrow{}T\otimes_{R}Q
, we have the following diagram with the first two rows exact

166.61725pt\raise-26.16112pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.83888pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}$}}}\kern 245.81549pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 245.81549pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{0}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern-3.0pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 68.169pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{T\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{m}))}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 199.96637pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 248.31549pt\raise-64.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces,

(5.9)

For (1), by matrix relations (
5.2
) and (
5.4
), we have the multiplication of matrices

(1-e)h=0
and
h(1-f)=0
, which is equivalent to that
ehf=h
. Thus
R\langle i:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q}\rangle
is isomorphic to
R\langle h\rangle/{\langle ehf=h\rangle}
.

For (2), by Lemma
5.1
, for
g:P\rightarrow Q
, there is a unique
h_{g}
from
\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})
to
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{m})
, and we have
\overline{g}=0
if and only if
h_{g}=0
. By the proof of Theorem
5.4
the desired universal ring
R\langle\overline{g}=0\rangle
is the quotient of
R
by the two-sided ideal generated by entries of the matrix
h_{g}
.
∎

We have the following two consequences which will be used in the following subsections.

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and let
P
and
Q
be non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules.
We have the
\Gamma
-graded algebra isomorphism

R\big\langle i,i^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle\cong R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle/\big\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*},hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=f\big\rangle,

where
h=(h_{jk})
is a
n\times m
-matrix with
h_{jk}
symbols of degree
\gamma_{k}-\beta_{j}
and
h^{*}=(h^{*}_{kj})
is a
m\times n
-matrix with
h^{*}_{kj}
symbols of degree
\beta_{j}-\gamma_{k}
, for
j=1,\cdots,n
and
k=1,\cdots,m
, and homogeneous idempotents
e\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\beta})_{0}
and
f\in\mathbb{M}_{m}(R)(\overline{\gamma})_{0}
, where
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})
and
\overline{\gamma}=(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{m})
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.7
(1) we have that
R\langle i_{0}:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q},i_{0}^{\prime}:\overline{Q}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle\cong R\langle h,h^{*}\rangle/\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*}\rangle
. Now for the
R\text{-gr-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}

S=R\langle i_{0}:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{Q},i_{0}^{\prime}:\overline{Q}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
, we have the following two diagrams (see (
5.9
))

\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-f}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k})}

with the first two rows exact, the right square commutative and
(1-e)h=0
and
h(1-f)=0
, and

\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-f}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f}}
\scriptstyle{h^{*}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}Q\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i_{0}^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})}

with the first two rows exact, the right square commutative and
(1-f)h^{*}=0
and
h^{*}(1-e)=0
. Combining the above two diagrams, we have the following diagram

\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{e-h^{*}\circ h}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\operatorname{id}_{S\otimes P}-i_{0}^{\prime}\circ i_{0}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))}

with the first two rows exact, the right square commutative and the matrix form
(1-e)(e-hh^{*})=0
and
(e-hh^{*})(1-e)=0
. By Lemma
5.1

e-h^{*}\circ h
is the corresponding map for
\operatorname{id}_{S\otimes P}-i_{0}^{\prime}\circ i_{0}
. The corresponding matrix representation of
e-h^{*}\circ h
is
e-hh^{*}
(see
2.7
). Similarly one can show that
f-h^{*}h
is the corresponding matrix of

. By Lemma

(2), we then have

R\big\langle i,i^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle\cong R\big\langle h,h^{*}\big\rangle/\big\langle ehf=h,fh^{*}e=h^{*},hh^{*}=e,h^{*}h=f\big\rangle.

This completes the proof.
∎

Lemma 5.9
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and
P
a non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. We have the
\Gamma
-graded algebra isomorphism

R\big\langle i:\overline{P}\to\overline{P};~i^{2}=i\big\rangle\cong R\big\langle h\big\rangle/\big\langle ehe=h,h^{2}=h\big\rangle,

where
h=(h_{kl})
is a
n\times n
-matrix with
h_{kl}
symbols of degree
\beta_{l}-\beta_{k}
, for
k,l=1,\cdots,n
and
e\in\mathbb{M}_{n}(R)(\overline{\beta})_{0}
, where
\overline{\beta}=(\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n})
is a homogeneoues idempotent.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.7
(1) we have that
R\langle i^{\prime}:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle\cong R\langle h\rangle/\langle ehe=h\rangle
. Now for the graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

S^{\prime}=R\langle i^{\prime}:\overline{P}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle
, we have the following diagram

\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{i^{\prime}}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\overline{e}}
\scriptstyle{\overline{1-e}}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}P\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})}

with the first two rows exact, the right square commutative and
(1-e)h=0
and
h(1-e)=0
. Observe that
h-h^{2}
corresponds to the matrix of
i^{\prime}-i^{\prime}\circ i^{\prime}
(Lemma
5.1
). Now by Lemma
5.7
(2), the proof is completed.
∎

Given a graded homomorphism
g:P\xrightarrow{}Q
between graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules
P
and
Q
, we may construct a
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

R\langle\overline{g}^{-1}\rangle
in which
\overline{g}
become an isomorphism, by adjoining a map
g:\overline{Q}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}
, then setting
1_{\overline{P}}-g\circ\overline{g}
and
1_{\overline{Q}}-\overline{g}\circ g
equal to zero.

Recall that by Lemma
5.1
there is a unique graded homomorphism
h_{g}:\bigoplus_{j=1}^{n}R(\beta_{j})\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{k=1}^{m}R(\gamma_{k})
corresponding to
\gamma:P\xrightarrow{}Q
satisfying
eh_{g}f=h_{g}
.
We can describe the universal localisation algebra of the
R
-module homomorphism
g:P\rightarrow Q
as follows (see
[
9
, page 293]
).

Lemma 5.10
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and
g:P\xrightarrow{}Q
a graded
R
-module homomorphism.
We have the
\Gamma
-graded algebra isomorphism

R\big\langle\overline{g}^{-1}\big\rangle\cong R\big\langle h^{*}\big\rangle/\big\langle fh^{*}e=h,h_{g}h^{*}=e,h^{*}h_{g}=f\big\rangle,

where
h^{*}=(h^{*}_{kj})
is a
m\times n
-matrix with
h^{*}_{kj}
symbols of degree
\beta_{j}-\gamma_{k}
, for
j=1,\cdots,n
and
k=1,\cdots,m
.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.7
(1) we have
S_{1}=R\langle i:\overline{Q}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P}\rangle\cong R\langle h^{*}\rangle/fh^{*}e=h^{*}\rangle
, for some
m\times n
-matrix
h^{*}
. Similarly as in Lemma
5.8
, one has that
h^{*}\circ h_{g}-e
is the unique graded homomorphism corresponding to
i\circ(S_{1}\otimes_{R}g)-1_{S_{1}\otimes_{R}P}
and that
h_{g}\circ h^{*}-f
is the unique graded homomorphism corresponding to
(S_{1}\otimes_{R}g)\circ i-1_{S_{1}\otimes_{R}Q}
. We omit the details here.
∎

5.4.
Universal morphisms and representable functors

We recall some facts on representable functors and universal morphisms.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a small category and
F:\mathcal{C}\xrightarrow{}\operatorname{Sets}
a functor, where
\operatorname{Sets}
is the category of sets with set functions as morphisms. We say that
F
is
representable
if there exists a natural isomorphism
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,-)\xrightarrow{}F
for some object
A
in
\mathcal{C}
. For an element
A
in
\mathcal{C}
, we say that an element
u\in F(A)
is
universal
if for any object
B
in
\mathcal{C}
and
v\in F(B)
, there is a unique morphism
f:A\xrightarrow{}B
with
v=F(f)(u)
. In this case, this property is called the universal property of
(A,u)
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
A
be an object in
\mathcal{C}
. The representations
\alpha:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,-)\xrightarrow{}F
are in bijection to universal elements of the form
(A,u)
via
\alpha\xrightarrow{}\alpha_{A}(1_{A})
.

Proof.

By Yoneda Lemma, the natural transformations
\alpha:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,-)\xrightarrow{}F
correspond to elements in
F(A)
via
\alpha\mapsto\alpha_{A}(1_{A})
. It remains to check that
\alpha
is an isomorphism if and only if
\alpha_{A}(1_{A})
in
F(A)
is an universal element. Actually, for any object
B
in
\mathcal{C}
, we have
\alpha_{B}:\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)\xrightarrow{}F(B)
. By the naturality of
\alpha
, for any morphism
f:A\xrightarrow{}B

in
\mathcal{C}
, we have
\alpha_{B}(f)=\alpha_{B}(f\circ 1_{A})=F(f)(\alpha_{A}(1_{A}))
. Thus
\alpha
is a natural isomorphism if and only if for any object
B
in
\mathcal{C}
, the map
\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathcal{C}}(A,B)\xrightarrow{}F(B),f\mapsto F(f)(\alpha_{A}(1_{A}))
is bijection, which is a restatement of saying that
\alpha_{A}(1_{A})
in
F(A)
is a universal element.
∎

In what follows, we continue with the assumption that
\Gamma
is an abelian group,
\mathsf{k}
a
\Gamma
-graded commutative ring and
R
a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Suppose that
M
is a
\Gamma
-graded
R
-module and
P
a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module.

Theorem
5.2
now says that the functor from
\Gamma
-graded
R\text{-}\text{rings}_{\mathsf{k}}
to
\operatorname{Sets}
, associating to
T
the set
\operatorname{Hom}_{T\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}(T\otimes_{R}M,T\otimes_{R}P)
, is representable. More precisely, we have the functor

\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{\operatorname{gr}}_{M,P}:R\text{-gr-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}&\longrightarrow{}\operatorname{Sets}\\
T&\longmapsto\operatorname{Hom}_{T\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}}(T\otimes_{R}M,T\otimes_{R}P)\end{split}

such that
F^{\operatorname{gr}}_{M,P}(f)=T^{\prime}\otimes_{T}-
for any graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
f:T\xrightarrow{}T^{\prime}
. Then, there is a natural isomorphism
\alpha:\operatorname{Hom}_{R\text{-gr-rings}_{\mathsf{k}}}(S,-)\xrightarrow{}F^{\operatorname{gr}}_{M,P}
with
S
the graded algebra constructed in Theorem
5.2
.

We recall from
[
25
, Chapter 4]
the notion of universal localization of algebras. Let
R
be
an algebra and
\sigma
be a family of morphisms between finitely generated projective
R
-modules. A homomorphism
\theta:R\xrightarrow{}S
of algebras is called
\sigma
-inverting
if for each morphism
\xi\in\sigma
, the morphism
S\otimes_{R}\xi
in
S
-
{\mathbf{Mod}}
is invertible;
\theta
is called a
universal localization
with respect to
\sigma
if in addition each
\sigma
-inverting homomorphism
\theta^{\prime}:R\xrightarrow{}S^{\prime}
factors
uniquely through
\theta
.

We adopt this to the setting of graded algebras. Let
\Gamma
be an abelian group,
R,

S
two
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras and
\Sigma
a family of morphisms between
\Gamma
-graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. A homomorphism
\theta:R\xrightarrow{}S
of
\Gamma
-graded algebras is
graded
\Sigma
-inverting
, if for all
\xi\in\Sigma
, the morphism
S\otimes_{R}\xi
in
S
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
is invertible;
\theta
is called a
graded universal localization with repect to
\Sigma
if in addition every
\Sigma
-inverting homomorphism
\theta^{\prime}:R\xrightarrow{}S^{\prime}
of
\Gamma
-graded algebras factors uniquely through
\theta
. In
[
10
, Proposition 3.1]
, it was shown that, the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded universal localisation exists. Using our graded constructions, here we show that, for any arbitrary abelian group
\Gamma
, the
\Gamma
-graded universal localisation exists.

Recall from §
5.1.1
that given a graded homomorphism
f:P\rightarrow Q
between graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules
P
and
Q
, we can construct a graded ring
R\langle{\overline{f}}^{-1}\rangle
where the homomorphism
\overline{f}
becomes invertible.

Lemma 5.12
.

Let
R
and
S
be
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras and
\Sigma
a family of morphisms between
\Gamma
-graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. If
\theta:R\xrightarrow{}{}S
is a
\Gamma
-graded universal localization with respect to
\Sigma
, then
S\cong R\langle\overline{\xi}^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
.

Proof.

For each
\xi:P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}{}Q_{\xi}
in
\Sigma
, collect the pair of graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules
(Q_{\xi},P_{\xi})
.
By a repeated application of Theorem
5.2
for each pair
(Q_{\xi},P_{\xi})
, the
\Gamma
-graded ring

S_{\Sigma}=R\big\langle\Delta_{\xi}:\overline{Q}_{\xi}\rightarrow\overline{P}_{\xi}\mid\xi\in\Sigma\big\rangle,

is the graded
R\text{-}\text{ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
with a universal family of graded module homomorphisms

\big\{\Delta_{\xi}:S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{R}Q_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}{}S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{R}P_{\xi}\,|\,(Q_{\xi},P_{\xi}),\xi\in\Sigma\big\}.

As
S_{\Sigma}
is a graded
R\text{-}\text{ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
, we have the graded algebra homomorphism
\theta_{1}:R\xrightarrow{}S_{\Sigma}
. Let
R_{\Sigma}
be the graded
S_{\Sigma}\text{-}\text{ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
such that

\begin{cases}R_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{2}}(1-\Delta_{\xi}\circ S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi)=0;\\
\ R_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{2}}(1-S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi\circ\Delta_{\xi})=0.\end{cases}

(5.10)

Here
\theta_{2}:S_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{}{}R_{\Sigma}
is the graded algebra homomorphism such that
R_{\Sigma}
is a graded
S_{\Sigma}\text{-}\text{ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
. From the construction, we have
R_{\Sigma}=R\langle\overline{\xi}^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
.

Since
S
is a graded localisation ring, for each
\xi:P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}{}Q_{\xi}\in\Sigma
, we have the isomorphism
S\otimes_{R}\xi:S\otimes_{R}P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}{}S\otimes_{R}Q_{\xi}
. Now by the universal property of
\Delta_{\xi}
, there exists a unique graded algebra homomorphism
\lambda:S_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{}{}S
such that
(S\otimes_{R}\xi)^{-1}=S\otimes_{\lambda}\Delta_{\xi}
. Hence
S
satisfies (
5.10
), that is,

\begin{cases}1-(S\otimes_{\lambda}\Delta_{\xi})\circ(S\otimes_{R}\xi)=S\otimes_{\lambda}(1-\Delta_{\xi}\circ(S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi))=0;\\
1-(S\otimes_{R}\xi)\circ(S\otimes_{\lambda}\Delta_{\xi})=S\otimes_{\lambda}(1-(S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi)\circ\Delta_{\xi})=0.\end{cases}

Next we prove that
S
has the universal property of
R_{\Sigma}
. For any graded
S_{\Sigma}\text{-}\text{ring}_{\mathsf{k}}

T
with
T\otimes(1-\Delta_{\xi}\circ(S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi))=0
and
T\otimes(1-(S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi)\circ\Delta_{\xi})=0,
there exists a unique graded algebra homomorphism
\eta:S\xrightarrow{}{}T
in the following diagram

\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\theta}
\textstyle{S_{\Sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{2}^{T}}
\textstyle{T}
\textstyle{S\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}

such that
\theta_{2}^{T}\circ\theta_{1}=\eta\circ\theta
. The proof is completed.
∎

We are in a position to extend
[
10
, Proposition 3.1 (1)]
, which only worked for
\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebras. The existence of a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded universal localization follows from the argument in
[
25
, Theorem 4.1]
adapted for categories with automorphisms. Here we prove that the existence of any
\Gamma
-graded universal localization follows from graded Bergman’s constructions where
\Gamma
is any abelian group.

Recall that a
\Gamma
-graded ring
R
(with unit) is called left graded hereditary if any graded submodule of a graded projective left
R
-module is (graded) projective, which is equivalent to that the graded global dimension for
R
being less than or equal to
1
(see §
2.5
).

Proposition 5.13
.

Let
\Gamma
be an abelian group,
R
a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra and
\Sigma
a family of morphisms between
\Gamma
-graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. We have the following statements.

(1)

The induced homomorphism
R\xrightarrow{}R\langle{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
,
r\mapsto\overline{r}
, is a
\Gamma
-graded universal localization of
R
with respect to
\Sigma
, denoted by
\theta_{\Sigma}
, and is unique up to isomorphism.

(2)

Moreover, the underlying homomorphism
\theta_{\Sigma}:R\xrightarrow{}R\langle{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
of ungraded algebras is a universal localization of
R
with respect to
\sigma=U(\Sigma)
which is the image of
\Sigma
under the forgetful functor
U
.

(3)

If
R
is graded hereditary, so is
R\langle{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
.

Proof.

(1) Recall from (
5.10
) that
S_{\Sigma}=R\langle\Delta_{\xi}:\overline{Q_{\xi}}\xrightarrow{}\overline{P_{\xi}}|\xi:P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}Q_{\xi}\in\Sigma\rangle
and that
R_{\Sigma}
satisfies that

R_{\Sigma}\otimes_{R}\xi:R_{\Sigma}\otimes_{R}P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{\sim}R_{\Sigma}\otimes_{R}Q_{\xi},

is an isomorphism for each
\xi:P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}Q_{\xi}\in\Sigma
.

For any
\Sigma
-inverting graded homomorphism
\theta^{\prime}:R\xrightarrow{}S^{\prime}
, there exists a unique graded
R
-algebra homomorphism
\lambda:S_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{}{}S^{\prime}
such that
(S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}\xi)^{-1}=S^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda}\Delta_{\xi}
. Hence we have

\theta^{\prime}=\lambda\circ\theta_{1}.

(5.11)

For each
\xi
in
\Sigma
, we have the isomorphism
S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}\xi:S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}P_{\xi}\xrightarrow{}{}S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}Q_{\xi}
, and

\begin{cases}1-(S^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda}\Delta_{\xi})\circ(S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}\xi)=S^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda}(1-\Delta_{\xi}\circ(S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi))=0;\\
1-(S^{\prime}\otimes_{R}\xi)\circ(S^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda}\Delta_{\xi})=S^{\prime}\otimes_{\lambda}(1-(S_{\Sigma}\otimes_{\theta_{1}}\xi)\circ\Delta_{\xi})=0.\end{cases}

There exists a unique graded
S_{\Sigma}
-algebra homomorphism
\eta:R_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{}S^{\prime}
in the following diagram.

\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\theta^{\prime}}
\textstyle{S_{\Sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\theta_{2}}
\textstyle{R_{\Sigma}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\eta}
\textstyle{S^{\prime}}

Thus we have

\eta\circ\theta_{2}=\lambda.

(5.12)

Therefore we have
\theta_{2}\circ\theta_{1}\circ\eta=\theta^{\prime}
. Suppose that there exists
\eta^{\prime}:R_{\Sigma}\xrightarrow{}S^{\prime}
such that
\theta_{2}\circ\theta_{1}\circ\eta^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}
. Combining with (
5.11
) we have that
\theta_{2}\circ\theta_{1}\circ\eta^{\prime}=\theta^{\prime}=\lambda\circ\theta_{1}
. By the uniqueness of
\lambda
we have
\lambda=\eta^{\prime}\circ\theta_{2}
. By comparing with (
5.12
) we have
\eta=\eta^{\prime}
. Then we prove that
\theta_{2}\circ\theta_{1}:R\xrightarrow{}R_{\Sigma}
is a graded universal localization of
R
with respect to
\Sigma
, which will be denoted by
\theta_{\Sigma}
. The uniqueness of
\theta_{\Sigma}
up to isomorphism follows from the universal property of Bergman algebras.

(2) Take
\Gamma
to be the trivial group. Then we have the second statement.

(3) By Bergman’s construction
R\langle{\overline{\xi}}^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
is hereditary. As it is a graded ring, it is graded hereditary.
∎

5.5.
Path algebras as universal algebras

Bergman’s proof of Theorem 5.3 in
[
7
]
indicates that any lower triangle matrix ring can be realised as the universal algebra of the form
R\langle f_{i}:\overline{P}_{i}\rightarrow\overline{Q}_{i}\rangle
, where
R
can be chosen as an appropriate
commutative
\mathsf{k}
-semisimple ring. Furthermore, inverting these maps, i.e.,
R\langle f_{i},f_{i}^{-1}:\overline{P}_{i}\cong\overline{Q}_{i}\rangle
would then produce the whole matrix ring. As the lower triangle matrix rings are path algebras of certain kind of line graphs, it is natural to ask whether all path algebras of graphs can be obtained as universal algebras. Here we show this is in fact the case, namely for any finite graph
E
, the path algebra
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\cong R\langle f_{i}:\overline{P}_{i}\rightarrow\overline{Q}_{i}\rangle
, where
R
is an commutative
\mathsf{k}
-semisimple ring and
(P_{i},Q_{i})
are finitely generated projective
\mathsf{k}
-modules determined by the shape of the graph. Although it is usually quite difficult to describe the universal ring obtained by inverting morphisms (see for example
[
26
]
), here we show that the Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
is the universal algebra, which inverts the maps
f_{i}:P_{i}\rightarrow Q_{i}
.

Although the results of this section might be known (or predictable) to the experts, we provide the details to demonstrate what it means to build a graded ring from the inset and not to assign the grading from the outset, as it has been done so far in the theory of combinatorial algebras. Namely, previously we would construct (Leavitt) path algebras and then assign a grading to them. By using our graded Bergman machinery, the algebras obtained are already equipped with a grading.

Let
E
be a finite graph. We use
E^{\operatorname{reg}}
to denote the set of vertices in
E
at which there are at least one edge starting from it.
Recall that the
path algebra
of the graph
E
is defined as

P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)=\mathsf{k}\big\langle E^{0},E^{1}\big\rangle\big/\big\langle uv=\delta_{u,v},s(e)e=er(e)=e,u,v\in E^{0},e\in E^{1}\big\rangle,

(5.13)

where
\mathsf{k}\big\langle E^{0},E^{1}\big\rangle
is the free (non-unital) algebra with vertices and edges of the graph as the generators.
Assigning zero to vertices and
1
to edges, since the defining relations in (
5.13
) are homogeneous,
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
becomes a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring (with positive support).
In Lemma
5.14
we will realise
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded universal algebra.

Consider the semisimple
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R=\prod_{E^{0}}\mathsf{k}
, the product of
|E^{0}|
-copies of the field
\mathsf{k}
as a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring which is concentrated in the zero component.
Denote
p_{v}(i)
, where
v\in E^{0}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the graded finitely generated projective
R
-module with
\mathsf{k}(i)
appears in
v
-th component of
R
and zero elsewhere. Throughout, we write
p_{v}
for
p_{v}(0)
. Recall that
p_{v}(i),i\in\mathbb{Z}
, is the shift of the projective
R
-module
p_{v}
by
i
. Note that
\bigoplus_{v\in E^{0}}p_{v}\cong R
as graded
R
-modules.

Lemma 5.14
.

Let
E
be a finite graph and
\mathsf{k}
a field. Let
R=\prod_{E^{0}}\mathsf{k}
be the
\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebra concentrated in degree zero, with graded finitely generated projective modules
p_{v}(i)
as above. Then we have a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra isomorphism

P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\cong R\,\big\langle g^{v}:\overline{p_{v}}\longrightarrow{}\overline{\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}{p_{r(e)}(1)}},\,{v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}}\big\rangle.

Proof.

We observe that for any regular vertex
v\in E^{0}
, we have the following diagram with exact rows of
R
-modules

\textstyle{\bigoplus_{w\neq v}p_{w}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{u}
\textstyle{R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{h^{v}}
\textstyle{p_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{Q(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}R(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{1-f^{v}}
\scriptstyle{f^{v}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}
\textstyle{\bigoplus\limits_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}R(1)}

where
Q=\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}(R/p_{r(e)})
with

\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}\bigoplus Q=\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}R,

that is, the second row of the above diagram splits. And
f^{v}
is an idempotent endomorphism of
\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}R(1)
such that
f^{v}|_{Q(1)}=\operatorname{id}_{Q(1)}
and
\operatorname{Ker}f^{v}=\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)
. Note that the graded module homomorphism
h^{v}
can be written as the matrix

\begin{pmatrix}{e_{1}^{v}}\\
e_{2}^{v}\\
\vdots\\
e_{n_{v}}^{v}\end{pmatrix}

such that
e_{1}^{v},e_{2}^{v},\cdots,e_{n_{v}}^{v}
are elements in
R
corresponding to the index set of all the edges starting from
v
in
E
. By Theorem
5.2
, the universal algebra
S
with universal graded module homomorphisms

g^{v}:S\otimes_{R}p_{v}\longrightarrow S\otimes_{R}\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)

for regular vertices
v\in E^{0}
is obtained via adjoining to
R
generators
e_{i}^{v}
for
v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}
and
1\leq i\leq n_{v}
subject to the relations
h^{v}\circ u=0=f^{v}\circ h^{v}
. More precisely, we have
h^{v}\circ u=0
implying

{\mathbf{e}}_{w}e^{v}_{i}=0

(5.14)

for any
w\neq v
and all
1\leq i\leq n_{v}
. Here,

\mathbf{e}_{w}=\begin{pmatrix}0,\cdots,0,1,0\cdots,0\end{pmatrix}\in p_{w}.

Hence by the universal property of the cokernel
\varepsilon
, there exists a unique graded homomorphism
\tau:p_{v}\xrightarrow{}\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}R(1)
such that
h^{v}=\tau\circ\varepsilon
. This deduce that

\mathbf{e}_{v}e_{i}^{v}=e^{v}_{i}

(5.15)

for all
1\leq i\leq n_{v}
. Also we have
f^{v}\circ h^{v}=0
implying

e^{v}_{i}\mathbf{e}_{r(e^{v}_{i})}=e^{v}_{i},\qquad e^{v}_{i}{\mathbf{e}}_{w}=0\text{~for~}w\neq r(e^{v}_{i}),

(5.16)

for each
1\leq i\leq n_{v}
. One can see that
S
is isomorphic to the path algebra
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
when one observes that
\mathbf{e}_{v}
corresponds to the vertex
v\in E^{0}
, and for each
v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}
,
e^{v}_{i}
corresponds to the edge starting from
v
for
1\leq i\leq n_{v}
.
∎

One observes that the universal graded module homomorphism

g^{v}:P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\otimes_{R}p_{v}\longrightarrow P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\otimes_{R}\big(\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)\big)

in the following diagram corresponding to
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\otimes_{R}h^{v}
is given by
g^{v}(xv)=\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}xe
for any
x\in P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
.

\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 29.90494pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\hbox{\vtop{\halign{\entry@#!@&&\entry@@#!@\cr&\cr&\crcr}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-29.90494pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\otimes_{R}p_{v}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 37.68346pt\raise 6.26584pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.90474pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g^{v}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 53.90494pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 53.90494pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\otimes_{R}(\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.61513pt\raise-16.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.75pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\cong}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 119.61513pt\raise-24.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern-20.17001pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\,v\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 41.54808pt\raise-25.73416pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.90474pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{g^{v}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 72.76956pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\kern 72.76956pt\raise-32.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\,r(e)}$}}}}}}}\ignorespaces}}}}\ignorespaces(1)

We have the following consequence (compare with
[
10
, Proposition 4.3(1)]
). We mention that in
[
10
]
the authors constructed the maps
g^{v}
which are exactly the graded universal homomorphisms of Bergman algebras.

Theorem 5.15
.

The
\mathbb{Z}
-graded algebra homomorphism
\iota:P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\xrightarrow{}L(E),\,p\mapsto p
, for
p
a path in
E
, is a graded universal localization with respect to

\Sigma=\Big\{g^{v}:P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)v\longrightarrow\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)r(e)(1)\,|\,v\in E^{\operatorname{reg}}\Big\},

where
g^{v}(x)=\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}xe
for
x\in P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)v
.

Proof.

By Proposition
5.13
(1) we have that
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\xrightarrow{}P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\langle\xi^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}
,
p\mapsto p
, is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded universal localization of
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
with respect to
\Sigma
. One observes that

\begin{split}P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\langle\xi^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}&\cong R\big\langle\overline{p_{v}}\cong\overline{\oplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)},v\in E^{\rm reg}\big\rangle\\
&\cong B_{R}(e,f)\\
&\cong L(H)\\
&\cong L(E)\end{split}

(5.17)

where
R=\prod_{E^{0}}\mathsf{k}
and
(e,f)=\{(e_{h^{v}},f_{h^{v}})\,|\,v\in E^{\rm reg}\}
with
e_{h^{v}}=(\epsilon_{v})\in R_{0}
and
f_{h^{v}}=\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{|J_{v}|\times|J_{v}|}(R)(1,1,\cdots,1)_{0}
the idempotent matrix whose entry at position
(j,j^{\prime})
is
\delta_{jj^{\prime}}\epsilon_{r(e^{v,j})}
. Here in the equation (
5.17
) the first isomorphism follows by Lemma
5.14
, the second isomorphism follows by Lemma
5.8
, the third isomorphism is given in Theorem
4.10
and the last isomorphism is given in Example
3.4
. Moreover all the four isomorphisms preserve elements. Hence the composition
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\xrightarrow{}P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\langle\xi^{-1}\rangle_{\xi\in\Sigma}\xrightarrow{}L(E)
,
p\mapsto p
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded universal localization of
P_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
with respect to
\Sigma
.
∎

6.
Smash products of graded Bergman algebras

Throughout this section,
\Gamma
is an abelian group,
\mathsf{k}
a commutative
\Gamma
-graded ring concentrated in degree
0
, and
R
is a
\Gamma
-graded
k
-algebra, i.e.,
R
is equipped with a graded homomorphism
\mathsf{k}\rightarrow Z(R)
. Note that, by our assumptions
\mathsf{k}\subseteq R_{0}
.

6.1.
Smash products of graded Bergman algebras I

Let
P
and
Q
be non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules and set

S:=R\big\langle h,h^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle.

We write
P
as the image of an idempotent graded endomorphism
d
of a graded free
R
-module of finite rank
\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
, and similarly
Q
as the image of an idempotent graded endomorphism
e
of a graded free
R
-module of finite rank
\bigoplus_{m\in M}R(\gamma_{m})
. Clearly
\deg(d_{ij})=\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}
, for any
i,j\in J
, and
\deg(e_{km})=\gamma_{m}-\gamma_{k}
, for any
k,m\in M
. Recall from Lemma
5.8
that
S
can be obtained from
R
by adjoining homogeneous generators
h_{jm},h^{\prime}_{mj}~(j\in J,m\in M)
of degree
\deg(h_{jm})=\gamma_{m}-\beta_{j}
, respectively
\deg(h^{\prime}_{mj})=\beta_{j}-\gamma_{m}
, and relations
dh=h=he,~eh^{\prime}=h^{\prime}=h^{\prime}d,~hh^{\prime}=d,~h^{\prime}h=e
. Hence
S\cong R\langle X\rangle/I
, where
X=\{h_{jm},h^{\prime}_{mj}\mid j\in J,m\in M\}
and
I
is the ideal of
R\langle X\rangle
generated by the relations

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}d_{ji}h_{im}

\displaystyle=h_{jm}=\sum_{k\in M}h_{jk}e_{km}

\displaystyle(j\in J,m\in M),

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in M}e_{mk}h^{\prime}_{kj}

\displaystyle=h^{\prime}_{mj}=\sum_{i\in J}h^{\prime}_{mi}d_{ij}

\displaystyle(j\in J,m\in M),

\displaystyle\sum_{m\in M}h_{im}h^{\prime}_{mj}

\displaystyle=d_{ij}

\displaystyle(i,j\in J),

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in J}h^{\prime}_{kj}h_{jm}

\displaystyle=e_{km}

\displaystyle(k,m\in M).

It follows from Lemma
2.8
and Corollary
2.11
that
S\#\Gamma
has the presentation

\begin{split}S\#\Gamma=\Big\langle&rp_{\gamma},h_{jm}p_{\gamma},h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma}\quad(r\in R^{h},\gamma\in\Gamma,j\in J,m\in M)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}=(r+s)p_{\gamma}\quad(r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}=rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\quad(r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&(h_{jm}p_{\gamma})(1p_{\gamma})=h_{jm}p_{\gamma}=(1p_{\gamma+\gamma_{m}-\beta_{j}})(h_{jm}p_{\gamma})\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&(h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma})(1p_{\gamma})=h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma}=(1p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\gamma_{m}})(h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma})\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma)\\
&\sum_{i\in J}d_{ji}p_{\gamma+\gamma_{m}-\beta_{i}}h_{im}p_{\gamma}=h_{jm}p_{\gamma}=\sum_{k\in M}h_{jk}p_{\gamma+\gamma_{m}-\gamma_{k}}e_{km}p_{\gamma}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma)\\
&\sum_{k\in M}e_{mk}p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\gamma_{k}}h^{\prime}_{kj}p_{\gamma}=h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma}=\sum_{i\in J}h^{\prime}_{mi}p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}}d_{ij}p_{\gamma}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma)\\
&\sum_{m\in M}h_{im}p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\gamma_{m}}h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma}=d_{ij}p_{\gamma}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma)\\
&\sum_{j\in J}h^{\prime}_{kj}p_{\gamma+\gamma_{m}-\beta_{j}}h_{jm}p_{\gamma}=e_{km}p_{\gamma}\quad(k,m\in M;~\gamma\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.1)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
,
i,j\in J
and
k,m\in M
, define

\displaystyle d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle:=d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}},

\displaystyle e^{(\gamma)}_{km}

\displaystyle:=e_{km}p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}},

\displaystyle h^{(\gamma)}_{jm}

\displaystyle:=h_{jm}p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}},

\displaystyle(h^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}

\displaystyle:=h^{\prime}_{mj}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}.

Using these conventions it follows from (
6.1
) that

\begin{split}S\#\Gamma=\Big\langle&rp_{\gamma},h^{(\gamma)}_{jm},(h^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}\quad(r\in R^{h},\gamma\in\Gamma,j\in J,m\in M)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}=(r+s)p_{\gamma}\quad(r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}=rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\quad(r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})=h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&(h^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=(h^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})(h^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{i\in J}d^{(\gamma)}_{ji}h_{im}^{(\gamma)}=h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{k\in M}h_{jk}^{(\gamma)}e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{k\in M}e_{mk}^{(\gamma)}(h^{\prime}_{kj})^{(\gamma)}=(h^{\prime}_{mj})^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{i\in J}(h^{\prime})_{mi}^{(\gamma)}d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{m\in M}h_{im}^{(\gamma)}(h^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}=d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{j\in J}(h^{\prime})_{kj}^{(\gamma)}h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\quad(k,m\in M;~\gamma\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.2)

Next, for any finite subset
A\subseteq\Gamma
we define the
k
-algebra
T_{A}
by

\begin{split}T_{A}=\Big\langle&rp_{\beta}~~(r\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A});\quad h^{(\gamma)}_{jm},(h^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}~~(\gamma\in A,j\in J,m\in M)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\beta}+sp_{\beta}=(r+s)p_{\beta}\quad(r,s\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&rp_{\beta}sp_{\beta^{\prime}}=rs_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}p_{\beta^{\prime}}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~s\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}};~\alpha+\beta,\beta,\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})=h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),\\
&(h^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=(h^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})(h^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{i\in J}d^{(\gamma)}_{ji}h_{im}^{(\gamma)}=h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{k\in M}h_{jk}^{(\gamma)}e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{k\in M}e_{mk}^{(\gamma)}(h^{\prime}_{kj})^{(\gamma)}=(h^{\prime}_{mj})^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{i\in J}(h^{\prime})_{mi}^{(\gamma)}d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{m\in M}h_{im}^{(\gamma)}(h^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}=d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{j\in J}(h^{\prime})_{kj}^{(\gamma)}h_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\quad(k,m\in M;~\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle,\end{split}

(6.3)

where
\Gamma_{A}=\{\gamma-\gamma_{m},\gamma-\beta_{j}\mid\gamma\in A,j\in J,m\in M\}
. Clearly
T_{A}
is a unital algebra with identity
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{A}}p_{\gamma}
. Moreover,

S\#\Gamma=\varinjlim_{A}T_{A},

where
A
runs through all finite subsets of
\Gamma
. We will show that the
T_{A}
’s are isomorphic to Bergman algebras.

Let
A\subseteq\Gamma
a finite subset. We denote by
R\#\Gamma_{A}
the subalgebra of
R\#\Gamma
consisting of all sums of elements
rp_{\beta}
, where
r\in R_{\alpha}
and
\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}
. Note that
R\#\Gamma_{A}
is is a unital algebra with identity
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{A}}p_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 6.1
.

The
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R\#\Gamma_{A}
has the presentation

\begin{split}R\#\Gamma_{A}=\Big\langle&rp_{\beta}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A})\mid{}\\
&rp_{\beta}+sp_{\beta}=(r+s)p_{\beta}\quad(r,s\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&rp_{\beta}sp_{\beta^{\prime}}=rs_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}p_{\beta^{\prime}}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~s\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}};~\alpha+\beta,\beta,\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{A})\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.4)

Proof.

Denote by
Z
the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra presented by (
6.4
). It follows from Lemma
2.8
that there is a
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\phi:Z\to R\#\Gamma
such that
\phi(rp_{\gamma})=rp_{\gamma}
, where
r\in R_{\alpha}
and
\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}
. Clearly one can write any element
z\in Z
in the form
z=\sum_{\beta\in\Gamma_{A}}\sum_{\alpha+\beta\in\Gamma_{A}}r_{\beta}^{(\alpha)}p_{\beta}
, where each
r^{(\alpha)}_{\beta}\in R_{\alpha}
. It follows that
\phi(Z)=R\#\Gamma_{A}
. It remains to show that
\phi
is injective. Suppose that
\phi(z)=0
, for some
z\in Z
. Write
z=\sum_{\beta\in\Gamma_{A}}\sum_{\alpha+\beta\in\Gamma_{A}}r_{\beta}^{(\alpha)}p_{\beta}
, where each
r^{(\alpha)}_{\beta}\in R_{\alpha}
. Since
\phi(z)=0
, it follows that each
r^{(\alpha)}_{\beta}=0
. Thus
\phi
is injective and therefore
Z\cong\phi(Z)=R\#\Gamma_{A}
.
∎

For any
\gamma\in A
define the matrices

\displaystyle d^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle:=(d_{ij}^{(\gamma)})_{i,j\in J}=(d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})_{i,j\in J}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{J\times J}(R\#\Gamma_{A}),

\displaystyle e^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle:=(e_{km}^{(\gamma)})_{k,m\in K}=(e_{km}p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})_{k,m\in K}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{M\times M}(R\#\Gamma_{A}).

A straightforward computation shows that
d^{(\gamma)}
and
e^{(\gamma)}
are idempotent matrices. Hence they define idempotent endomorphisms of
\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma_{A}
and
\bigoplus_{m\in M}R\#\Gamma_{A}
, respectively. It follows that
P_{A}^{(\gamma)}:=(\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma_{A})d^{(\gamma)}
and
Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}:=(\bigoplus_{m\in M}R\#\Gamma_{A})e^{(\gamma)}
are non-zero finitely generated projective
R\#\Gamma_{A}
-modules. Define the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra

B_{A}:=R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle g^{(\gamma)},(g^{(\gamma)})^{-1}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\cong\overline{Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle.

We will show that
T_{A}\cong B_{A}
. By Lemma
5.8
and (
6.4
) we have

\begin{split}B_{A}=\Big\langle&rp_{\beta}~(r\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A});~~~~g^{(\gamma)}_{jm},(g^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}~~(\gamma\in A,j\in J,m\in M)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\beta}+sp_{\beta}=(r+s)p_{\beta}\quad(r,s\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&rp_{\beta}sp_{\beta^{\prime}}=rs_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}p_{\beta^{\prime}}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~s\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}};~\alpha+\beta,\beta,\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&\sum_{i\in J}d^{(\gamma)}_{ji}g_{im}^{(\gamma)}=g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{k\in M}g_{jk}^{(\gamma)}e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{k\in M}e_{mk}^{(\gamma)}(g^{\prime}_{kj})^{(\gamma)}=(g^{\prime}_{mj})^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{i\in J}(g^{\prime})_{mi}^{(\gamma)}d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{m\in M}g_{im}^{(\gamma)}(g^{\prime})^{(\gamma)}_{mj}=d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{j\in J}(g^{\prime})_{kj}^{(\gamma)}g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\quad(k,m\in M;~\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle,\end{split}

(6.5)

Lemma 6.2
.

In
B_{A}
the relations

\displaystyle g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})=g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),

(6.6)

\displaystyle(g^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=(g^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})(g^{\prime})_{mj}^{(\gamma)}\quad(j\in J,m\in M,\gamma\in A),

(6.7)

hold.

Proof.

We only prove relation (
6.6
) and leave relation (
6.7
) to the reader.
Clearly

\displaystyle g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{k\in M}g_{jk}^{(\gamma)}e_{km}^{(\gamma)}\Big)(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in M}g_{jk}^{(\gamma)}(e_{km}p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})(1p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in M}g_{jk}^{(\gamma)}(e_{km}p_{\gamma-\gamma_{m}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in M}g_{jk}^{(\gamma)}e_{km}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}.

Similarly

\displaystyle(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}=

\displaystyle(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})\sum_{i\in J}d_{ji}^{(\gamma)}g^{(\gamma)}_{im}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})\sum_{i\in J}(d_{ji}p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})g^{(\gamma)}_{im}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})(d_{ji}p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})g^{(\gamma)}_{im}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}((d_{ji})_{\gamma-\beta_{j}-(\gamma-\beta_{i})}p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})g^{(\gamma)}_{im}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}(d_{ji}p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})g^{(\gamma)}_{im}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}d_{ji}^{(\gamma)}g^{(\gamma)}_{im}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle g_{jm}^{(\gamma)}

since
\deg(d_{ji})=\beta_{i}-\beta_{j}
.
∎

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
A\subseteq\Gamma
be a finite subset. Then
T_{A}\cong B_{A}
.

Proof.

The proposition follows from (
6.3
), (
6.5
), (
6.6
) and (
6.7
).
∎

The theorem below is the main result of this subsection. It follows directly from Proposition
6.3
and the fact that
S\#\Gamma=\varinjlim_{A}T_{A}
, where
A
runs through all finite subsets of
\Gamma
.

Theorem 6.4
.

The
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R\big\langle h,h^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle\#\Gamma
is the direct limit of the Bergman algebras

R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle g^{(\gamma)},(g^{(\gamma)})^{-1}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\cong\overline{Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle,

where
A
runs through all finite subsets of
\Gamma
.

We finish this subsection by proving a lemma which will be used in §7. For
\gamma\in\Gamma
we define the
R\#\Gamma
-modules
P^{(\gamma)}:=(\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma)d^{(\gamma)}
and
Q^{(\gamma)}:=(\bigoplus_{m\in M}R\#\Gamma)e^{(\gamma)}
.

Lemma 6.5
.

Let
\gamma\in\Gamma
. Then
P^{(\gamma)}\cong P(-\gamma)\#\Gamma
and
Q^{(\gamma)}\cong Q(-\gamma)\#\Gamma
as
R\#\Gamma
-modules.

Proof.

We only prove that
P(-\gamma)\#\Gamma\cong P^{(\gamma)}
. The proof that
Q(-\gamma)\#\Gamma\cong Q^{(\gamma)}
is similar. Recall that

P(-\gamma)\#\Gamma=P(-\gamma)=P=\operatorname{Im}(d)

as a set. Hence any element of
P(-\gamma)\#\Gamma
can be written as
xd
where
x\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
. We define the map

\displaystyle\theta:P(-\gamma)\#\Gamma

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle xd

\displaystyle\longmapsto x^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)},

where
x^{(\gamma)}\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma
is defined by
x^{(\gamma)}_{j}=x_{j}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}~(j\in J)
. Clearly

\displaystyle xd=yd

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle(x-y)d=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i}-y_{i})d_{ij}=0~\forall j\in J~(\text{in }R)

\displaystyle\ \Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle(\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i}-y_{i})d_{ij})p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}=0~\forall j\in J~(\text{in }R\#\Gamma)

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i}-y_{i})d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}=0~\forall j\in J

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i}-y_{i})p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}}d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}=0~\forall j\in J

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle\sum_{i\in J}(x^{(\gamma)}_{i}-y^{(\gamma)}_{i})d^{(\gamma)}_{ij}=0~\forall j\in J

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle(x^{(\gamma)}-y^{(\gamma)})d^{(\gamma)}=0

\displaystyle\Leftrightarrow~~

\displaystyle x^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}=y^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}

for any
x,y\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
. Hence
\theta
is well-defined and injective.

Next we show that
\theta
is surjective. Let
z\in P^{(\gamma)}=\operatorname{Im}(d^{(\gamma)})
. Then there is a
y\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma
such that
z=yd^{(\gamma)}
. For any
i\in J
write
y_{i}=\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}y_{i,\alpha}p_{\alpha}\in R\#\Gamma
. Then

(yd^{(\gamma)})_{j}=\sum_{i\in J}y_{i}d^{(\gamma)}_{ij}=\sum_{i\in J}(\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}y_{i,\alpha}p_{\alpha})d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}=\sum_{i\in J}\sum_{\alpha\in\Gamma}y_{i,\alpha}(d_{ij})_{\alpha-\gamma+\beta_{j}}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}=\sum_{i\in J}y_{i,\gamma-\beta_{i}}d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}},

since
\deg(d_{ij})=\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}
. Hence we may assume that
y_{i,\alpha}=0
, for any
i\in J
and
\alpha\neq\gamma-\beta_{i}
. Define
x\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
by
x_{j}=y_{j,\gamma-\beta_{j}}~(j\in J)
. Then clearly
y=x^{(\gamma)}
and hence
z=yd^{(\gamma)}=x^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}=\theta(xd)
. Thus
\theta
is surjective.

It remains to show that
\theta
is a module homomorphism. Clearly

\theta(xd+yd)=\theta((x+y)d)=(x+y)^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}=x^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}+y^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}=\theta(xd)+\theta(yd),

(6.8)

for any
x,y\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
. Let now
x\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
,
r\in R
and
\alpha\in\Gamma
. Then

\theta(rp_{\alpha}.xd)=\theta(r(xd)_{\alpha}).

(6.9)

For any
j\in J
we have

((xd)_{\alpha})_{j}=((xd)_{j})_{\alpha}=\Big(\sum_{i\in J}x_{i}d_{ij}\Big)_{\alpha}=\sum_{i\in J}(x_{i})_{\alpha-\gamma+\beta_{i}}d_{ij}

(6.10)

since
\deg((x_{i})_{\delta}d_{ij})=\delta-\beta(i)+\gamma
in
R(\beta(j)-\gamma)
. Define
y\in\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
by
y_{j}=r(x_{j})_{\alpha-\gamma+\beta_{j}}~(j\in J)
. It follows from (
6.9
) and (
6.10
) that

\theta(rp_{\alpha}.xd)=\theta(yd)=y^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}.

(6.11)
