On the other hand we have

rp_{\alpha}.\theta(xd)=(rp_{\alpha}).x^{(\gamma)}d^{(\gamma)}.

(6.12)

Clearly

((rp_{\alpha}).x^{(\gamma)})_{j}=(rp_{\alpha}).(x^{(\gamma)})_{j}=(rp_{\alpha})(x_{j}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=r(x_{j})_{\alpha-\gamma+\beta_{j}}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}=y^{(\gamma)}_{j},

(6.13)

for any
j\in J
. Hence

(rp_{\alpha}).x^{(\gamma)}=y^{(\gamma)}.

(6.14)

It follows from (
6.11
), (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) that

\theta(rp_{\alpha}.xd)=rp_{\alpha}.\theta(xd).

(6.15)

In view of (
6.8
) and (
6.15
) we have shown that
\theta
is a module homomorphism.
∎

6.2.
Smash products of graded Bergman algebras II

Let
P
be a non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-module and set

S:=R\big\langle e:\overline{P}\to\overline{P};~e^{2}=e\big\rangle.

We write
P
as the image of an idempotent graded endomorphism
d
of a free graded
R
-module of finite rank
\bigoplus_{j\in J}R(\beta_{j})
. Clearly
\deg(d_{ij})=\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}
for any
i,j\in J
. Recall from Lemma
5.9
that
S
can be obtained from
R
by adjoining homogeneous generators
e_{ij}~(i,j\in J)
of degree
\deg(e_{ij})=\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}
and relations
de=e=ed
and
ee=e
. Hence
S\cong R\langle X\rangle/I
where
X=\{e_{ij}\mid i,j\in J\}
and
I
is the ideal of
R\langle X\rangle
generated by the relations

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}e_{kj}

\displaystyle=e_{ij}=\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}d_{kj}

\displaystyle(i,j\in J),

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}e_{kj}

\displaystyle=e_{ij}

\displaystyle(i,j\in J).

It follows from Lemma
2.8
and Corollary
2.11
that
S\#\Gamma
has the presentation

\begin{split}S\#\Gamma=\Big\langle&rp_{\gamma},e_{ij}p_{\gamma}\quad(r\in R^{h};~\gamma\in\Gamma;~i,j\in J)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}=(r+s)p_{\gamma}\quad(r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}=rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\quad(r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&(e_{ij}p_{\gamma})(1p_{\gamma})=e_{ij}p_{\gamma}=(1p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\beta_{i}})(e_{ij}p_{\gamma})\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\beta_{k}}e_{kj}p_{\gamma}=e_{ij}p_{\gamma}=\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\beta_{k}}d_{kj}p_{\gamma}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}p_{\gamma+\beta_{j}-\beta_{k}}e_{kj}p_{\gamma}=e_{ij}p_{\gamma}(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.16)

For any
\gamma\in\Gamma
and
i,j\in J
define
d_{ij}^{(\gamma)}:=d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}
and
e^{(\gamma)}_{ij}:=e_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}}
. Using these conventions it follows from (
6.16
) that

\begin{split}S\#\Gamma=\Big\langle&rp_{\gamma},e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(r\in R^{h};~\gamma\in\Gamma;~i,j\in J)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\gamma}+sp_{\gamma}=(r+s)p_{\gamma}\quad(r,s\in R_{\delta};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&rp_{\gamma}sp_{\delta}=rs_{\gamma-\delta}p_{\delta}\quad(r,s\in R^{h};~\gamma,\delta\in\Gamma),\\
&e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}^{(\gamma)}e_{kj}^{(\gamma)}=e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}^{(\gamma)}d_{kj}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}^{(\gamma)}e_{kj}^{(\gamma)}=e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.17)

For any finite subset
A\subseteq\Gamma
we define the
k
-algebra
T_{A}
by

\begin{split}T_{A}=\Big\langle&rp_{\beta}~(r\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A});~~~~e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}~(\gamma\in A;~i,j\in J)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\beta}+sp_{\beta}=(r+s)p_{\beta}\quad(r,s\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&rp_{\beta}sp_{\beta^{\prime}}=rs_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}p_{\beta^{\prime}}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~s\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}};~\alpha+\beta,\beta,\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}^{(\gamma)}e_{kj}^{(\gamma)}=e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}^{(\gamma)}d_{kj}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}e_{ik}^{(\gamma)}e_{kj}^{(\gamma)}=e_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle,\end{split}

(6.18)

where
\Gamma_{A}=\{\gamma-\beta_{j}\mid\gamma\in A,j\in J\}
. Clearly
T_{A}
is a unital algebra with identity
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{A}}p_{\gamma}
. Moreover,
S\#\Gamma=\varinjlim_{A}T_{A}
, where
A
runs through all finite subsets of
\Gamma
. We will show that the
T_{A}
’s are isomorphic to Bergman algebras.

Let
A\subseteq\Gamma
a finite subset. We denote by
R\#\Gamma_{A}
the subalgebra of
R\#\Gamma
consisting of all sums of elements
rp_{\beta}
, where
r\in R_{\alpha}
and
\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}
. Note that
R\#\Gamma_{A}
is is a unital algebra with identity
\sum_{\gamma\in\Gamma_{A}}p_{\gamma}
.

Lemma 6.6
.

The algebra
R\#\Gamma_{A}
has the presentation

\begin{split}R\#\Gamma_{A}=\Big\langle&rp_{\beta}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A})\mid{}\\
&rp_{\beta}+sp_{\beta}=(r+s)p_{\beta}\quad(r,s\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&rp_{\beta}sp_{\beta^{\prime}}=rs_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}p_{\beta^{\prime}}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~s\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}};~\alpha+\beta,\beta,\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{A})\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.19)

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
6.1
.
∎

For any
\gamma\in A
define the matrices

\displaystyle d^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle:=(d_{ij}^{(\gamma)})_{i,j\in J}=(d_{ij}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})_{i,j\in J}\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{J\times J}(R\#\Gamma_{A}).

A straightforward computation shows that the matrices
d^{(\gamma)}
are idempotent. Hence they define idempotent endomorphisms of
\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma_{A}
. It follows that
P_{A}^{(\gamma)}:=(\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma_{A})d^{(\gamma)}
are non-zero finitely generated projective
R\#\Gamma_{A}
-modules. Define the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra

B_{A}:=R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle f^{(\gamma)}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\to\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}};~(f^{(\gamma)})^{2}=f^{(\gamma)}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle.

We will show that
T_{A}\cong B_{A}
. By Lemma
5.8
and (
6.19
) we have

\begin{split}B_{A}=\Big\langle&rp_{\beta}~(r\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A});~~~~f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}~(\gamma\in A;~i,j\in J)\mid{}\\
&rp_{\beta}+sp_{\beta}=(r+s)p_{\beta}\quad(r,s\in R_{\alpha};~\alpha+\beta,\beta\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&rp_{\beta}sp_{\beta^{\prime}}=rs_{\beta-\beta^{\prime}}p_{\beta^{\prime}}\quad(r\in R_{\alpha};~s\in R_{\alpha^{\prime}};~\alpha+\beta,\beta,\alpha^{\prime}+\beta^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}\in\Gamma_{A}),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}^{(\gamma)}f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}=f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=\sum_{k\in J}f_{ik}^{(\gamma)}d_{kj}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A),\\
&\sum_{k\in J}f_{ik}^{(\gamma)}f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}=f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(6.20)

Lemma 6.7
.

In
B_{A}
the relations

\displaystyle f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}\quad(i,j\in J;~\gamma\in A)

(6.21)

hold.

Proof.

Clearly

\displaystyle f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})=

\displaystyle\Big(\sum_{k\in J}f_{ik}^{(\gamma)}d_{kj}^{(\gamma)}\Big)(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}f_{ik}^{(\gamma)}(d_{kj}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}f_{ik}^{(\gamma)}(d_{kj}p_{\gamma-\beta_{j}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}f_{ik}^{(\gamma)}d_{kj}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}.

Similarly

\displaystyle(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}=

\displaystyle(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})\Big(\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}^{(\gamma)}f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}(1p_{\gamma-\beta_{i}})(d_{ik}p_{\gamma-\beta_{k}})f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}((d_{ik})_{\gamma-\beta_{i}-(\gamma-\beta_{k})}p_{\gamma-\beta_{k}})f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}(d_{ik}p_{\gamma-\beta_{k}})f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{k\in J}d_{ik}^{(\gamma)}f_{kj}^{(\gamma)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{ij}^{(\gamma)}

since
\deg(d_{ik})=\beta_{k}-\beta_{i}
.
∎

Proposition 6.8
.

Let
A\subseteq\Gamma
be a finite subset. Then
T_{A}\cong B_{A}
.

Proof.

The proposition follows from (
6.18
), (
6.20
) and (
6.21
).
∎

The theorem below is the main result of this subsection. It follows directly from Proposition
6.8
and the fact that
S\#\Gamma=\varinjlim_{A}T_{A}
, where
A
runs through all finite subsets of
\Gamma
.

Theorem 6.9
.

The
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R\big\langle e:\overline{P}\to\overline{P};~e^{2}=e\big\rangle\#\Gamma
is the direct limit of the Bergman algebras

R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle f^{(\gamma)}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\to\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}};~(f^{(\gamma)})^{2}=f^{(\gamma)}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle,

where
A
runs through all finite subsets of
\Gamma
.

We finish this subsection with a lemma which will be used in §7. For
\gamma\in\Gamma
we define the
R\#\Gamma
-module
P^{(\gamma)}:=(\bigoplus_{j\in J}R\#\Gamma)d^{(\gamma)}
.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
\gamma\in A
. Then
P^{(\gamma)}\cong P(-\gamma)\#\Gamma
as
R\#\Gamma
-modules.

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
6.5
.
∎

7.
The graded
\mathcal{V}
-monoid of a graded Bergman algebra

7.1.
Basic definitions and results

Let
R
be a not necessarily unital ring. Recall that a
R
-module
M
is called
unital
if
RM=M
. We denote by
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
the category of unital
R
-modules. Furthermore, we denote by
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
the full subcategory of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
whose objects are the projective objects of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}
that are finitely generated as a
R
-module. If
R
has local units, we define

\mathcal{V}(R)=\{[P]\mid P\in R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}\}

where
[P]
denotes the isomorphism class of
P
as a
R
-module.
\mathcal{V}(R)
becomes an abelian monoid by defining
[P]+[Q]=[P\oplus Q]
. It is well-known that
\mathcal{V}
is a functor that commutes with direct limits. There is a different, but equivalent definition of
\mathcal{V}
using idempotent matrices over
R
, cf.
[
3
, Subsection 4A]
.

Let
R
now be a
\Gamma
-graded ring. Recall that a
R
-module
M
is called
\Gamma
-graded
if there is a decomposition
M=\bigoplus_{\gamma\in\Gamma}M_{\gamma}
such that
R_{\alpha}M_{\gamma}\subseteq M_{\alpha\gamma}
for any
\alpha,\gamma\in\Gamma
. We denote by
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
the category of
\Gamma
-graded unital
R
-modules with morphisms the
R
-module homomorphisms that preserve grading. Moreover, we denote by
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
the full subcategory of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
whose objects are the projective objects of
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}
that are finitely generated as a
R
-module. If
R
has graded local units, we define

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)=\{[P]\mid P\in R\text{-}\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}\}

where
[P]
denotes the isomorphism class of
P
as a graded
R
-module.
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
becomes a
\Gamma
-monoid by defining
[P]+[Q]=[P\oplus Q]
and
\gamma.[P]=[P(\gamma)]
.

Proposition 7.1

(
[
24
, Proposition 66]
)
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded ring with graded local units. Then
R
-
\operatorname{{\bf Gr}}_{\operatorname{proj}}\cong R\#\Gamma
-
\operatorname{{\bf Mod}}_{\operatorname{proj}}
by an isomorphism that commutes with direct sums. It follows that
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\cong\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma)
.

Let
R
be a unital ring and
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
an idempotent matrix. Then
\epsilon
defines an endomorphism of the
R
-module
R^{n}
, which we also denote by
\epsilon
. Clearly the image
M(\epsilon)=\operatorname{Im}\epsilon
is a finitely generated projective
R
-module (since
R^{n}=\operatorname{Ker}\epsilon\oplus\operatorname{Im}\epsilon
). Now let
M
be a finitely generated projective
R
-module. Then
M\oplus N=R^{n}
for some
R
-module
N
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
. Define an endomorphism
\epsilon
of
R^{n}
by
\epsilon|_{M}=\operatorname{id}_{M}
and
\epsilon|_{N}=0
. Then clearly
M=\operatorname{Im}\epsilon
and
\epsilon
is idempotent. We denote the idempotent matrix in
\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
corresponding to
\epsilon
also by
\epsilon
. Clearly
M(\epsilon)=M
. Hence any finitely generated projective
R
-module equals
M(\epsilon)
for some idempotent square matrix
\epsilon
over
R
.

Now let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded ring and
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
an idempotent matrix with homogeneous entries such
\deg(\epsilon_{ij})=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. Then
\epsilon
defines an endomorphism of the graded
R
-module
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}R(\gamma_{k})
, which we also denote by
\epsilon
. Clearly the image
M(\epsilon)=\operatorname{Im}\epsilon
is a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module (since
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}R(\gamma_{k})=\operatorname{Ker}\epsilon\oplus\operatorname{Im}\epsilon
). Now let
M
be a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. Then
M\oplus N=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}R(\gamma_{k})
for some graded
R
-module
N
and
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. Define an endomorphism
\epsilon
of
\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}R(\gamma_{k})
by
\epsilon|_{M}=\operatorname{id}_{M}
and
\epsilon|_{N}=0
. Then clearly
M=\operatorname{Im}\epsilon
and
\epsilon
is idempotent. We denote the idempotent matrix in
\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
corresponding to
\epsilon
also by
\epsilon
. Clearly
\deg(\epsilon_{ij})=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
and
M(\epsilon)=M
. Hence any graded finitely generated projective
R
-module equals
M(\epsilon)
for some idempotent square matrix
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
such that
\deg(\epsilon_{ij})=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
.

Lemma 7.2
.

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded ring and
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
an idempotent matrix with homogeneous entries such
\deg(\epsilon_{ij})=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. Then
M(\epsilon)\#\Gamma\cong M(\epsilon\#\Gamma)
, where
\epsilon\#\Gamma\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
is defined by
(\epsilon\#\Gamma)_{ij}=\epsilon_{ij}p_{-\gamma_{j}}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
.

Proof.

See the proof of Lemma
6.5
.
∎

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
\phi:R\to S
be a ring homomorphism and
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
an idempotent matrix. Then
S\otimes_{R}M(\epsilon)\cong M(\phi(\epsilon))
, where
\phi(\epsilon)\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(S)
is obtained from
\epsilon
by applying
\phi
to each entry.

Proof.

Recall that as an abelian monoid,
S\otimes_{R}M(\epsilon)
has the presentation

\begin{split}\Big\langle s\otimes m~(s\in S,m\in M(\epsilon))\mid{}&(s_{1}+s_{2})\otimes m=s_{1}\otimes m+s_{2}\otimes m~(s_{1},s_{2}\in S;~m\in M(\epsilon)),\\
&s\otimes(m_{1}+m_{2})=s\otimes m_{1}+s\otimes m_{2}~(s\in S;~m_{1},m_{2}\in M(\epsilon)),\\
&s.r\otimes m=s\otimes r.m~(s\in S;~r\in R;~m\in M(\epsilon))\Big\rangle.\end{split}

(7.1)

Clearly for any
k,l\in\mathbb{N}
,
\phi
induces a map
\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{k\times l}(R)\to\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{k\times l}(S)
, which we also denote by
\phi
. We define a monoid homomorphism
\theta
from the free abelian monoid
\big\langle s\otimes m~(s\in S,m\in M(\epsilon))\big\rangle
to the abelian monoid
M(\phi(\epsilon))
by

\theta(s\otimes m)=s\phi(m)

for any
s\in S
and
m\in M(\epsilon)
. Note that if
m\in M(\epsilon)
, then
m=x\epsilon
for some
x\in R^{n}
and hence
\phi(m)=\phi(x)\phi(\epsilon)\in M(\phi(\epsilon))
. One checks routinely that
\theta
preserves the defining relations in presentation (
7.1
). Hence
\theta
induces a monoid homomorphism
\eta:S\otimes_{R}M(\epsilon)\to M(\phi(\epsilon))
.

First we show that
\eta
surjective. For any
1\leq j\leq n
, let
\alpha_{j}
be the element of
R^{n}
whose
j
-th component is
1
and whose other components are
0
. Define
\beta_{1},\dots,\beta_{n}\in S^{n}
similarly. Let now
m\in M(\phi(\epsilon))
. Then there is a
y\in S^{n}
such that
m=y\phi(\epsilon)
. It follows that
m=y\phi(\epsilon)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}y_{k}\beta_{k}\phi(\epsilon)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\eta(y_{k}\otimes\alpha_{k}\epsilon)
. Hence
\eta
is surjective.

Next we show that
\eta
is injective. Suppose that
\eta(s\otimes m)=\eta(s^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime})
. Then
s\phi(m)=s^{\prime}\phi(m^{\prime})
in
S^{n}
, i.e.
s\phi(m_{k})=s^{\prime}\phi(m^{\prime}_{k})~(1\leq k\leq n)
in
S
. It follows that

s\otimes m=s\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{n}m_{k}\alpha_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}s\otimes m_{k}\alpha_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}s\phi(m_{k})\otimes\alpha_{k}=\\
=\sum_{k=1}^{n}s^{\prime}\phi(m^{\prime}_{k})\otimes\alpha_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}s^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime}_{k}\alpha_{k}=s^{\prime}\otimes\sum_{k=1}^{n}m^{\prime}_{k}\alpha_{k}s^{\prime}\otimes m^{\prime}.

Hence
\eta
is injective. We leave it to the reader to show that
\eta
preserves the action of
S
.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

Let
\phi:R\to S
be a homomorphism of
\Gamma
-graded rings and
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
an idempotent matrix with homogeneous entries such
\deg(\epsilon_{ij})=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. Then
S\otimes_{R}M(\epsilon)\cong M(\phi(\epsilon))
as graded
S
-modules, where
\phi(\epsilon)\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(S)
is obtained from
\epsilon
by applying
\phi
to each entry.

Proof.

The proof of the previous lemma shows that there is an isomorphism
\eta:S\otimes_{R}M(\epsilon)\to M(\phi(\epsilon))
of
S
-modules. We leave it to the reader to check that
\eta
preserves the grading.
∎

7.2.
The graded
\mathcal{V}
-monoid of a graded Bergman algebra I

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
k
-algebra where
\Gamma
is an abelian group and
\mathsf{k}
is a graded field concentrated in degree zero. Moreover, let
P
and
Q
be non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. Set

S:=R\big\langle h,h^{-1}:\overline{P}\cong\overline{Q}\big\rangle.

Theorem 7.5
.

There is a monoid isomorphism

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big\langle[P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)],\gamma\in\Gamma\big\rangle,

given by tensoring.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
7.1
, Theorem
6.4
,
[
7
, Theorem 5.2]
and Lemma
6.5
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(S\#\Gamma)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\varinjlim R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle g^{(\gamma)},(g^{(\gamma)})^{-1}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\cong\overline{Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\varinjlim\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle g^{(\gamma)},(g^{(\gamma)})^{-1}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\cong\overline{Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\varinjlim\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma_{A})/\big([P_{A}^{(\gamma)}]=[Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}]~~(\gamma\in A)\big)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma)/\big([P^{(\gamma)}]=[Q^{(\gamma)}]~~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma)/\big([P(\gamma)\#\Gamma]=[Q(\gamma)\#\Gamma]~~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big).

It remains to show that the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)
obtained above is given by tensoring. Let
M
be a graded finitely generated projective
R
-module. By §
7.1
,
M=M(\epsilon)
for some idempotent matrix
\epsilon\in\operatorname{\mathbb{M}}_{n\times n}(R)
such that
\deg(\epsilon_{ij})=\gamma_{j}-\gamma_{i}~(1\leq i,j\leq n)
where
\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\in\Gamma
. Applying the isomorphisms above in reversed order to
[M]\in\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)
yields (in view of Lemmas
7.2
and
7.3
)

[M(\epsilon)]\mapsto[M(\epsilon\#\Gamma)]\mapsto[M(\epsilon\#\Gamma_{A})]\mapsto[M(\phi_{A}(\epsilon\#\Gamma_{A}))]\mapsto[M(\psi(\epsilon\#\Gamma))]=[M(\xi(\epsilon)\#\Gamma)]\mapsto[M(\xi(\epsilon))],

where
A
is chosen such that all entries of
\epsilon\#\Gamma
lie in
R\#\Gamma_{A}
,
\epsilon\#\Gamma_{A}
is just
\epsilon\#\Gamma
viewed as matrix over
R\#\Gamma_{A}
, and

\displaystyle\phi_{A}:{}

\displaystyle{}R\#\Gamma_{A}\to R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle g^{(\gamma)},(g^{(\gamma)})^{-1}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\cong\overline{Q_{A}^{(\gamma)}}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle,

\displaystyle\psi:{}

\displaystyle{}R\#\Gamma\to S\#\Gamma,

\displaystyle\xi:{}

\displaystyle{}R\to S

are the canonical homomorphisms. It follows from Lemma
7.4
that the isomorphism

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S),

obtained above maps
[M(\epsilon)]
to
[M(\xi(\epsilon))]=[S\otimes_{R}M(\epsilon)]
.
∎

Corollary 7.6
.

There is a
\Gamma
-monoid isomorphism
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big\langle[P]=[Q]\big\rangle
given by tensoring.

Proof.

By Theorem
7.5
there is a monoid isomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)
given by tensoring. Clearly the monoid congruence on
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
generated by the relations
[P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)
is a
\Gamma
-monoid congruence. It follows that
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)
is a
\Gamma
-monoid since
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
is a
\Gamma
-monoid. Since tensoring preserves the action of
\Gamma
,
\phi:\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)
is an isomorphism of
\Gamma
-monoids. Clearly the relation
[P]=[Q]
generates the same
\Gamma
-monoid congruence on
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
as the relations
[P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)
. Hence
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P(\gamma)]=[Q(\gamma)]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big)=\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)/\big([P]=[Q]\big)
.
∎

Example 7.7
.

Consider the Leavitt algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
. As already observed by Bergman
[
7
]
,

L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)\cong\mathsf{k}\langle h,h^{-1}:\overline{\mathsf{k}^{n}}\cong\overline{\mathsf{k}^{n+k}}\rangle.

Then by
[
7
, Theorem 5.2]

\mathcal{V}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k))\cong\mathcal{V}(\mathsf{k})/\langle[\mathsf{k}^{n}]=[\mathsf{k}^{n+k}]\rangle\cong M_{n,n+k}.

Here, for
n,k\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
M_{n,n+k}
is the finite commutative monoid

M_{n,n+k}:=\big\{0,x,2x,\dots,nx,\dots,(n+k-1)x\big\},\ \ \ \ \mbox{with relation}\ (n+k)x=nx.

Returning to the graded setting, we have

L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\mathsf{k}\langle h,h^{-1}:\overline{\mathsf{k}^{n}}\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}\overline{\mathsf{k}^{n+k}(-1)}\rangle,

and by Corollary
7.6

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k))\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\mathsf{k})/\langle[\mathsf{k}^{n}]=[\mathsf{k}^{n+k}(-1)]\rangle\cong\big\langle 1,(n/n+k)^{i},i\in\mathbb{Z}\big\rangle\subseteq\mathbb{Q}.

Since
[L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k))]
represents
1
in this monoid, which is then a strongly order unit, by Proposition
2.6
, we conclude that
L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)
is a strongly graded ring. (This can also be obtained from looking at the generators and relations of the algebra.) Therefore, an application of Dade’s theorem, imply that

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k))\cong\mathcal{V}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)_{0})
, which in turn implies that the zero component ring
L_{\mathsf{k}}(n,n+k)_{0}
has IBN.

7.3.
The graded
\mathcal{V}
-monoid of a graded Bergman algebra II

Let
R
be a
\Gamma
-graded
k
-algebra where
\Gamma
is an abelian group and
k
a field concentrated in degree zero. Moreover, let
P
be a non-zero graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules. Set

S:=R\big\langle e:\overline{P}\to\overline{P};~e^{2}=e\big\rangle.

Theorem 7.8
.

There is a monoid isomorphism

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\Big\langle[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}],[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in A)\mid[P(\gamma)]=[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}]+[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\Big\rangle,

given by tensoring.

Proof.

It follows from Proposition
7.1
, Theorem
6.9
,
[
7
, Theorem 5.1]
and
6.10
that

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(S\#\Gamma)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(\varinjlim R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle f^{(\gamma)}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\to\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}};~(f^{(\gamma)})^{2}=f^{(\gamma)}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\varinjlim\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma_{A}\Big\langle f^{(\gamma)}:\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}}\to\overline{P_{A}^{(\gamma)}};~(f^{(\gamma)})^{2}=f^{(\gamma)}~(\gamma\in A)\Big\rangle)

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\varinjlim\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma_{A})\big\langle[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}],[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in A)\mid[P_{A}^{(\gamma)}]=[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}]+[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in A)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma)\big\langle[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}],[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\mid[P^{(\gamma)}]=[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}]+[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}(R\#\Gamma)\big\langle[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}],[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\mid[P(\gamma)\#\Gamma]=[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}]+[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big\rangle

\displaystyle\cong~

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\big\langle[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}],[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\mid[P(\gamma)]=[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}]+[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big\rangle.

It remains to show that the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\big\langle[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}],[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in A)\mid[P(\gamma)]=[P_{1}^{(\gamma)}]+[P_{2}^{(\gamma)}]~(\gamma\in\Gamma)\big\rangle\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)
is given by tensoring. But that follows from Lemmas
7.2
,
7.3
and
7.4
, see the proof of Theorem
7.5
.
∎

Corollary 7.9
.

There is a
\Gamma
-monoid isomorphism
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\big\langle[P_{1}],[P_{2}]\mid[P]=[P_{1}]+[P_{2}]\big\rangle
given by tensoring.

Proof.

See the proof of Corollary
7.6
.
∎

8.
Realisation of
\Gamma
-monoids as non-stable
K
-theory of graded rings

Throughout this section,
\Gamma
is an abelian group,
\mathsf{k}
is a
\Gamma
-graded field concentrated in degree zero, and
R
is a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Recall from §
2.1
that if
(M,i)
is a pointed
\Gamma
-monoid with
i\in M
an
\Gamma
-order unit, then
i
is a strong order unit if for any
m\in M
, we have
m\leq ki
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Furthermore,
i
is an invariant order unit if
{}^{\gamma}i=i
for any
\gamma\in\Gamma
.

Theorem 8.1
.

Let
(M,i)
be a pointed conical
\Gamma
-monoid with a distinguished order unit
i
. Then there is a hereditary graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
such that
\phi:(M,i)\cong(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R),[R])
as pointed
\Gamma
-monoids. Furthermore,
R
has a weak universal property: if
S
is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra with
\Gamma
-monoid homomorphism
\psi:(M,i)\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S),[S])
, then there is a (nonunique) graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow S
such that the following diagram commutes.

\textstyle{(M,i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R),[R])\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\otimes_{R}S}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S),[S])}

Furthermore,

(1)

R
is a strongly graded ring if and only if

i
is a strong order unit.

(2)

R
is a crossed-product ring if and only if
i
is an invariant order unit.

Proof.

Let
X=\{i,p_{\phi},q_{\phi}\mid\phi\in\Phi\}
be a set of non-zero generators for the
\Gamma
-monoid
M
such that for each
\phi\in\Phi
there are
\gamma_{\phi,1},\dots,\gamma_{\phi,n_{\phi}}\in\Gamma
such that

p_{\phi}+q_{\phi}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{\phi}}{}^{\gamma_{\phi,k}}i.

(8.1)

Let
Y=\{u_{\psi}=v_{\psi}\mid\psi\in\Psi\}
be a set of relations, including the relations (
8.1
), such that
M=\langle X\mid Y\rangle
. Since
M
is conical, we may assume that
u_{\psi},v_{\psi}\neq 0
for any
\psi\in\Psi
. Let
Z
be the set of all pairs
(A,B)
, where
A
is a finite subset of
\Phi
and
B
is a finite subset of
\Psi
such that

(I)

in the relations
u_{\psi}=v_{\psi}~(\psi\in B)
only the generators
i,p_{\phi},q_{\phi}~(\phi\in A)
appear,

(II)

the relations
u_{\psi}=v_{\psi}~(\psi\in B)
include the relations
p_{\phi}+q_{\phi}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{\phi}}{}^{\gamma_{\phi,k}}i~(\phi\in A)
.

Define a partial order
\leq
on
Z
by
(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
if
A\subseteq A^{\prime}
and
B\subseteq B^{\prime}
. Clearly
(Z,\leq)
is a directed set. For any
(A,B)\in Z
let
M_{(A,B)}=\langle X_{(A,B)}\mid Y_{(A,B)}\rangle
, where
X_{(A,B)}=\{i,p_{\phi},q_{\phi}\mid\phi\in A\}
and
Y_{(A,B)}=\{u_{\psi}=v_{\psi}\mid\psi\in B\}
. If
(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, then clearly there is a canonical
\Gamma
-monoid homomorphism
\alpha_{(A,B)}^{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}:M_{(A,B)}\to M_{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}
. One checks easily that
M
is the direct limit of the direct system
\{M_{(A,B)},\alpha_{(A,B)}^{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}\mid(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in Z\}
in the category of
\Gamma
-monoids.

Now fix an
(A,B)\in Z
. Let
R^{\prime}_{(A,B)}
be the
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra obtained from
\mathsf{k}
by adjoining idempotent endomorphisms

e_{\phi}:\overline{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq n_{\phi}}\mathsf{k}(\gamma_{\phi,k})}\longrightarrow\overline{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq n_{\phi}}\mathsf{k}(\gamma_{\phi,k})},

for any
\phi\in A
. Then

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R^{\prime}_{(A,B)})=\Big\langle I,P_{\phi},Q_{\phi}~(\phi\in A)\mid P_{\phi}+Q_{\phi}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{\phi}}{}^{\gamma_{\phi,k}}I~(\phi\in A)\Big\rangle,

by Corollary
7.9
. Let
R_{(A,B)}
be the
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra obtained from
R^{\prime}_{(A,B)}
by adjoining isomorphisms
\overline{U_{\psi}}\cong\overline{V_{\psi}}~(\psi\in B)
, where
U_{\psi}
and
V_{\psi}
are the graded
R^{\prime}_{(A,B)}
-modules corresponding to
u_{\psi}
and
v_{\psi}
, respectively. Then
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R_{(A,B)})\cong M_{(A,B)}
by Corollary
7.6
, and this isomorphism maps
[R_{(A,B)}]
to
i
.

If
(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})
, then clearly there is a canonical graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\beta_{(A,B)}^{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}:R_{(A,B)}\to R_{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}
. Let
R
be the direct limit of the direct system
\big\{R_{(A,B)},\beta_{(A,B)}^{(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})}\mid(A,B)\leq(A^{\prime},B^{\prime})\in Z\big\}
in the category of graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. Since
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}
commutes with direct limits, we obtain

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)=\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\varinjlim R_{(A,B)})=\varinjlim\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R_{(A,B)})\cong\varinjlim M_{(A,B)}=M.

Clearly the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\cong M
maps
[R]
to
i
.

It remains to show that
R
is hereditary. We closely follow the approach used in Theorem 3.7 in
[
4
]
. Choose an ordinal
\lambda
and a bijection
\alpha\to\phi_{\alpha}
from
[0,\lambda)
to
\Phi
. We build graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras
R_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in[0,\lambda)
as follows.

(a)

Start with
R_{0}=\mathsf{k}
.

(b)

For
\alpha\in[0,\lambda)
, let
R_{\alpha+1}
be obtained from
R_{\alpha}
by adjoining an idempotent endomorphism

e_{\phi_{\alpha}}:\overline{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq n_{\phi_{\alpha}}}\mathsf{k}(\lambda_{\phi_{\alpha},k})}\to\overline{\bigoplus_{1\leq k\leq n_{\phi_{\alpha}}}\mathsf{k}(\lambda_{\phi,k})}.

(c)

If
\beta\leq\lambda
is a limit ordinal and
R_{\alpha}
has been defined for all
\alpha<\beta
, take
R_{\beta}
to be the
direct limit of
(R_{\alpha})_{\alpha<\beta}
.

By
[
9
, Theorem 3.4]
the algebra
R_{\lambda}
is hereditary. Now choose an ordinal
\mu>\lambda
and a bijection
\alpha\to\psi_{\alpha}
from
[\lambda,\mu)
to
\Psi
. We build graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras
R_{\alpha}
for
\alpha\in[\lambda,\mu)
as follows.

(d)

Start with the algebra
R_{\lambda}
constructed above.

(e)

For
\alpha\in[\lambda,\mu)
, let
R_{\alpha+1}
be obtained from
R_{\alpha}
by adjoining an isomorphism
\overline{U_{\psi_{\alpha}}}\cong\overline{V_{\psi_{\alpha}}}
, where
U_{\psi_{\alpha}}
and
V_{\psi_{\alpha}}
are the graded
R_{\alpha}
-modules corresponding to
u_{\psi_{\alpha}}
and
v_{\psi_{\alpha}}
, respectively.

(f)

If
\beta\in[\lambda,\mu)
is a limit ordinal and
R_{\alpha}
has been defined for all
\alpha<\beta
, take
R_{\beta}
to be the
direct limit of
(R_{\alpha})_{\lambda\leq\alpha<\beta}
.

By
[
9
, Theorem 3.4]
the algebra
R_{\mu}
is hereditary. Clearly
R_{\mu}\cong R
.

In order to prove the weak universality of the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R
, suppose that
S
is a
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra with
\Gamma
-monoid homomorphism
\psi:(M,i)\rightarrow(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S),[S])
. Consider the following diagram, which gives a
\Gamma
-monoid homomorphism,
f_{(A,B)}
, for any
(A,B)\in Z
.

\textstyle{\varinjlim(M_{(A,B)},i)\cong(M,i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S),[S])}
\textstyle{(M_{(A,B)},i)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{(A,B)}}

(8.2)

Since
M_{(A,B)}\cong\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R_{(A,B)})
, and
R_{(A,B)}
is the universal ring such that

\overline{P_{\phi}}\oplus\overline{Q_{\phi}}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{\phi}}{}^{\gamma_{\phi,k}}I~(\phi\in A)
and
\overline{U_{\psi}}\cong\overline{V_{\psi}}~(\psi\in B)
and the images of these modules in
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)
give the same relations, it follows that there is a
\Gamma
-graded homomorphism
R_{(A,B)}\rightarrow S
which induces

f_{(A,B)}=-\otimes_{R_{(A,B)}}S
on the level of monoids. Since the direct limit of
R_{(A,B)}
,
(A,B)\in Z
is
R
and
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}
commutes with direct limits,

\textstyle{\varinjlim R_{(A,B)}\cong R\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi_{1}}
\textstyle{S}
\textstyle{R_{(A,B)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{(A,B)}}

we obtain that
\overline{\psi_{1}}:\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(S)
coincides with
\psi
in Diagram
8.2
.

The second part of the theorem, statements (1) and (2), immediately follow from Proposition
2.6
.
∎

If
M
is generated by a finite number of elements whose sum is the order unit
i
, then the construction of the algebra
R
in the proof of Theorem
8.1
above simplifies a bit, as it is demonstrated in the proof of Theorem
8.2
below.

Theorem 8.2
.

Let
(M,i)
be a pointed conical
\Gamma
-monoid with a distinguished order unit
i
. Suppose that
M
is generated by finitely many elements whose sum is
i
. Then there is a hereditary unital hyper Leavitt path algebra
R=L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
with a
\Gamma
-grading induced by a weight map, such that
\phi:(M,i)\cong(\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R),[R])
as pointed
\Gamma
-monoids. Furthermore,
R
has a weak universal property.

Proof.

Let
X=\{p_{\phi}\mid\phi\in\Phi\}
, where
\Phi
is a finite set, be a set non-zero generators for
M
such that
\sum_{\phi\in\Phi}p_{\phi}=i
. Let
Y=\{u_{\psi}=v_{\psi}\mid\psi\in\Psi\}
be a set of relations such that
M=\langle X\mid Y\rangle
. Since
M
is conical, we may assume that
u_{\psi},v_{\psi}\neq 0
for any
\psi\in\Phi
. Let
R^{\prime}
be the
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
\mathsf{k}^{\Phi}
which is concentrated in degree
0
. Then

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R^{\prime})=\big\langle P_{\phi}~(\phi\in\Phi)\rangle

is freely generated by generators
P_{\phi}~(\phi\in\Phi)
whose sum is
[R^{\prime}]
. Let
Z
be the set of all finite subsets of
\Psi
. Clearly
Z
is a directed set together with the inclusion relation. For any
A\in Z
let
M_{A}=\langle X\mid Y_{A}\rangle
where
Y_{A}=\{u_{\psi}=v_{\psi}\mid\psi\in B\}
. If
A\subseteq A^{\prime}\in Z
, then clearly there is a canonical
\Gamma
-monoid homomorphism
\alpha_{A}^{A^{\prime}}:M_{A}\to M_{A^{\prime}}
. One checks easily that
M
is the direct limit of the direct system
\{M_{A},\alpha_{A}^{A^{\prime}}\mid A\subseteq A^{\prime}\in Z\}
in the category of
\Gamma
-monoids.

Now fix an
A\in Z
. Let
R_{A}
be the
\Gamma
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra obtained from
R^{\prime}
by adjoining isomorphisms
\overline{U_{\psi}}\cong\overline{V_{\psi}}~(\psi\in A)
where
U_{\psi}
and
V_{\psi}
are the graded
R^{\prime}
-modules corresponding to
u_{\psi}
and
v_{\psi}
, respectively. Then
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R_{A})\cong M_{A}
by Corollary
7.6
, and this isomorphism maps
[R_{A}]
to
i
.

If
A\subseteq A^{\prime}\in Z
, then clearly there is a canonical graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\beta_{A}^{A^{\prime}}:R_{A}\to R_{A^{\prime}}
. Let
R
be the direct limit of the direct system
\{R_{A},\beta_{A}^{A^{\prime}}\mid A\subseteq A^{\prime}\in Z\}
in the category of graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebras. Since
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}
commutes with direct limits, we obtain

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)=\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(\varinjlim R_{A})=\varinjlim\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R_{A})\cong\varinjlim M_{A}=M.

Clearly the isomorphism
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\cong M
maps
[R]
to
i
. By Lemma
4.10
,
R
is graded isomorphic to a unital hyper Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(H)
whose grading is induced by a weight map. That
R
is hereditary and has a weak universal property follow as in the proof of Theorem
8.1
.
∎

9.
Application to graph algebras

The Graded Classification Conjecture for Leavitt path algebras states that the graded Grothendieck group,
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}
, is a complete invariant for
these algebras (
[
13
,
15
]
,
[
1
, §7.3.4]
):

Conjecture 9.1
.

Let
E
and

F
be finite graphs.

(1)

For any order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module homomorphism
\phi:K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))
with

\phi([L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)])=L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
, there exists a unital
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-homomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
such that
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(\psi)=\phi
.

(2)

For any order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module
isomorphism
\phi:K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))
with

\phi([L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)]=[L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)]
, there exists a unital
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-isomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
such that
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(\psi)=\phi
.

Here the order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module isomorphism
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\cong K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))
should give that these algebras are graded Morita equivalent (see Conjecture
9.4
).

Since the graded
K
-theory of Leavitt path algebras coincides with equivariant
K
-theory of graph
C^{*}
-algebras one can extend the conjecture to the setting of
C^{*}
-algebras.
Denote by
\gamma_{E}
the gauge circle actions on
C^{*}(E)
and
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(C^{*}(E))
the equivariant
K
-theory of
C^{*}(E)

[
23
]
. There are canonical order preserving isomorphisms of
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-modules (see
[
15
, p. 275]
,
[
16
]
and
[
14
, Proof of Theorem A]
).

K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(L(E))\cong K_{0}(L(E\times\mathbb{Z}))\cong K_{0}(C^{*}(E\times\mathbb{Z}))\cong K_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}(C^{*}(E)).

(9.1)

Thus one can pose the analytic version of Conjecture
9.1
as follows.

Conjecture 9.2
.

Let
E
and
F
be finite graphs. Then there is an order preserving
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module
isomorphism

\phi:K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(C^{*}(E))\longrightarrow K_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}(C^{*}(F)),

with

\phi([C^{*}(E)])=[C^{*}(F)]

if and only if

C^{*}(E)\cong C^{*}(F)
which respect the gauge action.

In fact in Conjecture
9.1
if the graded Grothendieck groups are isomorphic, one should have that the isomorphism between the Leavitt path algebras is indeed a
*
-isomorphism. If this is the case, then Conjecture
9.1
implies Conjecture
9.2
. For, if
K^{\mathbb{T}}_{0}(C^{*}(E))\cong K_{0}^{\mathbb{T}}(C^{*}(F))
, then by (
9.1
),
K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathbb{C}}(E))\cong K_{0}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathbb{C}}(F))
, so
L_{\mathbb{C}}(E)\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}L_{\mathbb{C}}(F)
via a
*
-isomorphism. This implies that
C^{*}(E)\cong C^{*}(F)
which respects the gauge action (see
[
2
, Theorem 4.4]
).

The notion of
talented monoids
allows us to write the classification conjectures for Leavitt and
C^{*}
-algebras in a unified manner. The positive cone of the graded Grothendieck group of a Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
can
be described purely based on the underlying graph, via the so-called talented monoid
T_{E}
of
E

[
18
]
. The benefit of talented monoids is that they give us control over elements of the monoids (such as minimal elements, atoms, etc.)
and consequently on the “geometry” of the graphs such as the number of cycles, their lengths, etc. (see
[
18
,
12
]
).

Definition 9.3
.

Let
E
be a row-finite directed graph. The
talented monoid
of
E
, denoted
T_{E}
, is the commutative
monoid generated by
\{v(i)\mid v\in E^{0},i\in\mathbb{Z}\}
, subject to

v(i)=\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}r(e)(i+1)

for every
i\in\mathbb{Z}
and every
v\in E^{0}
that is not a sink. The additive group
\mathbb{Z}
of integers acts on
T_{E}
via monoid automorphisms by shifting indices: For each
n,i\in\mathbb{Z}
and
v\in E^{0}
, define
{}^{n}v(i)=v(i+n)
, which extends to an action of
\mathbb{Z}
on
T_{E}
. Throughout we will denote elements
v(0)
in
T_{E}
by
v
.

The crucial ingredient for us is the action of
\mathbb{Z}
on the monoid
T_{E}
. The general idea is that the monoid structure of
T_{E}
along with the action of
\mathbb{Z}
resemble the graded ring structure of a Leavitt path algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
.
Thus the conjectures above roughly state that isomorphism of talented monoids can lift to the isomorphisms of graded and equivariant
K
-theory of respected Leavitt and graph
C^{*}
-algebras. We therefore can formulate both conjectures as follows:

Conjecture 9.4
.

Let
E
and
F
be finite graphs and
\mathsf{k}
a field. Then the following are equivalent.

(1)

The talented monoids
T_{E}
and
T_{F}
are
\mathbb{Z}
-isomorphic;

(2)

The Leavitt path algebras
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
and
L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
are graded Morita equivalent;

(3)

The graph
C^{*}
-algebras
C^{*}(E)
and
C^{*}(F)
are equivariant Morita equivalent.

Furthermore, the following are equivalent.

(1)

The talented monoids
T_{E}
and
T_{F}
are pointed
\mathbb{Z}
-isomorphic;

(2)

The Leavitt path algebras
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
and
L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
are graded isomorphic;

(3)

The graph
C^{*}
-algebras
C^{*}(E)
and
C^{*}(F)
are equivariant isomorophic.

G. Arnone in
[
6
]
and L. Vas in
[
27
]
answered part (2) of Conjecture
9.1
, independently and with different approaches, in positive. In fact Arnone shows that the lifting map can be diagonal preserving graded
*
-homomorphism and Vas shows that the graphs can consist of infinite edges.

Using theory developed here, we can in fact show that for any
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
A
and any pointed
\mathbb{Z}
-homomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
, we can lift
\phi
to a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow A
, which preserves the injectivity.

Theorem 9.5
.

Let
E
be a finite graph and let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra. Let
\phi:T_{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(A)
be a
\mathbb{Z}
-monoid homomorphism with

\phi(1_{E})=[A]
. Then there exists a unital
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow A
such that
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(\psi)=\phi
. Furthermore, if
\phi(v)\not=0
, for all
v\in E^{0}
, then
\psi
is monomorphism.

Proof.

Consider the semisimple
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
R=\prod_{E^{0}}\mathsf{k}
, the product of
|E^{0}|
-copies of the field
\mathsf{k}
.
Then

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\cong\big\langle v(i)\mid v\in E^{0},\,i\in\mathbb{Z}\big\rangle,

(9.2)

with the
\mathbb{Z}
-action
{}^{n}v(i)=v(i+n)
, for
n,i\in\mathbb{Z}
.
Denote
p_{v}(i)
, where
v\in E^{0}
and
i\in\mathbb{Z}
, the graded finitely generated projective
R
-module with
\mathsf{k}(i)
appears in
v
-th component of
R
and zero elsewhere. Throughout, we write
p_{v}
for
p_{v}(0)
. Note that
\bigoplus_{v\in E^{0}}p_{v}\cong R
as graded
R
-module and the isomorphism classes
[p_{v}(i)]
correspond to
v(i)
in (
9.2
). Next we consider the pairs of graded finitely generated projective
R
-modules

p_{v}
and
\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)
, for
v\in E^{0}
that are not sinks. We construct the graded ring

S:=R\big\langle h_{v},h_{v}^{-1}:\overline{p_{v}}\cong\overline{\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)}\big\rangle,

(9.3)

for vertices
v
which are not sink. This amounts to adjoining matrices to
R
with relations which define the
\mathsf{k}
-algebra
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
(see Theorem
5.15
), and thus

S\cong_{\operatorname{gr}}L_{\mathsf{k}}(E).

(9.4)

On the other hand, by Theorem
7.5
, the monoid
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))
is obtained by
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)
subject to relations

[p_{v}(i)]\cong\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}[p_{r(e)}(i+1)],

for
i\in\mathbb{Z}
. In (
9.2
) these relations translate to

v(i)=\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}r(e)(i+1)
. Thus
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))
is precisely the talented monoid
T_{E}
, where the graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
R\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
, induces

\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)),[p_{v}]\mapsto[L_{K}(E)v]
.

Now suppose
\phi:T_{E}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)
is a pointed
\mathbb{Z}
-monoid homomorphism. Since
\phi(1_{E})=\sum_{v\in E^{0}}\phi(v)=[A]
, we obtain a graded
A
-module isomorphism
A\cong\bigoplus_{v\in E^{0}}q_{v}
, where
q_{v}
are graded finitely generated
A
-modules with
\phi(v)=[q_{v}]
. It follows that
A=\bigoplus_{v\in E^{0}}Ae_{v}
, where
e_{v}
’s are pairwise orthogonal idempotents of homogeneous degree zero in
A
, with
Ae_{v}\cong q_{v}
as graded
A
-modules. Thus there is a natural graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\eta:R=\prod_{E^{0}}\mathsf{k}\rightarrow A
, making the ring
A
a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded
R\text{-ring}_{\mathsf{k}}
. Since
A\otimes_{R}p_{v}\cong q_{v}
(use
A\otimes_{R}R/I\cong A/AI
, for an ideal
I
of
R
), we obtain the following commutative diagram of
\mathbb{Z}
-monoids:

\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A\otimes_{R}-}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)}
\textstyle{T_{E}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\phi}

Thus

[q_{v}]=\phi(v)=\phi\big(\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}r(e)(i+1)\big)=\sum_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}[q_{r(e)}(i+1)],

implying graded
A
-module isomorphisms

\displaystyle q_{v}

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}q_{r(e)}(1)

\displaystyle A\otimes_{R}p_{v}

\displaystyle\cong A\otimes_{R}\big(\bigoplus_{e\in s^{-1}(v)}p_{r(e)}(1)\big),

for vertices
v
which are not sink.

Since
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
is the universal ring providing these isomoprhisms (see
9.3
and
9.4
), it follows that there is a graded
\mathsf{k}
-algebra homomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow A
, such that the following diagram is commutative:

\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(R)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{A\otimes_{R}-}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A)}
\textstyle{\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\;\phi=A\otimes_{L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)}-}

Finally, suppose
\phi(v)\not=0
, for all
v\in E^{0}
. Since
\phi(v)=[A\otimes_{L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)}L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)v]=[A\psi(v)]
, it follows that
\psi(v)\not=0
, for all
v\in E^{0}
. Now the graded uniqueness theorem for Leavitt path algebras (
[
1
, Theorem 2.2.15]
) implies that
\psi
is injective.
∎

Corollary 9.6
.

Let
E
and
F
be finite graphs and
\phi:K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))
a preordered
\mathbb{Z}[x,x^{-1}]
-module homomorphism with

\phi([L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)])=L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
. Then there exists a unital
\mathbb{Z}
-graded homomorphism
\psi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(F)
such that
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(\psi)=\phi
. Furthermore if
\phi
is injective, so is
\psi
.

Proof.

Since the positive cone of
K^{\operatorname{gr}}_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))
is precisely
\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))
(see
[
3
]
) and
\phi
is preordered, we obtain a
\mathbb{Z}
-monoid homomorphism
\phi:\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(F))
. The Corollary now follows from Theorem
9.5
.
∎

Remark 9.7
.

Although throughout the paper, by setting the grade group
\Gamma
to be trivial, we recover the non-graded universal constructions, Corollary
9.6
, however, is not valid for the (non-graded) Grothendieck group
K_{0}
. This is because the positive cone of
K_{0}(L(E))
is not necessarily
\mathcal{V}(L(E))
. As an example, since
K_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2))=0
and
K_{0}(L(2,3))=0
, we have a preordered pointed monoid homomorphism
K_{0}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2))\rightarrow K_{0}(L(2,3))
, however there does not exist any unital ring homomorphsim
L_{\mathsf{k}}(1,2)\rightarrow L_{\mathsf{k}}(2,3).

We finish this section by giving a new proof for the graded
uniqueness theorem for Leavitt path algebras (See
[
1
, Theorem 2.2.15]
for an element-wise proof). Although this proof can be extended to arbitrary graphs, for simplicity we work with Leavitt path algebras associated to finite graphs, where all our applications are concerned.

Theorem 9.8
.

Let
E
be a finite graph,
A
a
\mathbb{Z}
-graded ring and
\phi:L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\rightarrow A
a graded ring homomorphism. If
\phi(v)\not=0
for all
v\in E^{0}
, then
\phi
is injective.

Proof.

The ring homomorphism
\phi
induces a
\Gamma
-monoid homomorphism

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}:\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(L_{\mathsf{k}}(E))

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\operatorname{gr}}(A),

\displaystyle[L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)v]

\displaystyle\longmapsto[A\phi(v)]

Since any graded finitely generated projective
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
-module, is generated by some
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)v
,
v\in E^{0}
(see the first part of the proof of Theorem
9.5
) and since
\phi(v)\not=0
for all
v\in E^{0}
, then
\overline{\phi}^{-1}(0)=\{0\}.

Suppose
\phi(a)=0
, for some
0\not=a\in L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
. Without the loss of generality we can assume
a
is homogeneous. Consider the left ideal
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)a
. Since
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)
is graded von Neumann regular,
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)a=L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)e
, for some homogeneous idempotent
e
. Thus
L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)a
is a graded finitely generated projective module and
\overline{\phi}([L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)a])=[L_{\mathsf{k}}(E)\phi(a)]=0
, which is a contradiction.
∎
